Some Studies On Exact Solutions


Abstract
This thesis explores exactly solvable quantum mechanical models in noncommutative spaces under time dependent backgrounds. It starts by examining a two dimensional damped harmonic oscillator in a time varying noncommutative space. Using the Lewis invariant method and the Ermakov-Pinney equation associated with this method, the study derives analytical eigenfunctions and explicit solutions for the noncommutative parameters under specific damping factors and oscillator frequencies. These solutions allow for exact expressions for the Lewis phase, linking the Hamiltonian’s eigenstates to those of the Lewis invariant operator. The Hamiltonian’s expectation value is computed, revealing time varying energy profiles based on different damping factors and oscillator frequencies. Next, the study extends the model by introducing an externally applied time varying magnetic field within a dynamic noncommutative framework. The system is solved using the same Lewis treatment, yielding explicit solutions for the noncommutative parameters and the Lewis phase. The study examines how the combined effects of damping and the magnetic field impact the system’s energy profile, comparing the results with those obtained without the magnetic field. The third study investigates Berry’s geometric phase in periodic models of the harmonic oscillators solvable in time varying noncommutative frameworks. Two systems are considered in this regard. One with a time reversal symmetry breaking scale invariant term in the Hamiltonian, and another where the scale invariant term arises from transforming noncommutative to commutative variables. The geometric phase is derived from the Lewis phase using the Lewis Riesenfeld treatment, and the Berry phase is explicitly calculated. The study finds out that a time reversal symmetry breaking scale invariant term cannot ensure a nontrivial Berry phase always. The final part explores a simple harmonic oscillator in a two dimensional dynamic noncommutative framework, a generalization of standard noncommutativity. The study solves the time dependent system, deriving exact analytical eigenfunctions and energy expressions, and investigates their dynamics graphically. It also establishes generalized uncertainty relations for both noncommutative and standard canonical operators. The results are consistent with previous findings for similar models under standard noncommutativity.

Chapter 1
Introduction

The exactly solvable quantum mechanical models in noncommutative (NC)
1
1

1

We will consider NC phase space in our discussion but will generally refer to it as NC space.
spaces are currently an interesting area of study. The concept of NC space time or discrete space time, is crucial for unifying the quantum mechanics and theory of gravitation. Difficulties arise when a particle attempts to probe a very small distances at the Planck scale. According to the Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle
[
1
]
, the particle probes this extremely small length scale with a large amount of energy, resulting in the breakdown of space time manifold. Consequently, the laws of gravitation becomes irrelevant at the absence of the conceptual space time. However, the concept of minimum measurable length can prevent the probing particle from measuring beyond a certain length in space time manifold. Therefore, the consideration of such length is necessary for unifying the quantum field theory and general relativity. In 1930, Heisenberg first proposed the formalism of NC quantum field theory in a letter he wrote to Peierls
[
2
]
. This idea was later formalized in the pioneering work of Snyder
[
3
]
, which sparked significant interest among theoretical physicists in studying quantum mechanical systems in NC spaces. In
[
3
]
, an example of a discrete space time which remains invariant under Lorentz transformation, was provided and it was demonstrated that Lorentz invariance does not necessitate the usual assumption of continuous space time. Following this, Snyder’s treatment was extended to curved space time, specifically de Sitter space, as shown by
[
4
]
. Although initially ignored, the NC nature of space time later gained strong support from string theory
[
5
]
-
[
9
]
, a leading theory of quantum gravity. String theory introduces the concept of strings having finite length
l_{s}
, which also defines the minimum distance in the space time manifold. Consequently, it is impossible to measure distances smaller than
l_{s}
. As demonstrated in a seminal work
[
10
]
, in a specific low energy regime, string theory can be interpreted as an effective quantum field theory in NC framework
[
11
,
12
]
. Furthermore, nearly all quantum gravity theories suggests the necessity of considering the concept of a minimum observable length, which is predicted to be near the Planck length,

l_{p}\,=\,\sqrt{\dfrac{G\,\hbar}{c^{3}}}\,=\,1.616\times 10^{-35}\penalty\ m\penalty\ ;

(1.1)

and the concept of discrete space time, which can prevent gravitational collapse at this extremely small length scale. Thus, the concept of NC space time can also ensure gravitational stability
[
13
,
14
]
.

The impact of discrete space time have been explored in the context of single field inflation
[
15
,
16
]
. Specifically, for curvature perturbations in Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker space time, the two and three point correlation functions were computed, revealing that the power spectrum exhibited anisotropy due to NC geometry. Notably, the presence of a fundamental length scale in NC space time results in the violation of Lorentz symmetry. This violation extends to Poincaré symmetry and consequently affects the CPT theorem, microcausality, and spin statistics. However, the introduction of a deformed coproduct, as discussed in the literature
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
, can restore Poincaré symmetry. In relativistic quantum field theory
[
21
]
, physicists have extensively explored such deformed coproducts, leading to investigations of its applications in various studies
[
22
,
23
]
.

Additionally, loop quantum gravity
[
24
,
25
]
offers another approach to understanding quantum space time. Consequently, in the literature
[
26
]
-
[
64
]
, there have been numerous studies on quantum mechanical systems in the framework of NC space.

In the context of a simple model of noncommutative space, we consider a quantum mechanical space in two dimensions, where the commutator brackets among the noncommutative canonical coordinate operators are given by
[X,Y]=i\theta
, with
\theta
being a real, positive constant. Apart from the configuration space, studies regarding the noncommutativity may also include NC commutation relations in momentum space such as
[P_{x},P_{y}]=i\,\Omega
, where
\Omega
is a real constant. The NC momentum operators in the system imply the existence of a magnetic field acting perpendicular to the configuration plane. However, in literature
[
65
,
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
]
, there exist more interesting forms of NC algebra that can be expressed as functions of the canonical coordinate and momentum operators. In the literature on noncommutativity, the standard Bopp-shift relations
[
71
]
have been found convenient for transforming a system from NC space to conventional quantum mechanical phase space. Nevertheless, a more generalized version of the Bopp-shift relations has been introduced in recent literature
[
72
]
.

Unlike the previous study where the NC parameters were held constant, our earlier research adopted both the coordinate mapping relations by introducing a time dependence in the NC parameters. The concept of dynamic noncommutativity can be considered a consequence of the renormalization group flow of
G
, Newton’s gravitational constant, when the inverse of the energy scale is proportional to cosmic time. Such a form of Newton’s constant arises from the solution of an exact functional renormalization group flow equation that is associated with an effective action that is dependent on the energy scale
[
73
,
74
,
75
]
. Given that the minimum observable length is directly related to Newton’s gravitational constant, it is reasonable to consider it also varies with time, similar to Newton’s constant. String theory in a time dependent background
[
76
,
77
,
78
]
also provides a motivation to look for dynamic NC parameters. In this context, time dependent NC parameters are emerged from a model system placed in a dynamic background, specifically a space time metric of the plane wave type that is supported by a Neveu–Schwarz two form potential
[
79
]
.
However,
[
80
]
also addresses both space and time dependent noncommutativity, considering Yang-Mills theories formulated on a spacetime dependent NC background.
The literature also includes other studies, especially regarding the exact quantum theory in dynamic NC framework. In
[
81
]
, a follow up to
[
82
]
, a harmonic oscillator in two dimensional dynamic NC background was studied, and its exact propagator was derived. The investigation of a simple prototype time independent model in such a varying NC space was initially pursued by
[
83
]
. Employing Lewis’s method of invariants
[
84
,
85
,
86
]
, the model of a time independent harmonic oscillator was solved exactly in two dimensional dynamic NC space. Since this method involves a nonlinear differential equation, namely Ermakov-Pinney (EP) equation
[
87
]
-
[
88
]
, it acts as a constraint relation governing the parameters existing in the Hamiltonian eigenfunctions. Interestingly, a specific integrability condition can aid in deriving explicit analytical forms of the parameters those adhere to the EP equation. On the other hand, the explicit solution of the EP equation presents the Hamiltonian eigenfunctions explicitly. Hence, a class of exact solutions for the model system can be generated by following this procedure. In
[
83
]
, the generated explicit solution of the model system analysed the uncertainty relations, revealing that time dependence arises in their lower bounds. Moreover, in the Glauber coherent states, the uncertainties were also determined. This study indicates the potential for proceeding with the quantum theory in dynamic NC background. Hence, it motivates exploration of various models on different types of NC backgrounds, an area that has been relatively less explored until now. The crucial aspect to investigate in these studies is whether the models with various dynamic noncommutativities still permit achieving the exact solution of the Hamiltonian. Following the example set by
[
83
]
, these studies should aim to discover exact solutions for prototype quantum mechanical systems within time dependent NC backgrounds, which promise to significantly enrich our understanding.

Motivated by this goal, we undertook our own investigations into exactly solvable models within dynamic NC spaces, employing the Lewis method of invariants for various analyses. In our various works, we address three key aspects related to the study of exactly solvable models. Firstly, we focus on developing effective procedures to obtain a class of explicit solutions in closed form for model systems and analyse these solutions using physical quantities such as energy expectation values, which can also be represented graphically. Secondly, the most intriguing aspect of this thesis involves separating the geometric component, under adiabatic approximation, from the Lewis phase factor which enables the construction of the eigenstate in an exact form for the periodic Hamiltonian. Lastly, we conduct a comparative study of an exactly solvable prototype model under two different types of noncommutativity. While our initial works
[
89
]
and
[
90
]
are related to the first aspect, the third study
[
91
]
is under the second aspect and the final work
[
92
]
corresponds to the last aspect. The thesis is organized in the following manner.

In chapter
2
, we provide an overview of the Lewis-Riesenfeld theory, a useful method for solving time dependent Schrödinger equations exactly. We begin by introducing the concept of a time dependent hermitian invariant operator associated with a time dependent Hamiltonian. Following this, we thoroughly explain how the hermitian invariant constructs the eigenstate of a time dependent Hamiltonian, including a discussion on the Lewis phase.

To illustrate the application of this theory, we examine the Hamiltonian of a one dimensional harmonic oscillator having a time varying angular frequency. We derive the structural form of the Lewis invariant operator and the Lewis phase factor for this system, and establish the exact eigenstate of the Hamiltonian using the Lewis approach.

We then explore another aspect of this theory, showing the methodology of deducing Berry’s geometric phase from the Lewis phase. For this purpose, we consider a time dependent periodic Hamiltonian of a generalized harmonic oscillator and use the Lewis technique to obtain its geometric phase.

In chapter
3
, we explore an exactly solvable two dimensional model in the NC framework by choosing a model of a damped quantum harmonic oscillator in NC space
[
89
]
, where the NC parameters vary with time. Initially, we establish the Hamiltonian that includes a damping factor and a time dependent angular frequency in NC space. We then transform it into standard commutative variables using the Bopp-shift relations. Following this, Lewis’s method of invariants is employed to determine the exact eigenstate of the Hamiltonian. We begin by constructing the Ermakov-Lewis invariant system, which involves the Lewis invariant operator coupled with the nonlinear EP equation
[
87
,
88
]
. For simplicity in calculations, we subsequently express the entire system in terms of polar coordinates. Finally, we solve the invariant system using the ladder operator approach and determine the structural form of the Lewis phase factor, closely following the procedure outlined in
[
83
]
.
It is important to note that the Hamiltonian eigenfunction is a combination of the invariant’s eigenfunction and the Lewis phase factor. Hence, it is also coupled with the nonlinear EP equation.
[
87
,
88
]
. In this context, we briefly review the procedure for deriving exact analytical solutions of the EP equation, as initially demonstrated in
[
83
]
using the Chiellini integrability condition
[
93
,
94
,
95
]
. Subsequently, to generate a class of explicit eigenfunctions corresponding to each solution set of the EP equation, we carefully select the parameters (damping factor and angular frequency) which constructed the original Hamiltonian of the damped system in NC space. These parameters are chosen in such a way that they are consistent with all the constraint relations present in the system and enable us to achieve the system’s solution in closed form. The analytical structures derived for the noncommutative parameters ensure that the Lewis phase, which is to be integrated out, is exactly integrable in various dissipative scenarios.

Next, we demonstrate how to calculate the matrix element of the position operator with finite, arbitrary power, with respect to the exact eigenfunctions of the Hamiltonian. Additionally, we derive the expectation value of the momentum operator and its square form with respect to the Hamiltonian eigenstate. To analyse the analytically generated solutions through a physical quantity, we use the obtained expectation values to compute the energy expectation values and explore their dynamical behaviour, both analytically and graphically, for various types of damping scenarios. Finally, we conclude the chapter with a summary of the entire discussion for clarity and convenience.

In chapter
4
, we discussed the continuation of our previous work. Here
[
90
]
, we focus on an exactly solvable model of a previously studied quantum harmonic oscillator with damping, now including an externally applied magnetic field that varies over time, while maintaining time dependent noncommutativity. We begin by formulating the two dimensional Hamiltonian for the previously considered model
with a time varying magnetic field. This Hamiltonian is then transformed into commutative variables, as was done in our earlier research. We proceed by solving the Hamiltonian using the Lewis-Riesenfeld theory. Consequently, the time dependent parameters associated with the eigenfunction of this Hamiltonian follow the nonlinear EP equation
[
87
,
88
]
.

Subsequently, similar to our previous work, to generate a class of explicit eigenfunctions corresponding to each solution set of the EP equation, we carefully select the dynamic variables (applied magnetic field, angular frequency and damping factor) that define the original Hamiltonian in NC space. Again, our choices make us able to have a class of explicit analytical solutions in a closed form.

Next, corresponding to those explicit solutions, the expressions for the energy expectation value are established analytically and presented graphically. In these graphs, the influence of various forms of externally applied magnetic fields (increasing, decaying, and constant) on the dynamical behaviour of the previously discussed dissipative model is examined. Finally, we conclude the chapter with a summary.

In chapter
5
, we explore another interesting aspect of this study concerning exactly solvable models in time dependent NC space. In the work
[
91
]
discussed in this chapter, we address periodic model systems in NC space to derive Berry’s geometric phase from the Lewis phase factor, which allows for the exact solution of these models. We formulate two periodic Hamiltonians for harmonic oscillators in two distinct types of NC space with time dependent backgrounds. The first Hamiltonian includes an explicit scale invariant term necessary for generating a geometric phase shift, while the second type lacks this term in its original form. To transform these Hamiltonians from NC space to conventional quantum mechanical phase space, the Bopp-shift relations
[
71
]
are employed for the first type, while a modified form of the Bopp-shift relation
[
72
]
is used for the second. We then apply Lewis prescription and continue our study concerning the exact results, up to finding the system’s analytical eigenfunctions, which also include the exact integral form of the Lewis phase factor.

Subsequently, we incorporate the adiabatic approximation into the exact form of the Lewis phase factor, deriving a generic form of the geometric phase acquired by the periodic system, as demonstrated by
[
96
]
in their work. We further explore the explicit presence of the Berry phase in both systems, varying time dependent parameters, including NC parameters periodically over time. Additionally, we conduct a detailed analysis supported by graphical illustrations and a significant finding from this study is concluded at the end of this chapter.

In Chapter
6
, we explored the latest work
[
92
]
of our research. We investigate a time independent model of a simple harmonic oscillator in a two dimensional dynamic NC space, governed by the modified version of standard Bopp-shift relations
[
72
]
. Starting with the construction of the Hamiltonian in NC framework, we subsequently transform it into a conventional quantum mechanical phase space, resulting in a Hamiltonian comprising a scale invariant term and Zeeman term. The Hamiltonian is then solved using the Lewis-Riesenfeld treatment. As a result, the dynamic coefficients existing in the Hamiltonian eigenfunctions satisfy the nonlinear EP equation
[
87
,
88
]
.

We extend the EP solution set outlined in
[
83
]
, under the Chiellini integrability condition
[
95
]
, to determine the explicit analytical form of the EP variable that arises due to the modification introduced in noncommutativity. Subsequently, we compute the expectation value of the Hamiltonian using its eigenstate. This expression allows us to examine the time evolution of the system’s energy expectation value across various types of EP solution sets, both through analytical methods and graphical representations. We also compare these energy dynamics with those observed in standard NC scenarios. Additionally, the generalized form of the uncertainty relations is established for both the noncommutative and standard canonical operators. Finally, we summarize our discussion at the end of the chapter.

Chapter 2
Lewis-Riesenfeld treatment for time dependent Hamiltonian

Lewis-Reisenfeld theory, also known as Lewis method of invariant is an exact quantum theory developed by Lewis
et
al.
[
84
,
85
,
86
]
for the quantum system whose Hamiltonians are explicitly time dependent. A Hamiltonian of this nature can arise from a system that is embedded in an environment that is fundamentally unknown. To effectively describe such systems, we can use
H(t)
, the explicitly time dependent Hamiltonians that, in classical physics, produce the proper equations of motion. In quantum physics, these systems are very elegantly handled by Lewis
et
al.
[
84
,
85
,
86
]
since they outlined a very useful and elegant method to solve a time dependent Schrödinger equation (considering
\hbar\,=\,1
) exactly:

i\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\psi}\,=\,H(t)\,\ket{\psi}\penalty\ .

(2.1)

The core aspect of this theory of general systems is to establish the connection between the eigenstates of an explicitly time dependent invariant and the solutions of the Schrödinger equation. For each eigenstate of an invariant, there exists a time dependent phase transformation that converts the eigenstate into a solution of the Schrödinger equation. This phase factor can be obtained appropriately by solving a simple first order differential equation. The time dependent invariant and the phase factor are respectively known as the Lewis invariant and the Lewis phase in literature.

We will first explain this theory for a general time dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)
and then demonstrate its application to a harmonic oscillator system, where the Hamiltonian varies with time due to a time dependent angular frequency. Additionally, by considering an example of a periodic Hamiltonian, we will discuss another interesting aspect of this theory, deriving Berry’s geometric phase from the Lewis phase under the adiabatic approximation.

2.1
Concept of Lewis invariant

Lewis invariant operator is basically an explicitly time dependent non trivial hermitian operator
I(t)
which is itself invariant with respect to time. Hence, the operator obeys the following relations

\dot{I}(t)=\,i\partial_{t}\,I\,+\,[I,H]=0

(2.2)

and

I^{\,\dagger}\,=\,I\penalty\ .

(2.3)

Though this hermitian invariant is generally utilized to find out the exact eigenstate of a time dependent Hamiltonian, the most significant feature of
I(t)
is revealed out when it acts on an eigenstate of the time dependent Schrödinger state
\ket{\psi(t)}
. We obtain the following,

\displaystyle i\,\partial_{t}\,(I\,\ket{\psi})\,

\displaystyle=\,i\,\left(\partial_{t}\,I\,\right)\,\ket{\psi}\,+\,i\,I\,\left(\partial_{t}\,\ket{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\,-[I,H\,]\,\ket{\psi}\,+\,i\,I\,\left(\partial_{t}\,\ket{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\,\left(HI-IH\right)\,\ket{\psi}\,+\,\,I\,\left(i\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\,\left(HI-IH\right)\,\ket{\psi}\,+\,\,IH\,\,\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=H\,(\,I\,\ket{\psi})\penalty\ .

It clearly concludes that a time dependent Schrödinger state produces another Schrödinger state after being operated by the hermitian invariant operator
I(t)
. In this way, we can generate a class of Schrödinger state vectors. Nevertheless, this characteristic of the hermitian invariant
I(t)
is more fundamental than its another feature of finding the exact eigenstate of a time dependent Hamiltonian because, the second feature is valid when
I(t)
does not include the time differentiation operator in its structure. Next, we describe the nice and explicit method of deriving the exact eigenstate of
H(t)
by using the Lewis invariant
I(t)
.

2.2
Lewis phase and exact eigenstate of Hamiltonian

Lewis Reisenfeld theory depicts that if the hermitian invariant does not include any time differentiation form in its structure, it is possible to find out the appropriate phases of the eigenstates of
I(t)
so that those eigenstates, after being multiplied to the found phase factors, can be the eigenstates of the Hamiltonian
H(t)
also.

With the aim of illustrating the method, we begin by assuming that
I(t)
has complete set of orthonormal eigenstates
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
associated with the real, time independent eigenvalues
\lambda\,:

\displaystyle I(t)\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\,=\,\lambda\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \langle\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa{{}^{\prime}}\,|\,\lambda,\kappa\rangle\,=\,\delta_{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\lambda}\,\delta_{\kappa^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa}\penalty\ ;

(2.4)

where
\kappa
is a quantum number other than
\lambda
.

Next, in an effort to verify the time independent nature of the eigenvalues
\lambda
of the hermitian invariant
I(t)
, we first differentiate Eqn.(
2.4
) and take a scalar product of it with
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
. Since,
\bra{\lambda,\kappa}\,I\partial_{t}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}=\lambda\,\bra{\lambda,\kappa}\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
, we can directly write

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\,\lambda\,=\,\bra{\lambda,\kappa}\,\partial_{t}\,I\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ .

(2.5)

Now, using Eqn.(
2.2
), the matrix element
\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,I\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
as well as
\partial_{t}\,\lambda
can be derived in the following way,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\,\lambda

\displaystyle=\bra{\lambda,\kappa}\,\partial_{t}\,I\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}=i\bra{\lambda,\kappa}(IH-HI)\ket{\lambda,\kappa}

\displaystyle=i\lambda\left[\bra{\lambda,\kappa}H\ket{\lambda,\kappa}-\bra{\lambda,\kappa}H\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\right]=0

(2.6)

Since, the eigenvalues of the hermitian invariant
I
is time independent, it is prominent from Eqn.(
2.4
) that the eigenstates of
I
must be time dependent.

With the above result in our hand, we proceed to establish the relation among the eigenstates of the hermitian invariant,
I(t)
and the eigenstates of the Hamiltonian,
H(t)
. We again differentiate Eqn.(
2.4
) and take a scalar product of it with
\ket{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa{{}^{\prime}}}
and have the following expression

\displaystyle\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,I\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}+\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,I\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\,=\,\lambda\,\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ .

(2.7)

The value of the matrix element
\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,I\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
can also be obtained from Eqn.(
2.2
) as

\displaystyle\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,I\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\,=\,i\,(\lambda^{{}^{\prime}}-\lambda)\,\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,H\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ ;

(2.8)

which is substituted in Eqn.(
2.7
) and it takes the following form as

\displaystyle i\,(\lambda^{{}^{\prime}}-\lambda)\,\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,H\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}+(\lambda^{{}^{\prime}}-\lambda)\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}=0

(2.9)

The above relation reduces to the Schrödinger equation only for the condition
\lambda^{{}^{\prime}}\,\neq\,\lambda\,:

\displaystyle i\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}=\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,H\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ .

(2.10)

Hence,
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
cannot belong to the eigenstates of the Hamiltonian,
H(t)
. Since, there is no restriction to multiply an arbitrary time dependent phase factor with
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
, a time dependent gauge transformation is performed on
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
and a new set of eigenstates of
I(t)
are defined in a following way

\displaystyle\ket{\lambda,\kappa}_{\alpha}\,=\,e^{i\,\alpha_{\lambda,\kappa}(t)}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ ;

(2.11)

where
\alpha_{\lambda\kappa}(t)
is real time dependent phase factor. As the invariant
I(t)
is not supposed to include any time derivative operators, it would not interact with the multiplied phase factor
\alpha_{\lambda,\kappa}(t)
. Hence, in the manner of the eigenstates
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
, the newly defined set of eigenstates
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}_{\alpha}
also holds the relation derived in Eqn.(
2.10
) for the condition
\lambda^{{}^{\prime}}\neq\lambda\,:

\displaystyle i\,{}_{\alpha}\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,\partial_{t}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}_{\alpha}={}_{\alpha}\bra{\lambda^{{}^{\prime}},\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,H\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}_{\alpha}\penalty\ .

(2.12)

At the same time, the above equality among the matrix elements should also be valid for the condition
\lambda^{{}^{\prime}}=\lambda\,
as the vector states
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}_{\alpha}
are formed to be the Schrödinger solution set. In this regard, Eqn.(
2.11
) is directly substituted to the structure of the above equation where
\lambda^{{}^{\prime}}
is made equal to
\lambda\,
. It gives rise to the following differential equation

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}_{\lambda\kappa}\,\delta_{\kappa\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}=\bra{\lambda,\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}}\,i\,\partial_{t}-H\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ .

(2.13)

The above equation implies that the invariant’s eigenstates
\ket{\lambda,\kappa}
are to be selected such that the matrix elements except the diagonal ones vanish for
\kappa\neq\kappa^{{}^{\prime}}
. Thus, to prepare the Schrödinger solution as defined in Eqn.(
2.11
), the phase factor
\dot{\alpha}_{\lambda\kappa}(t)
which is also known as Lewis phase in literature, simply obeys

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}_{\lambda\kappa}\,=\bra{\lambda,\kappa}\,i\,\partial_{t}-H\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ .

(2.14)

Therefore, the general form of the eigenstate of the Hamiltonian
H(t)
reads

\displaystyle\ket{\lambda,\kappa}_{H}=\sum_{\lambda,\kappa}\,C_{\lambda,\kappa}\,e^{i\,\alpha_{\lambda,\kappa}(t)}\,\ket{\lambda,\kappa}\penalty\ .

(2.15)

2.3
Application of Lewis theory to time dependent models

In order to show the application of this theory, we aim to discuss two aspects found from this theory. First, we will establish an exact eigenstate of an explicitly time dependent Hamiltonian. For this, we consider one of the simplest time dependent models and explain the procedure for deriving the form of the Lewis invariant and Lewis phase factor, as demonstrated by Lewis
et
al. in their work
[
86
]
. Second, to derive the geometric phase part from the Lewis phase, we will apply this method to a system of a time dependent generalized harmonic oscillator, following the approach outlined in
[
96
]
.

2.3.1
Exact eigenstate : Time dependent harmonic oscillator

The Hamiltonian of a one dimensional simple harmonic oscillator having time dependent frequency reads

\displaystyle H(t)

\displaystyle\,=\,\dfrac{p^{2}}{2M}\,+\,\dfrac{1}{2}\,M\,\omega^{2}(t)\,x^{2}\penalty\ ;

(2.16)

where
M
is time independent mass of the oscillator. It is obvious that
\omega(t)
is an arbitrary function of time and
M
is a real, positive constant. Since, it is a quantum mechanical model system and we have chosen to narrate this quantum theory in the natural unit (
\hbar=1
), the canonically conjugate operators satisfy the commutation relation
[x,p]\,=\,i
. In order to find out the exact eigenstate of the above Hamiltonian by using Lewis technique, the structural form of the hermitian invariant
I(t)
is to be considered as per the standard assumption that the Lewis invariant should be of the same order and form in the canonical variables as the original Hamiltonian Eqn.(
2.16
). Hence, we begin with the following homogeneous quadratic form of the invariant

\displaystyle I(t)\,=\,\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\alpha(t)\,p^{2}\,+\,\beta(t)\,x^{2}\,+\,\gamma(t)\,\left(x\,p\,+\,px\right)\right]\penalty\ ;

(2.17)

where
\alpha(t),\beta(t)
and
\gamma(t)
are real arbitrary time dependent coefficients. However, the choice of the invariant is not unique and the ansatz could have also be chosen without the factor
\dfrac{1}{2}
. But the above structure is suitable to provide its own eigensystem in a simple and convenient way. Next, in order to fulfil the property of invariance, we substitute the above ansatz in Eqn.(
2.2
). It restricts the arbitrariness of the time dependent coefficients of
I(t)
and those coefficients are constrained to obey the following differential equations,

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}(t)\,

\displaystyle=\,-\dfrac{2}{M}\,\gamma(t)

(2.18)

\displaystyle\dot{\beta}(t)\,

\displaystyle=\,2\,M\,\omega^{2}(t)\,\gamma(t)

(2.19)

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}(t)\,

\displaystyle=\,M\omega^{2}(t)\alpha(t)-\dfrac{\beta(t)}{M}\penalty\ .

(2.20)

In an effort to make the further procedure simpler, an auxiliary time dependent parameter
\rho(t)
is invented such that
\alpha(t)=\rho^{2}(t)
. Parametrizing this variable in Eqn.(
2.18
), the coefficient
\gamma(t)
can be represented in terms of
\rho(t)
and substituting the latest form of
\gamma(t)
in Eqn.(
2.20
) the parametrized form of
\beta(t)
can be reproduced. Hence, the above coefficients are taken the following form,

\displaystyle\alpha(t)

\displaystyle=\rho^{2}(t)

(2.21)

\displaystyle\beta(t)

\displaystyle=M^{2}\left[{{\rho}^{2}(t)}\,\omega^{2}(t)\,+\,\{\,\dot{\rho}^{2}(t)\,+\,\rho(t)\,\ddot{\rho}(t)\,\}\right].

(2.22)

\displaystyle\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=-\,M\dot{\rho}(t)\,\rho(t)\penalty\ .

(2.23)

However, the above three equations can be merged into a single differential equation. Substituting the earlier form of
\beta
into Eqn.(
2.19
) and multiplying both side with
\rho^{2}(t)
, the following non-linear differential equation can be found as,

\displaystyle M^{2}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left[\rho^{3}\left(\ddot{\rho}+\rho\,\omega^{2}(t)\right)\right]=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,\ddot{\rho}+\rho\,\omega^{2}(t)=\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{M^{2}\rho^{3}}\penalty\ ;

(2.24)

where the constant
M
can be absorbed into the integration constant
\xi^{2}
but we prefer to keep it as a separate
quantity as
M
is the mass parameter of the oscillator. The non-linear differential equation in Eqn.(
2.24
) is known as Ermakov-Pinney (EP)
[
87
,
88
]
equation in literature. The point is to be remembered that, this equation (Eqn.(
2.24
)) which is emerged due to the parametrization in Eqn.(
2.18
,
2.19
,
2.20
) actually carries the signature of the invariance property of Lewis invariant
I(t)
. Hence, the structural form of time dependent hermitian invariant in terms of
\rho(t)
always appears with that non-linear EP equation.

Even, the parametrized form of
\beta
in Eqn.(
2.22
) can be simplified further by substituting the expression of
\ddot{\rho}
from EP equation. Thus,
\beta
is reduced as

\displaystyle\beta(t)\,=\,\dot{\rho}^{2}\,M^{2}+\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\penalty\ .

(2.25)

With all the above expressions, we proceed with the following form of Lewis invariant

\displaystyle I(t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\rho^{2}\,p^{2}+\left\{\dot{\rho}^{2}\,M^{2}+\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right\}\,x^{2}-\rho\dot{\rho}\,M\left(x\,p\,+\,p\,x\right)\right]

(2.26)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\left(\rho\,p-M\dot{\rho}\,x\right)^{2}+\dfrac{\xi^{2}x^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right]\penalty\ ;

(2.27)

with an additional non-linear EP equation

\displaystyle\ddot{\rho}+\rho\,\omega^{2}(t)=\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{M^{2}\rho^{3}}\penalty\ .

(2.28)

Here, we can also facilitate the advantage of the existing differential equation. Since, it is possible to solve the above differential equation
[
88
]
exactly, a class of invariant operators can be generated corresponding to the family of real solution of EP equation.

Next, we proceed to find out the eigensystem of the above invariant (Eqn.(
2.27
)) by eliminating the integration constant
\xi
by making the scale transformation
\rho(t)\,\rightarrow\,\sqrt{\xi}\,\rho(t)
. This can be done in operator approach which is equivalent to the same
[
97
]
for the purpose of solving a time independent Hamiltonian of a harmonic oscillator.

Defining the following unitary operators (
\hat{U^{\dagger}}\hat{U}=\hat{U}\hat{U^{\dagger}}=\textbf{I}
)

\displaystyle\hat{U}=exp\left[\dfrac{-i\dot{\rho}\,M}{2\rho}x^{2}\right],\,\,\,

(2.29)

the invariant is transformed as
I^{{}^{\prime}}=\hat{U}I\hat{U}^{\dagger}\,
and it takes the following structure

\displaystyle I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)=\rho^{2}\,p^{2}+\dfrac{{x^{2}}}{\rho^{2}}\,\,;

(2.30)

which is similar to the Hamiltonian of a harmonic oscillator. Next, the ladder operators corresponding to the above form of invariant are defined as

\displaystyle{\hat{a}}^{{}^{\prime}}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\dfrac{x}{\rho}+i\rho\,{p}\right)\,\,\,,\,\,\,{{\hat{a}}^{{}^{\prime}\dagger}}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\dfrac{x}{\rho}-i\rho{\,p}\right)\penalty\ .

(2.31)

Now, applying the reverse transformation, it is very easy to bring back the ladder operators required for the purpose of solving the invariant in Eqn.(
2.27
). They are obtained as

\displaystyle\hat{a}(t)

\displaystyle={\hat{U}}^{\dagger}{\hat{a}}^{{}^{\prime}}\hat{U}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\dfrac{x}{\rho}+i\rho{p}-i\dot{\rho}\,M\,x\right]

\displaystyle\hat{{a}^{\dagger}}(t)

\displaystyle={\hat{U}}^{\dagger}{{\hat{a}}^{{}^{\prime}\dagger}}\hat{U}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\dfrac{x}{\rho}-i\rho{p}+i\dot{\rho}\,M\,x\right]\penalty\ .

(2.32)

It is straightforward to verify that the operators follow the canonical commutation relation
[\hat{a},\hat{a}^{\dagger}]\,=\,1
.
Nevertheless, the multiplication of above those operators imply that

I(t)\,=\,\left(\hat{{a}^{\dagger}}\,\hat{a}\,+\,\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\penalty\ .

(2.33)

Finally, denoting
\ket{n}
as the normalized eigenstate of
I(t)
, the eigensystem of Lewis invariant is described as

I(t)\ket{n}\,=\,\left(\,n+\dfrac{1}{2}\,\right)\,\ket{n}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ n\,=\,0,1,2,...

(2.34)

with the standard raising and lowering operations

\displaystyle\hat{a}\,\ket{n}\,=\,\sqrt{n}\,\ket{n-1}\penalty\

\displaystyle,\penalty\ \hat{a}^{\dagger}\,\ket{n}\,=\,\sqrt{n+1}\,\ket{n+1}

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\,\ket{n}\,=\,n\,\ket{n}\penalty\ .

(2.35)

In order to construct the eigenstate of the Hamiltonian according to Eqn.(
2.11
), we need to obtain the Lewis phase using Eqn.(
2.14
). For the sake of simplicity in the calculation, it is convenient to represent the diagonal matrix element in Eqn.(
2.14
) in terms of the ladder operators. Therefore, we first obtain

\displaystyle\bra{n}\,H(t)\,\ket{n}\,

\displaystyle=\,\dfrac{M}{4}\left(\dot{\rho}^{2}+\omega^{2}\rho^{2}+\dfrac{1}{M^{2}\rho^{2}}\right)\,\bra{n}\left(\hat{a}{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}+{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\right)\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\,\dfrac{M}{2}\left(\dot{\rho}^{2}+\omega^{2}\rho^{2}+\dfrac{1}{M^{2}\rho^{2}}\right)\,\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\penalty\ ;

(2.36)

and also express the diagonal matrix element
\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n}
in the following manner

\displaystyle\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n}\,

\displaystyle=\,\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\,\,{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}\,\ket{n-1}

\displaystyle=\,\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial\,{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}}{\partial\,t}\,\,\,\ket{n-1}\,+\,\,\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bra{n}\,\,{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}\,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n-1}

\displaystyle=\,\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial\,{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}}{\partial\,t}\,\,\,\ket{n-1}\,+\,\bra{n-1}\,\,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n-1}\penalty\ .

(2.37)

The partial derivative of
{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}
is computed as

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\,{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}}{\partial\,t}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\left\{-\dfrac{2\dot{\rho}}{\rho}+i\,M\,\left(\rho\ddot{\rho}-\dot{\rho}^{2}\right)\right\}\,\hat{a}\,+\,i\,M\,\left(\rho\ddot{\rho}-\dot{\rho}^{2}\right){\hat{a}}\dagger\right]\penalty\ .

(2.38)

Substituting Eqn.(
2.38
) in Eqn.(
2.37
), the form of the element
\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n}
is simplified as

\displaystyle\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n}\,

\displaystyle=\,\dfrac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\bra{n}i\,M\,\left(\rho\ddot{\rho}-\dot{\rho}^{2}\right){\hat{a}}\dagger\,\ket{n-1}\,+\,\bra{n-1}\,\,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n-1}

\displaystyle=i\,\dfrac{M}{2}\,\left(\rho\ddot{\rho}-\dot{\rho}^{2}\right)\,+\,\bra{n-1}\,\,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n-1}\penalty\ .

(2.39)

After
(n-1)
times iteration

\displaystyle\bra{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{n}\,=i\,n\,\dfrac{M}{2}\,\left(\rho\ddot{\rho}-\dot{\rho}^{2}\right)\,\,+\,\bra{0}\,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{0}\penalty\ .

(2.40)

The one and only information regarding the zero point contribution is that it should be purely imaginary as the operator
\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}
is non-hermitian. Taking this fact into consideration, the zero point contribution is required to be chosen in an appropriate manner. Thus, we adopt the following convenient form which vanishes while the EP parameter
\rho(t)
reduces to a constant value,

\bra{0}\,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\ket{0}\,=\,i\,\dfrac{M}{4}\left(\rho\ddot{\rho}-\dot{\rho}^{2}\right)\penalty\ .

(2.41)

Substituting Eqn.(
2.41
,
2.40
,
2.36
) in Eqn.(
2.14
) and simplifying using EP equation in Eqn.(
2.24
), the Lewis phase factor is obtained as

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}(T)\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\,\bra{n}\,i\,\partial_{t}-H(t)\,\ket{n}\,dt\,

\displaystyle=\,\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\dfrac{M}{2}\,\int_{0}^{T}\,\left[-\rho\,\left(\ddot{\rho}\,+\,\omega^{2}\rho\right)-\dfrac{1}{M^{2}\rho^{2}}\right]\,dt

\displaystyle=\,-\dfrac{1}{M}\,\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\int_{0}^{T}\,\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}\,dt\penalty\ .

(2.42)

Therefore, the time dependent eigenstate of the hamiltonian (Eqn.(
2.16
)) is found as

\ket{\psi}_{n}\,=\,\ket{n}\,e^{i\,\alpha_{n}}(t)\,;

(2.43)

where
\alpha_{n}(t)
is shown in Eqn.(
2.42
).

2.3.2
Berry phase from Lewis phase : Time dependent generalized harmonic oscillator (periodic Hamiltonian)

Here, we are about to review another nice aspect of the Lewis Riesenfeld treatment that not only builds up the eigenstate of a time dependent Hamiltonian but also provides a gentle way to derive Berry’s geometric phase from the time dependent Lewis phase factor under adiabatic conditions, if the corresponding Hamiltonian structure contains a suitable term capable of forming a geometric phase when the Hamiltonian parameters evolve periodically with respect to time. Hence, we chose such a time dependent Hamiltonian containing a time reversal symmetry breaking scale invariant term which is a necessary to generate a geometric phase form and follow the methodology described in
[
96
]
for deriving Berry phase from Lewis phase. In this context, we would like to highlight other noteworthy works
[
98
,
99
]
that discuss the connections between Berry phase and Lewis phase.

Thus, the Hamiltonian of a time dependent harmonic oscillator interacting with an arbitrary background field reads,

\displaystyle H(t)\,=\,\dfrac{1}{2M}\,p^{2}\,+\,\dfrac{M\.{\omega}^{2}(t)}{2}\,x^{2}+\,d(t)\,\left(x\,p\,+\,p\,x\right)\penalty\ ;

(2.44)

where the coefficient
d(t)
is an arbitrary field parameter varying periodically with respect to time. That parameter, for example, can be gravitational field parameter when the oscillator interacts with gravitational wave, as also studied in a recent communication
[
100
]
for the purpose of deriving Berry phase using Lewis approach. It is noteworthy that, in the considered model, the time dependent angular frequency
\omega(t)
also varies periodically with respect to time, and the presence of the parameter
d(t)
with its adjoining scale invariant term is a necessary condition for the system to exhibit a geometric phase shift after evolving through a full time period on the Hamiltonian parameter space. Thus, the Hamiltonian in Eqn.(
2.44
), describes one of the simplest periodic systems in the context of the harmonic oscillator model, which allows for the presence of a geometric phase.

Rearranging Eqn.(
2.44
), the Hamiltonian can also be expressed as

\displaystyle H(t)=\dfrac{1}{2M}\left(p+2\,M\,d(t)\,x\right)^{2}\,+\,\dfrac{M\,\omega^{2}_{e}(t)}{2}\,x^{2}\penalty\ ;

(2.45)

where
\omega_{e}(t)\,=\,\left[\,\omega^{2}(t)-4\,d^{2}(t)\right]^{1/2}
acts as the effective frequency of the model.

In order to derive the geometric phase from the Lewis phase, it is necessary to first find the eigenstate of that system using the Lewis technique. Since this methodology is discussed in detail in the earlier section, we will only present the expressions of the Lewis invariant and Lewis phase, along with their adjoining non linear EP equation, before proceeding towards the main goal.

Thus, in respective manner, the structural form of the hermitian invariant
I(t)
and Lewis phase factor are given by

I(t)=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\rho^{2}\,p^{2}+\left[M^{2}(\dot{\rho}-2\rho\,d)^{2}+\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right]\,x^{2}-\rho\,M(\dot{\rho}-2\rho\,d)(x\,p\,+\,p\,x)\right]\penalty\ ;

(2.46)

and

\Theta_{n}(t)\,=\,-\dfrac{1}{M}\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\int_{0}^{T}\,\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}\penalty\ dt\penalty\ ;

(2.47)

where, obviously, all the time dependent parameters adhere to the following version of non-linear EP equation,

\displaystyle\ddot{\rho}+\rho\left[\omega_{e}^{2}-2\,\dot{d}\,\right]=\dfrac{1}{M^{2}\rho^{3}}\penalty\ .

(2.48)

As we mentioned earlier that the Hamiltonian parameter
d(t)
plays a crucial rule for the emergence of geometric phase, and the phase is going to be derived from Lewis phase, in this regard, a notable observation can be marked that the Lewis phase, unlike the Lewis invariant operator, does not occur with that coefficient. However, it can be brought inside the phase factor (Eqn.(
2.47
)) by replacing the integrand from EP equation (Eqn.(
2.48
)).
After doing so, the Lewis phase can be rewritten as

\Theta_{n}(t)\,=\,-\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\int_{0}^{T}\,\sqrt{\dfrac{\ddot{\rho}}{\rho}+\left(\omega_{e}^{2}-2\,\dot{d}\right)}\penalty\ dt\penalty\ .

(2.49)

Now, if all the Hamiltonian parameters and the invented EP parameter
\rho(t)
vary adiabatically that means they vary very slowly in comparison with the unperturbed wave function (the wave function when the Hamiltonian would not vary), the adiabatic approximation can be employed in Eqn.(
2.49
).

In the first approximation, the terms higher than the single order derivative and the terms formed due to the multiplication between the derivative terms, are considered to be negligible. Hence, we now present Eqn.(
2.49
) in a following manner

\Theta_{n}(t)\,\approx\,-\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\int_{0}^{T}\,\omega_{e}\sqrt{\left(1-\dfrac{2\,\dot{d}}{\omega_{e}^{2}}\right)}\penalty\ dt\penalty\ .

(2.50)

Next, in the above equation, we apply the binomial approximation which is consistent with the limit
\dot{d}\,<<\,\omega_{e}^{2}
that basically implies that, at any moment, the variation of the perturbation is very less than the effective frequency of the system. Finally, the phase can be divided into two terms,

\Theta_{n}(t)\,\approx\,-\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\left[\int_{0}^{T}\,\omega_{e}(t)\,\penalty\ dt+\int_{0}^{T}\,\dfrac{1}{\omega_{e}}\dfrac{d\,d(t)}{dt}\,dt\right]\penalty\ .

(2.51)

Since, all the time dependent parameters are already considered to be periodic functions of time, they evolve a circular path on the parameter space
\mathcal{R}\,=\,\mathcal{R}(\omega,\,d)
over a full time period
T
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(0)\,=

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{R}(T)\penalty\ \penalty\ ;\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{R}\,=\,(\omega,d)

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{dt}\,=\,\dfrac{d\mathcal{R}}{dt}\,\nabla\,\mathcal{R}\penalty\ .

(2.52)

Substituting the above relation in the second additive term in Eqn.(
2.51
) and denoting it as
\Theta_{G}
we have

\Theta_{G}\,=\,\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\oint\,\dfrac{1}{\omega_{e}}\left(\,\nabla_{\mathcal{R}}\,d\,\right)\,d\mathcal{R}\penalty\ .

(2.53)

This is the well known expression of Berry’s geometric phase.

In a later chapter
5
, we will employ this method to deduce the geometric phase for the periodic Hamiltonians of various kind of harmonic oscillators in NC space.

Chapter 3
Noncommutative Damped harmonic oscillator

There have been numerous studies on time-dependent classical and quantum harmonic oscillators over a long period in the literature. When considering two such oscillators, the problem becomes even more intriguing in the context of a two dimensional system. To be consistent with practical scenarios, damping must be included in the system. In practical scenarios, dissipation is attributed to the interaction between the two systems: the system we observe and the thermal reservoir. Dissipation arises as a consequence of the irreversible flow of energy into the reservoir. Dissipation arises as a consequence of the irreversible flowing of energy into the reservoir. However, an explicitly time varying Hamiltonian in a closed form provides an effective description of a dissipated system where the source of dissipation is, in principle, unknown. In the literature, there have been numerous works on damped quantum harmonic oscillator in one dimension
[
101
]
-
[
105
]
, its two dimensional equivalent is less explored
[
106
,
82
,
81
]
.
A damped harmonic oscillator in two dimensional standard phase space is studied by Lawson
et al.

[
106
]
. Their solutions provide a foundation for exploring the construction of various coherent states with intriguing properties. While two dimensional quantum damped harmonic oscillators (two linearly coupled non interacting Caldirola-Kanai
[
107
,
108
]
damping models with friction terms) in dynamic NC space were studied in
[
81
]
to determine the exact propagator and statistical partition function of the system, our study differs from the work presented in
[
81
]
.

In our work detailed in
[
89
]
, which forms the subject of this chapter, the work by Lawson et al.
[
106
]
is extended in a two dimensional dynamic NC background. To treat the system, we employ Lewis method of invariant, following the procedure given in
[
83
]
for their two dimensional model of a simple harmonic oscillator with dynamic noncommutativity. Additionally, we also devise a fruitful procedure for obtaining a class of exact solutions for this system.

3.1
Hamiltonian of the damped harmonic oscillator in noncommutative space

Here, we start with the two dimensional time dependent Hamiltonian describing a model system consisting of a damped harmonic oscillator in a dynamic NC background.

Thus, within the NC framework, the Hamiltonian of the present system reads,

H(t)=\dfrac{f(t)}{2M}({P_{1}}^{2}+{P_{2}}^{2})+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)}{2f(t)}({X_{1}}^{2}+{X_{2}}^{2})\penalty\ ;

(3.1)

where
f(t)
denotes the damping factor and it is given by,

f(t)=e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\eta(s)ds}\penalty\ ,

(3.2)

where
\eta(t)
represents the friction coefficient. In literature, this structure of the damping factor is very well known when the friction coefficient is set to be a constant and a two dimensional Hamiltonian (placed in a standard quantum mechanical phase space) consisting such a coefficient of friction with a constant angular frequency is popularly known as Caldirola and Kanai Hamiltonian
[
107
,
108
]
. As we further explore this model in a NC framework, later, it will be observed that this kind of damping factor leads to a certain time intervals within which the system remains physical. Apart from it,
\omega(t)
is the oscillator’s angular frequency varying with respect to time.
M
is its constant mass parameter.

Since the system is considered in a dynamic NC space, the commutator brackets among the NC canonical operators existing in the Hamiltonian read (including
\hbar
)

\displaystyle[X_{j},X_{k}]\penalty\ =i\theta(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,

\displaystyle[P_{j},P_{k}]\penalty\ =i\Omega(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,[X_{j},P_{k}]\,=i\,\hbar\,\left[1+\frac{\theta(t)\Omega(t)}{4}\right]\delta_{jk};

(3.3)

\displaystyle(j,k=1,2\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{jk}=-\epsilon_{kj}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{12}=1)

where
\theta(t)
and
\Omega(t)
are the dynamic NC parameters for the configuration space
and the momentum space respectively. In the other hand, the canonical operators
(x_{i},p_{i})
in standard quantum mechanical phase space follow the commutator brackets
[x_{j},p_{k}]=i\hbar\delta_{jk}
,
[x_{j},x_{k}]=0=[p_{j},p_{k}]
; (
j,k=1,2
).

Next, the standard Bopp-shift relations
[
71
]
(
\hbar=1
) are to be implemented into the system for transforming original Hamiltonian (i.e. NC Hamiltonian) from NC space to into the standard canonical phase space. Now, the Bopp-shift relations are given by,

\displaystyle X_{1}=x_{1}-\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{2}\,\,\,;\,\,\,X_{2}=x_{2}+\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{1}

(3.4)

\displaystyle P_{1}=p_{1}+\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{2}\,\,\,;\,\,\,P_{2}=p_{2}-\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{1}\,\,.

(3.5)

Therefore, the original Hamiltonian in terms of
(x_{i},p_{i})
coordinate operators is revealed as
relation,

H=\dfrac{a(t)}{2}({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\dfrac{b(t)}{2}({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})+c(t)({p_{1}}{x_{2}}-{p_{2}}{x_{1}})\,\,\,;

(3.6)

where time dependent coefficients have the following structures,

\displaystyle a(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{f(t)}{M}+\dfrac{M{\omega^{2}(t)}\theta^{2}(t)}{4f(t)}

(3.7)

\displaystyle b(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{f(t){\Omega^{2}(t)}}{4M}+\dfrac{M{\omega^{2}(t)}}{f(t)}

(3.8)

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\dfrac{f(t)\Omega(t)}{M}+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)\theta(t)}{f(t)}\right].

(3.9)

Here, the appearance of the parameter
c(t)
can be remarked as a pure NC effect as it arises solely because of the NC parameters
\theta(t)
and
\Omega(t)
. It should also be mentioned that although our Hamiltonian given by Eqn.(
3.6
) is apparently identical to that in
[
83
]
, the time dependent coefficients in Eqn.(
3.9
) achieve different structures. The reason is that, while the system studied in
[
83
]
is a model of simple harmonic oscillator with dynamic noncommutativity, our study addresses the model of a time dependent damped harmonic oscillator in the same NC background as considered in
[
83
]
. Hence, the Hamiltonian coefficients are modified by
f(t)
, the damping factor in the present system.

3.2
Exact eigenstate of the model Hamiltonian

As performed in
[
83
]
, we also apply the Lewis technique
[
86
]
to solve our model system exactly. First, we construct a suitable structure of the time dependent Lewis invariant operator for the present Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
). Next, solving the Lewis invariant, such that

I(t)\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\epsilon\phi(x_{1},x_{2})

(3.10)

where
\epsilon
denotes the invariant’s eigenvalue corresponding to its time dependent eigenstate
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
, the eigenstate of
H(t)
can be obtained using the following relation

\psi(x_{1},x_{2},t)=e^{i\Theta(t)}\phi(x_{1},x_{2})\penalty\ ;

(3.11)

where
\psi(x_{1},x_{2},t)
is the eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
)) and
\Theta(t)
denotes the Lewis phase factor which is real.

3.2.1
The time dependent Lewis invariant

We begin by referring to the portion
2.3
in the previous chapter
2
where the construction procedure of Lewis invariant operator for a one dimensional system of a time dependent harmonic oscillator (actually studied by Lewis
et.al.

[
86
]
) is reviewed in detail. As per the methodology suggested by
[
86
]
and also followed in
[
83
]
, corresponding to
H(t)
Eqn.(
3.6
), we chose the following ansatz of the invariant,

I(t)=\alpha(t)({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\beta(t)({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})+\gamma(t)(x_{1}{p_{1}}+p_{2}{x_{2}}).

(3.12)

Now, implementing the property of invariance (Eqn.(
2.2
)) into the form of
I(t)
defined by
Eqn.(
3.12
) and comparing the invariant’s coefficients, we have the following differential equations,

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-a(t)\gamma(t)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\dot{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b(t)\gamma(t)

(3.14)

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left[\,b(t)\alpha(t)-\beta(t)a(t)\,\right]

(3.15)

The subsequent task, as shown in
[
86
,
83
]
, is to parametrize
\alpha(t)=\rho^{2}(t)
; where
\rho(t)
is an auxiliary parameter invented to simplify the computational procedure. In order to express the other coefficients in terms of
\rho(t)
, we substitute the above parametrization into the above relations. Hence, we find out

\displaystyle\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{2\rho\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}

(3.16)

\displaystyle\beta(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{a(t)}\left[\dfrac{{\dot{\rho}^{2}}}{a(t)}+{{\rho}^{2}}b+\dfrac{\rho\ddot{\rho}}{a(t)}-\dfrac{\rho\dot{\rho}\dot{a}}{a^{2}}\right].

(3.17)

Substituting the value of
\beta
in Eqn.(
3.14
), the non linear Ermakov-Pinney (EP) equation that includes a dissipative
term
[
83
,
87
,
88
]
, arises in the following form,

\ddot{\rho}-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\dot{\rho}+ab\rho={\xi^{2}}\dfrac{a^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\penalty\ .

(3.18)

where
{\xi^{2}}
is an integration constant. The above form of the EP equation exactly matches with the same in
[
83
]
. At this point, we must remember that, due to the presence of the damping factor
f(t)
, the explicit form of the time dependent
coefficients in the Hamiltonian in Eqn.(
3.6
) differs from the same in the Hamiltonian studied in
[
83
]
.

Now, from Eqn.(
3.17
), we can gain the simplest form of
\beta
by utilizing the EP equation. It is as follows,

\displaystyle\beta(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{a(t)}\left[\dfrac{{\dot{\rho}^{2}}}{a(t)}+\dfrac{{\xi^{2}}{a(t)}}{\rho^{2}}\right].

(3.19)

Therefore, the hermitian invariant operator with regard to the EP parameter
\rho
is presented as,

I(t)=\rho^{2}({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\left(\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}}{a^{2}}+\dfrac{{\xi^{2}}}{\rho^{2}}\right)({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})-\dfrac{2\rho\dot{\rho}}{a}(x_{1}{p_{1}}+p_{2}{x_{2}}).

(3.20)

In the subsequent section, we will determine the suitable form of the ladder operators to solve the invariant system. Later, the entire system along with the ladder operators in terms of the Cartesian coordinates will be transformed into the polar coordinate system to simplify the calculation.

3.2.2
The ladder operators for solving the invariant system

Having established the required Hermitian invariant
I(t)
, we can now move on to calculate its eigenstates using the operator approach. This involves constructing the ladder operators and representing the invariant in respect of them. To achieve this, the invariant
I(t)
is required to be transformed (as expressed in Eqn.(
3.20
)) into a more tractable form. This transformation is accomplished by applying a unitary transformation with a unitary operator
\hat{U}
in an appropriate form. First, we begin by the following unitary operators,

\displaystyle\hat{U}=exp\left[-\dfrac{i\dot{\rho}}{2a(t)\rho}({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})\right],\,\,\,\hat{U^{\dagger}}\hat{U}=\hat{U}\hat{U^{\dagger}}=\textbf{I}.

(3.21)

Next we define

\displaystyle\phi^{{}^{\prime}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\hat{U}\phi(x_{1},x_{2})\,\,\,,\,\,\,I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}I\hat{U^{\dagger}}\,\,\;

(3.22)

where
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
denotes the invariant’s eigenfunction (Eqn.(
3.10
)). This yields

\displaystyle I^{{}^{\prime}}\phi^{{}^{\prime}}=\hat{U}I\hat{U^{\dagger}}\hat{U}\phi=\hat{U}I\phi=\hat{U}\epsilon\phi=\epsilon\phi^{{}^{\prime}}.

(3.23)

With the unitary operators in Eqn.(s)(
3.21
,
3.22
), the invariant is transformed as,

\displaystyle I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)=\rho^{2}({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\dfrac{{\xi^{2}}}{\rho^{2}}({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})\,\,.

(3.24)

The above form of the transformed invariant is structurally identical to the time dependent harmonic oscillator in two dimension. Now, it is very straightforward to have the following ladder operators,

\displaystyle{\hat{a}_{j}}^{{}^{\prime}}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\xi}}\left(\dfrac{\xi}{\rho}{x}_{j}+i\rho{p}_{j}\right)\,\,\,,\,\,\,{{\hat{a}_{j}}^{{}^{\prime}\dagger}}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\xi}}\left(\dfrac{\xi}{\rho}{\hat{x}}_{j}-i\rho{p}_{j}\right)

(3.25)

where
j=1,2
. Expectedly, the operators hold
[{{\hat{a}_{i}}^{{}^{\prime}}},{{\hat{a}_{j}}^{{}^{\prime}\dagger}}]=\delta_{ij}
.

Next, a reverse transformation is employed to generate the ladder operators in unprimed space. Those are found as,

\displaystyle\hat{a_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\hat{U}}^{\dagger}{\hat{a}_{j}}^{{}^{\prime}}\hat{U}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\xi}}\left[\dfrac{\xi}{\rho}x_{j}+i\rho{p_{j}}-\dfrac{i\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}x_{j}\right]

(3.26)

\displaystyle\hat{{a_{j}}^{\dagger}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\hat{U}}^{\dagger}{{\hat{a}_{j}}^{{}^{\prime}\dagger}}\hat{U}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\xi}}\left[\dfrac{\xi}{\rho}x_{j}-i\rho{p_{j}}+\dfrac{i\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}x_{j}\right].

(3.27)

Here also, the above set of operators hold
[{{\hat{a}_{i}}},{{\hat{a}_{j}}^{\dagger}}]=\delta_{ij}
.

The above operators can be combined linearly for achieving the appropriate form of the ladder operators. Therefore, setting
\xi=1
, we define
1
1

1

The ladder operator
\hat{a}(t)
should not be confused with the Hamiltonian coefficient
a(t)
,

\displaystyle\hat{a}(t)=-\dfrac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{a}_{1}-i\hat{a}_{2})=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\rho(p_{1}-i\,p_{2})-\left(\dfrac{i}{\rho}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}\right)(x_{1}-i\,x_{2})\right]

(3.28)

and

\displaystyle{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}(t)=\dfrac{i}{\sqrt{2}}({\hat{a}_{1}}^{\dagger}+i{\hat{a}_{2}}^{\dagger})=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\rho(p_{1}+i\,p_{2})+\left(\dfrac{i}{\rho}-\dfrac{\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}\right)(x_{1}+i\,x_{2})\right].

(3.29)

Once again,
[\hat{a},\hat{a}^{\dagger}]=1
. These are the final structure of the ladder operators for the purpose of solving the invariant.

3.2.3
Polar representation of the system

In order to to map our two dimensional system in terms of the polar coordinate variables, we intend to discuss how the momentum operator transforms from the cartesian coordinates
(p_{1},p_{2})
to the polar coordinates
(p_{r},p_{\theta})
.

Using the well known transformation of the position variables,

\displaystyle x_{1}=r\,cos\theta\penalty\ ,\penalty\ x_{2}=r\,sin\theta\,,

\displaystyle r=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \theta=tan^{-1}\dfrac{x_{2}}{x_{1}}

(3.30)

we can express the momentum vector
\vec{p}
(in the classical framework) in its polar form,

\displaystyle\vec{p}

\displaystyle\,=\,p_{1}\,\hat{i}\,+\,p_{2}\,\hat{j}\,=\,p_{r}\,\hat{r}\,+\,\dfrac{p_{\theta}}{r}\,\hat{\theta}

(3.31)

\displaystyle\,\Rightarrow\,m\dot{\vec{r}}\,=\,m\dot{r}\,\hat{r}+m\,r^{2}\dot{\theta}\,\hat{\theta}

(3.32)

obtaining the radial and azimuthal components of the momentum vector (in classical picture) as follows:

\displaystyle p_{r}=m\dot{r}=\dfrac{x_{1}\,p_{1}+x_{2}\,p_{2}}{r}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ p_{\theta}=m\,r^{2}\dot{\theta}=x_{1}\,p_{2}-p_{1}\,x_{2}\penalty\ .

(3.33)

In quantum mechanical framework, since, the commutator brackets
[\dfrac{x_{i}}{r},p_{i}]\,\neq\,0
, the operator
p_{r}
is expressed as shown:

\displaystyle p_{r}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\dfrac{x_{1}}{r}p_{1}+p_{1}\dfrac{x_{1}}{r}+\dfrac{x_{2}}{r}p_{2}+p_{2}\dfrac{x_{2}}{r}\right]\penalty\ .

(3.34)

Next, using the following commutator brackets

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{x_{1}}{r},p_{1}\right]\,=\,i\,\dfrac{sin^{2}\,\theta}{r}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \left[\dfrac{x_{2}}{r},p_{2}\right]\,=\,i\,\dfrac{cos^{2}\,\theta}{r}

(3.35)

and the following relation regarding the radial partial derivative

\partial_{r}=\dfrac{\partial\,x_{1}}{\partial\,r}\partial_{1}+\dfrac{\partial\,x_{2}}{\partial\,r}\partial_{2}\,=\,\dfrac{x_{1}}{r}\partial_{1}+\dfrac{x_{2}}{r}\partial_{2}

(3.36)

the polar structure of
p_{r}
in Eqn.(
3.34
) can be found in this manner :

\displaystyle p_{r}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\dfrac{x_{1}}{r}p_{1}+p_{1}\dfrac{x_{1}}{r}+\dfrac{x_{2}}{r}p_{2}+p_{2}\dfrac{x_{2}}{r}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\dfrac{x_{1}}{r}p_{1}+\dfrac{x_{2}}{r}\,p_{2}\right]-\dfrac{i}{2r}

\displaystyle=-i\left[\dfrac{x_{1}\partial_{1}+x_{2}\partial_{2}}{r}\right]-\dfrac{i}{2r}

\displaystyle=-i\,\partial_{r}-\dfrac{i}{2r}\penalty\ .

(3.37)

Similarly, the azimuthal part in polar representation can be written as

\displaystyle p_{\theta}=x_{1}\,p_{2}-p_{1}\,x_{2}=-i\partial_{\theta}\penalty\ .

(3.38)

Now, from the following form of
p_{r}
and
p_{\theta}
,

\displaystyle p_{r}=\left(cos\theta\,p_{1}+sin\theta\,p_{2}\right)-\dfrac{i}{2r}

(3.39)

\displaystyle p_{\theta}=\left(-r\,sin\theta\,p_{1}+r\,cos\theta\,p_{2}\right)

(3.40)

the polar representation of the momentum variables
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are derived as

\displaystyle p_{1}=-i\,cos\theta\,\partial_{r}+\dfrac{i}{r}\,sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}

(3.41)

\displaystyle p_{2}=-i\,sin\theta\,\partial_{r}-\dfrac{i}{r}\,cos\theta\,\partial_{\theta}

(3.42)

In both the cartesian and polar coordinate frame, the position and momentum operators hold the following commutator brackets,

[r,p_{r}]=[\theta,p_{\theta}]=[x_{1},p_{1}]=[x_{2},p_{2}]=i.

(3.43)

Those operators hold the following anticommutator brackets,

[r,p_{r}]_{+}=[x_{1},p_{1}]_{+}+[x_{2},p_{2}]_{+}=2(x_{1}p_{1}+p_{2}x_{2})

(3.44)

In an effort to express the invariant
I(t)
with regard to the polar coordinate variables, we require the following relations,

\displaystyle({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left({p_{r}}^{2}+\dfrac{{p_{\theta}}^{2}}{r^{2}}-\dfrac{1}{4r^{2}}\right)

(3.45)

\displaystyle(p_{1}+i{p_{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\theta}\left[p_{r}+\dfrac{i}{r}p_{\theta}+\dfrac{i}{2r}\right]

(3.46)

\displaystyle(p_{1}-i{p_{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\theta}\left[p_{r}-\dfrac{i}{r}p_{\theta}+\dfrac{i}{2r}\right].

(3.47)

Finally, polar representation of the invariant operator reads,

\displaystyle I(t)=\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}r^{2}+\left(\rho{p_{r}}-\dfrac{\dot{\rho}}{a}r\right)^{2}+\left({\dfrac{\rho{p_{\theta}}}{r}}\right)^{2}-\left({\dfrac{\rho}{2r}}\right)^{2}

(3.48)

and the same for the ladder operators read,

\displaystyle\hat{a}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\left(\rho{p_{r}}-\dfrac{\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}r\right)-i\left(\dfrac{r}{\rho}+\dfrac{\rho{p_{\theta}}}{r}+\dfrac{\rho}{2r}\right)\right]e^{-i\theta}

\displaystyle{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}\left[\left(\rho{p_{r}}-\dfrac{\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}r\right)+i\left(\dfrac{r}{\rho}+\dfrac{\rho{p_{\theta}}}{r}+\dfrac{\rho}{2r}\right)\right].

(3.49)

Both the Lewis invariant
I(t)

and the corresponding ladder operators are structured similarly to those employed in
[
83
]
for examining the undamped harmonic oscillator in NC space. However, it is important to emphasize that the time dependent coefficients in our current study are distinct because they account for the damping present in our system.

3.2.4
Eigenfunction of the invariant system and Lewis phase factor

In the conventional method, as followed in
[
83
]
, the initial crucial step for solving the invariant
I(t)
is to express it using the ladder operators. It is found that

I(t)\,=\,4\left[\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{p_{\theta}}{2}\right]\penalty\ .

(3.50)

However, another form of invariant which is more convenient for solving purpose, can be obtained from the above relation and hence, it can be defined (also done in
[
83
]
) that

I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)=\dfrac{I}{4}-\dfrac{p_{\theta}}{2}=\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)-p_{\theta}\penalty\ .

(3.51)

It is straightforward to check that the defined operator
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
also shows the property of invariance as
\dot{p_{\theta}}\,=\,[p_{\theta},H]\,=\,0
. Consequently, we proceed with Eqn.(
3.51
) as the Lewis invariant to construct the eigenstates of the Hamiltonian.

The commutator bracket
[I^{{}^{\prime}},p_{\theta}]\,=\,0
indicates that both the Lewis invariant operator and the angular momentum operator can share the same eigenstate. Thus, we represent the eigenstates of
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
and
p_{\theta}
as
\ket{n,l}
. Therefore, the eigensystem for both operators is found as follows:

\displaystyle I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)\ket{n,l}=\left(n+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\ket{n,l}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ p_{\theta}\ket{n,l}=l\ket{n,l}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \langle n,l|n,l\rangle=1\penalty\ ;

(3.52)

where the action of the ladder operators are implemented as

\displaystyle\hat{a}\ket{n,l}=\sqrt{n+l}\ket{n,l-1}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \hat{a}^{\dagger}\ket{n,l}=\sqrt{n+l+1}\ket{n,l+1}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \hat{a}^{\dagger}\,\hat{a}\ket{n,l}=(n+l)\ket{n,l}\penalty\ .

(3.53)

It is evident from the above equations that the integers
n
and
l
can ensure that
n+l\geqslant 0
. Consequently, by introducing another positive integer
m
such that
m=n+l
, we derive the condition
l\geqslant-n
.

Following the methodology outlined in
[
83
]
, our objective is to obtain the invariant’s eigenfunction with regard to the polar coordinate variables. To achieve this, we express the state vector in terms of the functional form in polar coordinate system as

\displaystyle\langle r,\theta|n,l\rangle=\phi_{n,l}(r,\theta)=\Phi_{n}(r)\,\Phi_{n}(\theta)\penalty\ ,

(3.54)

and the azimuthal part of the eigenfunction can quickly be generated as

\displaystyle p_{\theta}\,\phi_{n}(r,\theta)=l\phi_{n}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,\phi_{n}(r,\theta)\,=\,\Phi_{n}(r)\,e^{i\,l\,\theta}\penalty\ .

(3.55)

In order to find out the radial part
\Phi_{n}(r)
, we consider the following operation

\displaystyle\hat{a}\ket{n,-n}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \langle n,-n|n,-n\rangle=1;

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,\hat{a}\,\,\phi_{n,-n}(r,\theta)=0\penalty\ .

(3.56)

Substituting the first relation from Eqn.(
3.49
) into the above operation and using the last relation from Eqn.(
3.37
), we find out that

\displaystyle\dfrac{d\Phi_{n}}{dr}\,=\,\dfrac{\left(n\,a\,\rho^{2}-a\,r^{2}\,+\,i\,r^{2}\,\rho\,\dot{\rho}\,\right)}{a\,r\,\rho^{2}}\Phi_{n}

(3.57)

Hence, the eigenfunction of the invariant
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
, for the lowest value of the integer
l=-n
, can be integrated out as

\displaystyle\phi_{n,-n}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\,r^{n}e^{-i\,n\theta-\dfrac{a-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{2a{\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}\penalty\ ;

(3.58)

where
\lambda_{n}
is the normalization factor which is to be calculated from the following condition

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}\,r\,\phi^{*}(r,\theta)\phi(r,\theta)\,dr\,d\theta\,=\,1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\pi\,\lambda_{n}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,(r^{2})^{n}\,e^{-r^{2}/\rho^{2}}\,d\,(r^{2})\,=\,1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\lambda_{n}^{2}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{\pi\,n!\rho^{2n+2}}\penalty\ .

(3.59)

The most general (for any arbitrary value of
m
) form of the eigenfunction of the invariant can also be deduced from the relation

{(\hat{a}^{\dagger})}^{m}\ket{n,-n}=\sqrt{m!}\ket{n,m-n}\penalty\ .

(3.60)

As also found in
[
83
]
, the eigenfunction of
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
for the higher quantum states, can be presented as

\displaystyle\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\dfrac{{(i\rho)}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{a(t)-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{2a(t){\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)

(3.61)

where
\lambda_{n}
holds the value found in Eqn.(
3.59
) and
U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)
denotes the Tricomi’s confluent hypergeometric function
[
109
,
110
]
. However, the above functional form can also be written in relation to the associated Laguerre polynomial. Using the following relation

U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)=\dfrac{m!}{(-1)^{m}}\,L^{n-m}_{m}\left(\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\penalty\

(3.62)

we can express the eigenfunction in the following manner:

\displaystyle\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{i^{-m}\sqrt{m!}\,\rho^{m-n-1}}{\sqrt{n!\pi}}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{a-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{2a{\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}\,L^{n-m}_{m}\left(\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\penalty\

\displaystyle=Q_{n,m-n}(t)\,R_{n,m-n}(r,t)\,\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\,;

(3.63)

where

\displaystyle R_{n,m-n}(r,t)\,=\,r^{n-m}\,e^{-\dfrac{a-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{2a{\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}\,L^{n-m}_{m}\left(\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)

(3.64)

\displaystyle Q_{n,m-n}(t)\,=\,\dfrac{i^{-m}\sqrt{m!}\,\rho^{m-n-1}}{\sqrt{n!\pi}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\,=\,e^{i(m-n)\theta}\penalty\ .

(3.65)

In a later section, we will find that the previously mentioned structural form of the eigenfunction is extremely useful for calculating the expectation value of the Hamiltonian.

The eigenfunction
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)
adhere to the following condition of orthonormality,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}rdr\phi^{*}_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)\phi_{n^{{}^{\prime}},m^{{}^{\prime}}-n^{{}^{\prime}}}(r,\theta)=\delta_{nn^{{}^{\prime}}}\delta_{mm^{{}^{\prime}}}.

(3.66)

Lewis phase :

The process for obtaining this phase factor from Eqn.(
2.14
) closely resembles the one outlined in the latter portion of Section
2.3
. Following the derivation presented by
[
83
]
, we outline some key intermediate steps here. Utilizing the ladder operators’ actions from Eqn.(
3.53
), the derivation method of this phase begins as follows,

\displaystyle\dot{\Theta}_{n,l}

\displaystyle\,=\,\bra{n,l}\,(i\,\partial_{t}-H\,)\,\ket{n,l}

\displaystyle\,=\,\dfrac{1}{n+l}\,\bra{n,l-1}\,\hat{a}\left(i\,\partial_{t}-H\,\right)\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}\ket{n,l-1}

\displaystyle\,=\,\dfrac{1}{n+l}\,\left[\bra{n,l-1}\,[\hat{a},i\,\partial_{t}-H]\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}\ket{n,l-1}+\bra{n,l-1}\,(i\,\partial_{t}-H)\,\hat{a}\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}\ket{n,l-1}\right]

(3.67)

The value of the commutator
[\hat{a},i,\partial_{t}-H]
is essential for determining the phase factor. This calculation can be performed in either the Cartesian or polar coordinate systems since the result does not need to be expressed in terms of the coordinate operators. We have carried out this calculation in the Cartesian coordinate system, determining the value of
\partial_{t}\hat{a}
as follows,

\partial_{t}\,\hat{a}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{2}\,\left[\dot{\rho}\,(p_{1}-i\,p_{2})\,+\,\left(\dfrac{a}{\rho^{3}}-b\,\rho-\dfrac{i}{\rho^{2}}\dot{\rho}\right)\,(x_{1}-i\,x_{2})\right]\penalty\ ,

(3.68)

the
[\hat{a},i\,\partial_{t}-H]
can be presented as

[\hat{a},i\,\partial_{t}-H]\,=\,\left(c-\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\right)\,\hat{a}\penalty\ .

(3.69)

Again substituting the value in Eqn.(
3.67
), we come upto the following step,

\displaystyle\bra{n,l}\,(i\,\partial_{t}-H\,)\,\ket{n,l}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{n+l}\,\bra{n,l-1}\,(i\,\partial_{t}-H\,)\,\ket{n,l-1}\,+\,\left(c-\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\right)

(3.70)

After iterating the above expression
(n+l-1)
times,

\displaystyle\bra{n,l}\,(i\,\partial_{t}-H\,)\,\ket{n,l}\,=\,\bra{n,-n}\,(i\,\partial_{t}-H\,)\,\ket{n,-n}\,+\,(n+l)\,\left(c-\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\right)

(3.71)

Conveniently, as the vacuum state contribution, it can be chosen
[
83
]
that
i\,\bra{n,-n}\partial_{t}\ket{n,-n}\,=\,\bra{n,-n}\,H\,\ket{n,-n}
. Hence, the Lewis phase factor
\Theta(t)
is revealed as

\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)\,=\,(\,n\,+\,l\,)\,\int_{0}^{t}\left(c(T)-\dfrac{a(T)}{\rho^{2}(T)}\right)dT\penalty\ .

(3.72)

For any provided given value of
l
( since
l=-n+m
), it would take the form,

\Theta_{\,n\,,\,m\,-\,n\,}(t)=m\int_{0}^{t}\left(c(T)-\dfrac{a(T)}{\rho^{2}(T)}\right)dT\penalty\ .

(3.73)

Since the Hamiltonian coefficient
c(t)
originates solely from the NC parameter, its presence in the above phase factor highlights a pure NC effect, as also observed in
[
83
]
. However, due to the inclusion of a damping factor in our model, our explicit form of
c(t)
differs from that in
[
83
]
. Later, for various physical scenarios of damping, we will perform this integration for achieving the Lewis phase factor in a closed form.

Therefore,
\psi_{n,m-n}\,(r,\theta,t)
, the eigensystem of the Hamiltonian can be obtained by using Eqn(s).(
3.61
,
3.73
) and it reads

\displaystyle\psi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\Theta_{n,m-n}(t)}\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)

(3.74)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\dfrac{{(i\rho)}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\exp{\left[im\int_0^t \left(c(T)-\dfrac{a(T)}{\rho^2(T)} \right)dT \right]}

\displaystyle\times\penalty\ r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{a(t)-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{2a(t){\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\penalty\ ;

where
\lambda_{n}
is shown in Eqn.(
3.59
).

3.3
Explicit solutions for the noncommutative damped oscillator

In this study, our primary focus is on damped oscillators in NC space. Our objective is to determine the Hamiltonian eigenfunctions in various scenarios of damping. In an effort to design various physical circumstances of damping, we consider various explicit form of the time dependent parameters existing in the structure of Hamiltonian eigenfunction. However, those must be consistent with the non linear EP equation as given in Eqn.(
3.18
). The method for constructing these exact analytical solutions relies on the Chiellini integrability condition
[
93
,
94
,
95
]
, a formalism previously utilized in
[
83
]
, and adopted in our work as well. This means that the values of
a(t)
,
b(t)
, and
\rho(t)
must together solve the EP equation in a manner consistent with the Chiellini integrability condition. Now, we are about to demonstrate the solving procedure of the EP equation, which will be consistent with this present study of damped oscillator in NC space.

3.3.1
Solution set of non linear Ermakov-Pinney equation

We begin by providing the EP equation (setting
\xi
as
1
) found for our system

\ddot{\rho}-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\dot{\rho}+ab\rho=\dfrac{a^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\penalty\ .

(3.75)

Defining the following,

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}=\eta\penalty\ ,\penalty\ g(\rho)=-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ h(\rho)=a\,b\,\rho-\dfrac{a^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\penalty\ ;

(3.76)

the EP equation can be written as

\eta\dfrac{d\eta}{d\rho}+\eta\,g(\rho)+h(\rho)=0\penalty\ .

(3.77)

As per the Chilleni integrability condition
[
95
]
, if

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{d\rho}\left(\dfrac{h(\rho)}{g(\rho)}\right)=q\,g(\rho)\penalty\ ;\penalty\ (q=\text{constant})

(3.78)

then we consider the following as the solution of
\eta
,

\displaystyle\eta=\lambda_{q}\dfrac{h(\rho)}{g(\rho)}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \lambda_{q}=\dfrac{-1\pm\sqrt{1-4\,q}}{2\,q}\penalty\ .

(3.79)

The interesting consequence emerged from the above condition is that, for a specific value of
g(\rho)
or
h(\rho)
, the other parameters can be integrated out in the following manner:

\displaystyle\eta(\rho)\,=\,q\,\lambda_{q}\int^{\rho}\,g(\rho)\,d\rho\penalty\ ,\penalty\ h(\rho)\,=\,q\,g(\rho)\int^{\rho}\,g(\rho)\,d\rho\penalty\ ;

(3.80)

where the first relation can be found by substituting Eqn.(
3.79
) into Eqn.(
3.78
) and the second relation can directly be derived from Eqn.(
3.78
).

Alternatively, when
h(\rho)
is known, substituting Eqn.(
3.79
) into Eqn.(
3.78
) and rewriting
g(\rho)
in terms of
h(\rho)
and
\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{d\eta}{d\rho}

\displaystyle=q\,\lambda_{q}^{2}\,\dfrac{h(\rho)}{\eta(\rho)}\penalty\ .

(3.81)

From the above relation
\eta
can be found and substituting it in Eqn.(
3.79
),
g(\rho)
can also be obtained. They are given by

\displaystyle\eta(\rho)\,=\,\lambda_{q}\sqrt{2\,q\int^{\rho}\,h(\rho)\,d\rho}\penalty\

\displaystyle,\penalty\ g(\rho)=\dfrac{\,h(\rho)}{\sqrt{2\,q\int^{\rho}\,h(\rho)\,d\rho}}\penalty\ .

(3.82)

In
[
83
]
, a set of EP parameters is explicitly determined by applying various assumed values of
g(\rho)
in Eqn.(
3.80
). When
g(\rho)
remains constant, the resulting time dependent solution varies exponentially. On the other hand, if
g(\rho)
