increases non linearly with respect to
\rho
, the resulting solution varies rationally over time. The method for obtaining these solutions is described below.

Exponential EP solution

In this context,
g(\rho)
is assumed to be
\Gamma
, a positive, real constant. From Eqn.(
3.76
), we can directly derive the explicit form of
a(t)
as follows,

\displaystyle g(\rho)=-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\,=\,\Gamma

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,a(t)\,=\,\sigma\,e^{-\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ ;

(3.83)

where
\sigma
is a constant. Subsequently, by equating the second relation in Eqn.(
3.79
) with the third relation in Eqn.(
3.76
), we obtain the following relation,

\displaystyle h(\rho)\,=\,q\,\Gamma\int^{\rho}\,\Gamma\,d\rho\,=\,q\,\Gamma^{2}\,\rho

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,ab\rho-\dfrac{a^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\,=\,q\,\Gamma^{2}\,\rho\penalty\ .

(3.84)

By comparing the powers of
\rho
on both sides of the above equation, it becomes clear that
a(t),b(t),\sim,
constant and
a(t)\sim,\rho^{2}(t)
. Consequently, the explicit forms of the remaining EP parameters can be derived as,

\displaystyle b(t)\,=\,\Delta\,e^{\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \rho(t)\,=\,\mu\,e^{-\Gamma\,t/2}\penalty\ ;

(3.85)

where
\Delta,\mu
are real, positive constants.

It should be noted that, the constants used in the solution are not independent, they also satisfy the following constraint relation

\mu^{4}=\dfrac{\xi^{2}{\sigma^{2}}}{\sigma\Delta-\dfrac{1}{4}\Gamma^{2}}\penalty\ ;

(3.86)

which is found by substituting the EP solution set in Eqn.(
3.18
).

Rational EP solution

Here,
g(\rho)
is assumed to vary in a non linear manner with the function
\rho(t)
. Thus, from Eqn.(
3.76
),

\displaystyle g(\rho)=-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\,=\,\Gamma\,\rho^{k}\penalty\ ;

(3.87)

where
k
is a positive integer.
By using the second relation in Eqn.(
3.80
) and the third relation in Eqn.(
3.76
),

\displaystyle h(\rho)\,=\,q\,\Gamma\rho^{k}\int^{\rho}\,\Gamma\rho^{k}\,d\rho\,=\,q\,\Gamma^{2}\,\dfrac{\rho^{2k+1}}{k+1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,ab\rho-\dfrac{a^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\,=\,q\,\Gamma^{2}\,\dfrac{\rho^{2k+1}}{k+1}

(3.88)

By comparing the powers of
\rho
on both sides of the above equation, it becomes clear that
a(t)\sim\,\rho^{k+2}
and
a(t)\,b(t)\sim\,\rho^{2k}
. The explicit form of
\rho
can be obtained as

\displaystyle g(\rho)=-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\,=\,\Gamma\rho^{k}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,-\dfrac{(k+2)\dot{\rho}}{\rho}\,=\,\Gamma\rho^{k}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,\rho\,=\,\dfrac{\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{1/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{1/k}}\penalty\ ;

(3.89)

where
\chi
appears as an integration constant, such that
(\Gamma{t}+\chi)\penalty\ \neq\penalty\ 0

In this context, we aim to adjust the previously derived value of
\mu
, by multiplying it by
\mu
, a real and positive constant. This adjustment will allow us to detect the presence of the parameter
\rho
within a quantity that is explicitly computed using multiple parameters. This approach will become evident as we proceed to calculate the physical properties of the damped harmonic oscillator with noncommutativity. Thus, in our work,
\rho(t)
holds the value

\displaystyle\rho\,=\,\dfrac{\mu\,\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{1/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{1/k}}\penalty\ .

(3.90)

The previously found proportionality relation
a(t)\sim\,\rho^{k+2}
and
a(t)\,b(t)\sim\,\rho^{2k}
implies that

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{\sigma\,\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{\,(k+2)/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k+2)/k}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=\dfrac{\Delta\,\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{\,(k-2)/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k-2)/k}}\penalty\ ;

(3.91)

where the constant
\sigma
and
\Delta
have real, positive value. It is noteworthy to mention that, similar to the previous solution set, the constants adhere to the following relation

\Gamma^{2}\mu=(k+2)^{2}\,\left(\sigma\Delta\mu-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{3}}\right)\penalty\ ;

(3.92)

which is found by substituting the EP solution set in Eqn.(
3.18
).

Now, we are about to enter into the core of our work done in
[
89
]
which explores a class of exact solution of a noncommutative damped harmonic oscillator.

3.3.2
Exponential solution for model system

We begin by mentioning the exponentially varying EP solution set which is provided in
[
83
]
and is also reviewed in the previous section. The exponential EP solution read,

\displaystyle a(t)=\sigma e^{-\Gamma{t}}\,\,\,,\,\,\,b(t)=\Delta e^{\Gamma{t}}\,\,\,,\,\,\rho(t)={\mu}e^{-\Gamma{t/2}}

(3.93)

with the constraint relation

\mu^{4}=\dfrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{\sigma\Delta-\dfrac{1}{4}\Gamma^{2}}\penalty\ ;

(3.94)

where
\sigma,\Delta,\mu
and
\Gamma
are the real, positive constants.

We will proceed with finding the eigenfunctions of the Hamiltonian using the selected time dependent coefficients (EP parameters). To accomplish this, the explicit analytical form of
f(t)
and
\omega(t)
, the damping factor and the oscillator’s frequency respectively, are to be specified. Since, we are finding the solution using Lewis technique, our solution comprises two main components :
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)
, the eigenfunction of Lewis invariant
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
and
\Theta_{n,m-n}(t)
, the Lewis phase factor. The specific form of
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)
in Eqn.(
3.61
) (also Eqn.
3.63
)) implies that once the EP (exact parameter) parameters are determined, the explicit form of
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)
is fixed accordingly. However, the phase factor
\Theta_{n,m-n}(t)
can take various explicit forms depending on the specific choices of
f(t)
and
\omega(t)
. Thus, while
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)
remains fixed once the EP parameter are set, a class of solution can be generated corresponding to each type of
\Theta_{n,m-n}(t)
.

Eigenfunction of Lewis invariant

The invariant’s eigenfunction (Eqn.(
3.61
)) with regard to the exponential EP variables is derived as,

\displaystyle\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)=\lambda_{n}\dfrac{{(i{\mu}e^{-\Gamma{t/2}})}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{2\sigma+i\mu^{2}\Gamma}{4\sigma\mu^{2}{e^{-\Gamma{t}}}}r^{2}}U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}{e^{\Gamma{t}}}}{\mu^{2}}\right)

(3.95)

where
\lambda_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{\,2}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{\pi\,n!\,[\mu^{2}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})]^{1+n}}\penalty\ .

(3.96)

Lewis phase factors

The calculation of the Lewis phases, influenced by diverse choices of the damping scenarios, is undoubtedly an interesting part of this work. As previously mentioned, the integral form of this phase (Eqn.(
3.72
)) reveals a clear NC signature through the Hamiltonian coefficient
c(t)
. Given suitable explicit forms of
f(t)
and
\omega(t)
, the structural forms of the NC parameters
\theta(t)
and
\Omega(t)
can be derived. These NC parameters, in turn, define the form of
c(t)
, which can be integrated exactly according to the relation provided in Eqn.(
3.72
).

\langle A\rangle
Solution Set-Ia

Firstly, the damping factor is removed by setting
f(t)=1
. Consequently, the system’s dissipation attributes to
\omega(t)
, the angular frequency decaying exponentially with respect to time. Therefore,

\displaystyle\eta(t)=0\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,f(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\,\eta(s)\,ds\,=\,1

(3.97)

\displaystyle\omega(t)={\omega_{0}}\,exp(-\Gamma{t}/2)\,\,\,.

(3.98)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
3.7
,
3.8
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{2}{M\omega_{0}}\,\sqrt{M\sigma\,-e^{\Gamma t}}

(3.99)

\displaystyle\Omega(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\,M[\Delta\,exp\,(\Gamma{t})-M\omega_{0}^{2}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})]}.

(3.100)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.9
), we
get,

c(t)=\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,exp\,(\Gamma{t})-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})}{M}}\,+\,\omega_{0}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t}/2)\sqrt{\,M\sigma\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})-1\,}.

(3.101)

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
3.72
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,(\,n\,+\,l\,)\,\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{2\sqrt{M\sigma}\Gamma}\ \left[log_{e}\dfrac{e^{{\Gamma}t}-2M\sigma-2\sqrt{M\sigma(M\sigma-e^{{\Gamma}t})}}{1-2M\sigma-2\sqrt{M\sigma(M\sigma-1)}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-{\Gamma}t-2\sqrt{M\sigma(M{\sigma}e^{-{2\Gamma}t}-e^{-{\Gamma}t})}+2\sqrt{M\sigma(M\sigma-1)}\ \right]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{2(n+l)}{\Gamma}\,\left[\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{M}e^{{\Gamma}t}-{\omega_{0}^{2}}e^{-{\Gamma}t}}-\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-{\omega_{0}^{2}}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2i{\omega_{0}}\left\{e^{-{\Gamma}t/2}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\left(-\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4},\frac{{\Delta}e^{{2\Gamma}t}}{M\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)-{{}_{2}F_{1}}\left(-\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4},\frac{\Delta}{M\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\right\}\right]-\frac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}(n+l)t\penalty\ .

where
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c;t)
denotes the Gauss hypergeometric function.
Interestingly, the NC parameters derived for the designed damping scenario, found to be appropriate for the calculating the Lewis phase in a closed form. Additionally, it is noteworthy that the phase includes a complex part, implying a decaying wave function over time.

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
), for the selected case of damping, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
3.95
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
3.11
).

\langle B\rangle
Solution Set-Ib

Here, the system’s damping
f(t)
attributes to the exponentially decaying damping factor since the angular frequency
\omega(t)
is set to be a constant. Therefore,

\displaystyle\eta(t)=\Gamma\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,f(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\,\eta(s)\,ds\,=\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \omega(t)={\omega_{0}}.

(3.103)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
3.7
,
3.8
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{2}{M\omega_{0}}\sqrt{M{\sigma}\,-1}\,\,e^{-\Gamma t}

(3.104)

\displaystyle\Omega(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2e^{\Gamma{t}}\sqrt{M\,[\Delta\,-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}]}.

(3.105)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.9
), we
get,

c(t)=\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,}{M}}\,+\,\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}\,=\,constant\penalty\ .

(3.106)

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
3.72
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)\,=

\displaystyle(\,n\,+\,l\,)\left[-\frac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}\,+\,\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\,-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,}{M}}\,+\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}\right]\,t\penalty\ .

(3.107)

Once more, we can derive an exact expression for the phase, which varies linearly with time in this scenario. It should be marked that, for being in real domain, phase is significantly influenced by the parameters
\Delta
,
M
,
\sigma
, and
\omega_{0}
. The phase
\Theta_{n,l}
remains real if and only if
\Delta-M\omega_{0}^{2}\geq 0
and
M\sigma\geq 1
; otherwise, it becomes complex.

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
), for the selected case of damping, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
3.95
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
3.11
).

\langle C\rangle
Solution Set-Ic

Here, the system’s damping
f(t)
attributes to the damping factor and the angular frequency
\omega(t)
both decaying exponentially with respect to time. Therefore,

\displaystyle f(t)=exp\,(-\Gamma{t})\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \omega(t)={\omega_{0}}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t}/2).

(3.108)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
3.7
,
3.8
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{2}{M\omega_{0}}\sqrt{(M{\sigma}\,-1)}e^{-\Gamma{t}/2}\,\,

(3.109)

\displaystyle\Omega(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{M\,[\Delta\,exp(\Gamma{t})-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,\,]}\,\,e^{\Gamma t/2}.

(3.110)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.9
), we
get,

\displaystyle c(t)=\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,\exp\left[-\Gamma{t}\right]}{M}}+{\omega_{0}}\,e^{-\Gamma t/2}\sqrt{M{\sigma}\,-1}\penalty\ .

(3.111)

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
3.72
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\dfrac{(\,n+l\,)}{\Gamma\,\sqrt{M}}\,\left[\,\sqrt{\Delta}\,\Gamma\,t\,+\,2\sqrt{\Delta-M\omega_{0}^{2}}\,-2\sqrt{\Delta-M\omega_{0}^{2}{\exp\,(-\Gamma{t})}}\right.

(3.112)

\displaystyle\left.+\,2\sqrt{\Delta}\,log\,\left(\frac{\Delta+\sqrt{\Delta[\Delta-M\omega_{0}^{2}\,\exp\,(-\Gamma{t})]}}{\Delta+\sqrt{\Delta[\Delta-M\omega_{0}^{2}\,]}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle-\,(\,n+l\,)\left[\dfrac{\sigma\,t}{\mu^{2}}\,+\,\dfrac{2}{\Gamma}\,\omega_{0}\,\left(e^{-\Gamma t/2}-1\right)\sqrt{\,M\sigma-1}\right].

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
), for the selected case of damping, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
3.95
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
3.11
).

3.3.3
Rational solution for model system

We begin by mentioning the rationally varying EP solution set which is provided in
[
83
]
and is also reviewed in the previous section
3.3.1
. The rational EP solution set read

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{\sigma\,\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{\,(k+2)/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k+2)/k}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=\dfrac{\Delta\,\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{\,(k-2)/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k-2)/k}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \rho(t)=\dfrac{\mu\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{1/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{1/k}}

(3.113)

with the following constraint relation

\Gamma^{2}\mu=(k+2)^{2}\,\left(\sigma\Delta\mu-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{3}}\right)\penalty\ ;

(3.114)

where
k
holds the integer values and
\sigma
,
\mu
,
\Delta
,
\chi
and
\Gamma
are the constants that take real, positive values and ensure that
(\Gamma{t}+\chi)\penalty\ \neq\penalty\ 0
.

Eigenfunction of Lewis invariant

The eigenfunction of the invariant (which is given by
Eqn.(
3.61
)) takes on the following form for the rational solution set of EP equation:

\displaystyle\phi_{n\,,\,m-n}(r,\theta)=\lambda_{n}\,\dfrac{{(i\mu)}^{\,m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\left[\dfrac{k+2}{k(\Gamma{t}+\chi)}\right]^{m/k}r^{n-m}e^{i\theta(m-n)-\dfrac{[\sigma\,(k+2)\,+\,i\mu^{2}\Gamma]\,\,(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{2/k}\,\,\,k^{2/k}}{2\sigma\,(k+2)^{\,(k+2)/k}\mu^{2}}r^{2}}

\displaystyle\times\,\,\,U\left(-m,1-m+n,\,\dfrac{r^{2}[k(\Gamma{t}+\chi)]^{2/k}}{\mu^{2}\left(k+2\right)^{2/k}}\,\right)

(3.115)

where
\lambda_{n}
is computed as

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{\,2}=\dfrac{1}{\pi\,n!\mu^{2n+2}}\left[\dfrac{k(\Gamma{t}+\chi)}{k+2}\right]^{2(1+n)/k}.

(3.116)

Lewis phase factor

Once again, in order to compute the Lewis phase factor for constructing the Hamiltonian eigenfunction varying rationally with respect to time, we require the prepare the damping situation as per the rationally varying EP solution.

Here, the damping factor is removed by setting
f(t)=1
. Consequently, the system’s dissipation attributes to
\omega(t)
, the angular frequency decaying rationally with respect to time. Therefore,

\displaystyle\eta(t)=0\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,f(t)=1

(3.117)

\displaystyle\omega(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\penalty\ .

(3.118)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
3.7
,
3.8
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{2\,(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{M\,\omega_{0}}\,\sqrt{M\sigma\,\left[\dfrac{(k+2)}{k\,(\Gamma{t}\,+\,\chi)}\right]^{(k+2)/k}\,-\,1}

(3.119)

\displaystyle\Omega(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2\,\sqrt{M\Delta\,\left[\dfrac{k+2}{k(\Gamma{t}+\chi)}\right]^{\,(k-2)/k}\,-\,\dfrac{M^{\,2}\omega_{0}^{\,2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}}\penalty\ .

(3.120)

Now, for the simplification of the calculation, we chose
k=2
. This also ensures the achievement of an exact integrated form of the Lewis phase factor (Eqn.(
3.72
). The EP variables are reduced to be,

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{4\sigma}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,2}}\,\,,\,\,b(t)\,=\,\Delta\,\,,\,\,\rho(t)=\left[\dfrac{2\mu^{\,2}}{\Gamma{t}+\chi}\right]^{1/2}.

(3.121)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.9
), we
get,

c(t)\,=\,\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\,\sqrt{\dfrac{4\sigma\,M}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{2}}\,-\,1}\,+\,\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}\,-\,\dfrac{\omega_{0}^{\,2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{\,2}}}\,\,\,\,.

(3.122)

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
3.72
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n,l\,}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{(n+l)}{\Gamma}\,\left[\omega_{0}\,\,tan^{\,-1}\left(\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{M}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}-\omega_{0}^{2}}}\right)+\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{2\sigma}{\mu^{2}}\,log_{e}\,\frac{(\chi+\Gamma\,t)}{\chi}\right.

(3.123)

\displaystyle\left.-{\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{M}{\chi^{2}}-\omega_{0}^{2}}}-\omega_{0}\,\,tan^{\,-1}\left(\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{M}{\chi^{2}}-\omega_{0}^{2}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\omega_{0}(n+l)}{\Gamma}\left[\dfrac{\sqrt{4\,\sigma\,M-\chi^{2}}}{\chi}-\dfrac{\sqrt{4\,\sigma\,M-(\chi+\Gamma\,t)^{2}}}{(\chi+\Gamma\,t)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+ilog_{e}\dfrac{(\chi+\Gamma\,t)+{\sqrt{(\chi+\Gamma\,t)^{2}-4\,\sigma\,M}}}{\chi+\sqrt{\chi^{2}-4\,\sigma\,M}}\right].

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
)), for the selected case of damping, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
3.115
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
3.11
).

3.3.4
Elementary solution for model system

We propose a straightforward approach to solving the EP equation. The method involves the following steps. When
\rho(t)
is remained to be an arbitrary time varying function, if we express the rest EP parameters such that
a(t)=constant\times\dot{\rho}
and
b(t)=constant\times\frac{a}{\rho^{4}}
, those variables, in the generic expressions, would always be consistent with the EP equation, provided there is a specific constraint relationship abided by the constants.
Here we proceed with a special solution set generated from the above generic form of the EP variables. We refer to those as the phrase “elementary solution set”, which we read as

\displaystyle a(t)={\sigma}\,\,\,\,,\,\,\,b(t)=\dfrac{{\Delta}}{{(\Gamma\,t\,+\,\chi)^{4}}}\,\,\,,\,\,\rho(t)=\mu(\Gamma{t}\,+\,\chi)\penalty\ ;

(3.124)

\sigma
,
\mu
,
\Delta
,
\chi
and
\Gamma
are the constants that take real, positive values and ensure that
(\Gamma{t}+\chi)\penalty\ \neq\penalty\ 0
. Moreover, they adhere to the following constraint relation,

\Delta\mu^{4}=\sigma\,\,.

(3.125)

Eigenfunction of the Lewis invariant

The eigenfunction of the invariant (which is given by
Eqn.(
3.61
)) takes on the following form for the elementary solution set of EP equation:

\displaystyle\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\dfrac{{[i\mu(\Gamma\,t+\chi)]}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}r^{n-m}e^{i\theta(m-n)-\dfrac{\sigma-i\mu^{2}\Gamma(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{2\sigma\mu^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}r^{2}}

(3.126)

\displaystyle\times\,\,U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\mu^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}\right)

where
\lambda_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}=\dfrac{1}{\pi{n!}{\left[\mu(\Gamma\,t+\chi)\right]}^{2+2n}}\penalty\ .

(3.127)

Lewis phase factors

In order to prepare a suitable circumstances of dissipation such that it ensures the achievement of the Lewis phase in an exact form, we again consider the damping scenario chosen to compute the rationally varying eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian. Therefore,

\displaystyle\eta(t)=0\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,f(t)=1

(3.128)

\displaystyle\omega(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\penalty\ .

(3.129)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
3.7
,
3.8
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\dfrac{2\,(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{\omega_{0}\,M}\,\sqrt{M\,\sigma-1}

(3.130)

\displaystyle\Omega(t)=2\sqrt{\dfrac{M\Delta}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{4}}-\dfrac{M^{2}\,\omega_{0}^{2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}}\penalty\ .

(3.131)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.9
), we
get,

\displaystyle c(t)=\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{4}}-\dfrac{\omega_{0}^{2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}}\,+\,\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\sqrt{M\,\sigma-1}.

(3.132)

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
3.72
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)

\displaystyle=\,(n+l\,)\left[\omega_{0}\dfrac{\sqrt{M\,\sigma-1}}{\Gamma}\log\,\dfrac{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{\chi}-\frac{{\sigma}t}{\mu^{2}\chi(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\right]+\,\dfrac{(\,n+l\,)}{\Gamma}\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M\chi^{2}}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}-\omega_{0}^{2}}+\omega_{0}\left\{\tan^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\omega_{0}\chi}{\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\chi^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}\right)-\tan^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\omega_{0}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}}\right)\right\}\,\,\right].

(3.133)

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
), for the selected case of damping, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
3.126
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
3.11
).

Subsequently, an exotic model of the quantum damped harmonic oscillator, derived from the generalized solution set introduced at the start of this section, is also explored in
[
111
]
.

3.4
Energy profile of the model system

Here, we aim to establish the expression of the energy expectation value, as obtaining an exact analytical estimation of the energy dynamics will also allow us to illustrate its behaviour graphically for various kinds of model systems, as discussed in the previous section. For doing so, we require to derive the Hamiltonian’s expectation value in respect of the Hamiltonian’s eigenstate. Therefore,
\langle H\rangle
, the expectation value of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
3.6
)) is expressed as,

\langle H\rangle=\dfrac{a(t)}{2}(\langle{p_{1}}^{2}\rangle+\langle{p_{2}}^{2}\rangle)+\dfrac{b(t)}{2}(\langle{x_{1}}^{2}\rangle+\langle{x_{2}}^{2}\rangle)+c(t)(\langle{p_{1}}{x_{2}}\rangle-\langle{p_{2}}{x_{1}}\rangle)\,\,\,.

(3.134)

While the expectation values of individual canonical operators are given in
[
83
]
, we will demonstrate the detailed calculation procedure for some of these operators in the following relation. We also describe the procedure for obtaining a generalized structure of the matrix element of the canonical coordinate operators with arbitrary finite powers. The specific value of
\langle x_{i}^{2}\rangle
, where
i\,\in\,[1,2]
as provided in
[
83
]
, can be verified as a special case of this generalized outcome.

This explanation aims to provide an understanding to calculate the expectation values for the remaining operators in the mentioned relation. In this regard, we denote the Hamiltonian eigenstate as
|n,l\rangle_{H}
.

3.4.1
Matrix elements of the canonical coordinate operators with arbitrary finite powers

First, we calculate
{}_{H}\langle n,l|x^{k}|n,l\rangle_{H}
, the matrix elements (including
\hbar
) of the canonical coordinate operators with arbitrary finite powers. The concerned matrix element can be expressed as,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\penalty\ _{H}\langle n,m-n|r,\theta\rangle\langle r,\theta|r^{k}cos^{k}\theta|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}\,rdrd\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2^{k}}e^{i(\Theta_{n,m^{\prime}-n}-\Theta_{n,m-n})}\int r^{k+1}drd\theta\penalty\ (e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta})^{k}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \times\phi^{*}_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)\phi_{n,m^{\prime}-n}(r,\theta)

where
|n,l\rangle_{H}\penalty\ =\penalty\ e^{i\Theta_{n,l}}|n,l\rangle
, the relation that presents the Lewis theory, is substituted in the above equation.
The invariant’s (
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
) eigenfunction
\phi_{n,m^{\prime}-n}(r,\theta)
is expressed as
\phi_{n,m^{\prime}-n}(r,\theta)
=
\langle r,\theta|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle
. Now, we proceed with Eqn.(
) in the following manner.

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\pi}{2^{k-1}}\sum_{r=0}^{k}{{}^{k}C_{r}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2r-k}A(n,m,m+2r-k)

(3.136)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}r\penalty\ dr\penalty\ r^{2(n-m-r+k)}e^{\dfrac{-r^{2}}{\hbar\rho^{2}}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\hbar\rho^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle U\left(-m-2r+k,1-m-2r+k+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\hbar\rho^{2}}\right)

where
A(n,m,m+2r-k)=e^{i(\Theta_{n,m-n+2r-k}-\Theta_{n,m-n})}\lambda_{n}^{2}\dfrac{(-i\hbar^{1/2}\rho)^{m}(i\hbar^{1/2}\rho)^{m+2r-k}}{\sqrt{m!(m+2r-k)!}}
.

To simplify the calculation, we denote
w=-\dfrac{r^{2}}{\hbar\rho^{2}}
and we have

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{k}\frac{\pi}{2^{k}}e^{i(\Theta_{n,m-n+2r-k}-\Theta_{n,m-n})}(-1)^{k+r}i^{-k}\penalty\ {{}^{k}C_{r}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2r-k}

(3.137)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}(\hbar^{1/2}\rho)^{2n+k+2}\sqrt{m!(m+2r-k)!}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dww^{n-m-r+k}e^{-w}L_{m}^{(n-m)}(w)L_{m+2r-k}^{(n-m-2r+k)}(w)

where we have also substituted the following expression of the hypergeometric function
[
109
,
110
]
with regard to the associated Laguerre polynomials
L_{n}^{(\zeta)}(w)
,

\displaystyle L_{n}^{(\zeta)}(w)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}U(-n,\zeta+1,w)

(3.138)

Now, using the Lewis phase expression in
[
83
]
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{n,l}=(n+l)\int^{t}\left[c(\tau)-\frac{a(\tau)}{\rho^{2}(\tau)}\right]d\tau

(3.139)

we deduce the following,

\displaystyle e^{i(\Theta_{n,m-n+2r-k}-\Theta_{n,m-n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i[\{(n+m-n+2r-k)-(n+m-n)\}\int^{t}(c(\tau)-\frac{a(\tau)}{\rho^{2}(\tau)})d\tau]}

(3.140)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i(0+2r-k)\int^{t}(c(\tau)-\frac{a(\tau)}{\rho^{2}(\tau)})d\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\Theta_{0,2r-k}}.

Finally, a generic form of the matrix element of
x^{k}
is deduced as,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{k}\frac{\pi}{2^{k}}e^{i\Theta_{0,2r-k}}(-1)^{k+r}i^{-k}\penalty\ {{}^{k}C_{r}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2r-k}

(3.141)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}(\hbar^{1/2}\rho)^{2n+k+2}\sqrt{m!(m+2r-k)!}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\penalty\ w^{n-m-r+k}e^{-w}L_{m}^{(n-m)}(w)L_{m+2r-k}^{(n-m-2r+k)}(w).

This represents a new finding in the present study and both the matrix element and the expectation value of that coordinate operator with any arbitrary power can be derived by utilizing the above result. Additionally, the above matrix element can also be expressed with respect to the invariant’s eigenstate, where the Lewis phase factor would not be present. It reads,

\displaystyle\langle n,m-n|x^{k}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{k}\frac{\pi}{2^{k}}(-1)^{k+r}i^{-k}\penalty\ {{}^{k}C_{r}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2r-k}

(3.142)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}(\hbar^{1/2}\rho)^{2n+k+2}\sqrt{m!(m+2r-k)!}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\penalty\ w^{n-m-r+k}e^{-w}L_{m}^{(n-m)}(w)L_{m+2r-k}^{(n-m-2r+k)}(w).

Now, using our new result derived in Eqn.(
3.141
), we proceed to obtain the matrix element

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}
at first. It reads,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}=_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=1;\,r=0}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle+_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=1;\,r=1}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}.

(3.143)

The above matrix elements provide,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=1;\,r=0}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}=-\frac{i}{2}{(\rho\hbar^{1/2})}\sqrt{m}e^{-i\Theta_{0},1}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}+1}

(3.144)

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=1;\,r=1}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}=\frac{i}{2}{(\rho\hbar^{1/2})}\sqrt{m^{\prime}}e^{i\Theta_{0},1}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+1}.

(3.145)

In order to obtain Eqn(s).(
3.144
,
3.145
), we require some useful relations related to the the associated Laguerre polynomials,

\displaystyle L_{n}^{(\zeta)}(w)=L_{n}^{(\zeta+1)}(w)-L_{n-1}^{(\zeta+1)}(w)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\penalty\ w^{\zeta}e^{-w}L_{n}^{(\zeta)}(w)L_{m}^{\zeta}(w)=\frac{(n+\zeta)!}{n!}\delta_{n,m}\penalty\ .

(3.146)

Combining Eqn(s).(
3.144
,
3.145
), we have

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \frac{i}{2}{(\rho\hbar^{1/2})}[\sqrt{m^{\prime}}e^{i\Theta_{0,1}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+1}-\sqrt{m}e^{-i\Theta_{0,1}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}+1}].

(3.147)

In a similar way, we can find out the expectation value of
x^{2}
. Therefore,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{2}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}=_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=0}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle+_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=1}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}+_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=2}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}.

(3.148)

The above matrix elements provide,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=0}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}{(\hbar\rho^{2})}e^{-i\Theta_{0,2}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m-2}\sqrt{m(m-1)}

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=1}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}{(\hbar\rho^{2})}e^{-i\Theta_{0,0}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}(m+n+1)

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=2}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}{(\hbar\rho^{2})}e^{i\Theta_{0,2}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2}\sqrt{(m+2)(m+1)}.

(3.149)

For computing the above results, apart from Eqn.(
3.146
), the following relation is also required.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\penalty\ w^{k+p}e^{-w}L_{n}^{k}(w)L_{n}^{k}(w)\penalty\ =\penalty\ \frac{(n+k)!}{n!}\times(2n+k+1)^{p}\penalty\ .

(3.150)

So we have,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|x^{2}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\hbar\rho^{2})}{2}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}(m+n+1)

(3.151)

\displaystyle-\frac{(\hbar\rho^{2})}{4}\left[e^{-i\Theta_{0,2}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m-2}\sqrt{m(m-1)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+e^{i\Theta_{0,2}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2}\sqrt{(m+2)(m+1)}\right].

It must be mentioned that the the matrix elements derived in Eqn(s).(
3.147
,
3.151
) are exactly similar to those found in
[
83
]
. Although their results are provided in the invariant’s eigenstate, our results are obtained in the Hamiltonian eigenstate.
Similarly, the matrix element of
y^{k}
are deduced in the Hamiltonian eigenstate and it is found to be,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|y^{k}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{k}\frac{\pi}{2^{k}}e^{i\Theta_{0,2r-k}}\penalty\ {{}^{k}C_{r}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2r-k}

(3.152)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}(\hbar^{1/2}\rho)^{2n+k+2}\sqrt{m!(m+2r-k)!}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\penalty\ w^{n-m-r+k}e^{-w}L_{m}^{(n-m)}(w)L_{m+2r-k}^{(n-m-2r+k)}(w).

In the invariant’s eigenstate, the above result is expressed as,

\displaystyle\langle n,m-n|y^{k}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{k}\frac{\pi}{2^{k}}\penalty\ {{}^{k}C_{r}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2r-k}

(3.153)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}(\hbar^{1/2}\rho)^{2n+k+2}\sqrt{m!(m+2r-k)!}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dw\penalty\ w^{n-m-r+k}e^{-w}L_{m}^{(n-m)}(w)L_{m+2r-k}^{(n-m-2r+k)}(w).

Using Eqn.(
3.152
), we find out the matrix elements of
y
and
y^{2}
in the Hamiltonian eigenstate. Those are as follows,

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|y|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|y^{k}\mid_{\,k=1;\,r=0}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

(3.154)

\displaystyle+_{H}\langle n,m-n|y^{k}\mid_{\,k=1;\,r=1}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}{(\rho\hbar^{1/2})}[\sqrt{m}e^{-i\Theta_{0,1}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m-1}+\sqrt{m+1}e^{i\Theta_{0,1}}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+1}].

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|y^{2}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|y^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=0}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle+_{H}\langle n,m-n|y^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=1}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle+_{H}\langle n,m-n|y^{k}\mid_{\,k=2;\,r=2}|n,m^{\prime}-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\hbar\rho^{2}}{4}\delta_{m^{\prime},m-2}\sqrt{m(m-1)}e^{-i\Theta_{0,2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}{(\hbar\rho^{2})}(m+n+1)+\frac{\hbar\rho^{2}}{4}\delta_{m^{\prime},m+2}\sqrt{(m+2)(m+1)}e^{i\Theta_{0,2}}.

We observe once more that the structural form of the matrix element of the coordinate operator
y^{k}
in the Hamiltonian eigenstate aligns with the results presented in
[
83
]
for
k=1,2
, However, they reported these results in the invariant’s eigenstate.

While the results above have included
\hbar
, we use natural units (
\hbar=1
) when applying those results to calculate the energy expectation value, as we have been working in natural units from the beginning.

3.4.2
Expectation value of momentum operator raised to second power

Here, we outline (in natural unit
\hbar\,=\,1
)the method for determining
\langle p_{i}^{2}\rangle
, the expectation value of the square form of the momentum operator. The procedure is significant as it yields a time dependent value related to the momentum operator and it also provides insight while obtaining the expectation value of other physical quantities, such as
\langle p_{i}\rangle=0
,
\braket{x_{i}\,p_{j}}=\dfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon_{ij}(m-n)
, where
i,j=1,2
and
\epsilon_{ij}=-\epsilon_{ji}
with
\epsilon_{12}=1
.

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|\,p_{1}^{2}\,|n,m-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int rdrd\theta\penalty\ _{H}\langle n,m-n|r,\theta\rangle\langle r,\theta|\left(-i\,cos\,\theta\,\partial_{r}+\dfrac{i}{r}sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}\right)^{2}|n,m-n\rangle_{H}

(3.156)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int rdrd\theta\penalty\ \langle n,m-n|r,\theta\rangle\langle r,\theta|\left(-i\,cos\,\theta\,\partial_{r}+\dfrac{i}{r}sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}\right)^{2}|n,m-n\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\penalty\ \phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,\theta,t)\left(-i\,cos\,\theta\,\partial_{r}+\dfrac{i}{r}sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}\right)^{2}\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)\,rdrd\theta\penalty\ ;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5}\penalty\ .

We now calculate the terms
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},I_{4}
and
I_{5}
by Using the structure of
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)
in Eqn.(
3.63
).

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|\,p_{1}^{2}\,|n,m-n\rangle_{H}=\,I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5};

(3.157)

where

\displaystyle I_{1}=

\displaystyle-\int\,\phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,\theta,t)\,cos^{2}\theta\,\partial_{r}^{2}\,\left[\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)\right]\,r\,dr\,d\theta

\displaystyle=-Q^{*}_{n,m-n}(t)\,Q_{n,m-n}(t)\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,R_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,t)\,\left[\partial_{r}^{2}\,R_{n,m-n}(r,t)\right]\,r\,dr\,

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{2\Pi}\,\Phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(\theta,t)\,cos^{2}\theta\,\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\,d\theta\penalty\ ,

(3.158)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\int\,\phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,\theta,t)\,\left(\dfrac{sin\,\theta\,cos\theta}{r^{2}}\right)\,\partial_{\theta}\,\left[\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)\right]\,r\,dr\,d\theta

\displaystyle=-2\,Q^{*}_{n,m-n}(t)\,Q_{n,m-n}(t)\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\dfrac{R_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,t)\,\,R_{n,m-n}(r,t)}{r}\,dr\,

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{2\Pi}\,\Phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(\theta,t)\,cos\theta\,sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}\left[\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\right]\,d\theta\penalty\ ,

(3.159)

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=2\int\,\phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,\theta,t)\,\left(\dfrac{sin\,\theta\,cos\theta}{r}\right)\,\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}\,\left[\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)\right]\,r\,dr\,d\theta

\displaystyle=2\,Q^{*}_{n,m-n}(t)\,Q_{n,m-n}(t)\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,R_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,t)\,\partial_{r}\,\left[R_{n,m-n}(r,t)\,\right]dr\,

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{2\Pi}\,\Phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(\theta,t)\,cos\theta\,sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}\left[\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\right]\,d\theta\penalty\ ,

(3.160)

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=-\int\,\phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,\theta,t)\,\left(\dfrac{sin^{2}\theta}{r}\right)\,\partial_{r}\,\left[\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)\right]\,r\,dr\,d\theta

\displaystyle=-Q^{*}_{n,m-n}(t)\,Q_{n,m-n}(t)\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,R_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,t)\,\left[\partial_{r}\,R_{n,m-n}(r,t)\right]\,dr\,

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{2\Pi}\,\Phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(\theta,t)\,sin^{2}\theta\,\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\,d\theta\penalty\ ,

(3.161)

and

\displaystyle I_{5}

\displaystyle=-\int\,\phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,\theta,t)\,\left(\dfrac{sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}}\right)\,\partial_{\theta}^{2}\,\left[\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)\right]\,r\,dr\,d\theta

\displaystyle=-Q^{*}_{n,m-n}(t)\,Q_{n,m-n}(t)\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\dfrac{R_{n,m-n}^{*}(r,t)\,R_{n,m-n}(r,t)}{r}\,dr\,

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{2\Pi}\,\Phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(\theta,t)\,sin^{2}\theta\,\partial_{\theta}^{2}\,\left[\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\right]\,d\theta\penalty\ .

(3.162)

It is evident from the azimuthal components of Eqn(s).(
3.158
,
3.159
,
3.160
,
3.161
,
3.162
) that the terms
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
would provide a zero contribution. It can also be verified as,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\Pi}\,\Phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(\theta,t)\,cos\theta\,sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}\left[\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\right]\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\,i\,(m-n)\,\int_{0}^{2\Pi}\,\Phi_{n,m-n}^{*}(\theta,t)\,cos\theta\,sin\theta\,\Phi_{n,m-n}(\theta,t)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ .

(3.163)

Hence, we proceed with
I_{2}\,=\,0\,=I_{3}
.
Next, from Eqs.(
3.64
,
3.65
), we substitute the
R
,
\Phi
, and
Q
into the remaining variables
I_{1}
,
I_{4}
, and
I_{5}
. Then the variable
z
is invented and we made a parametrization
z\,=\,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}
. To make the calculation simpler, we employ several useful relations related to the associated Laguerre polynomials
[
109
]
. First, the derivative of this polynomials is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\left[L^{n-m}_{m}\,\left(z\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\,L^{n-m+1}_{m-1}\,\left(z\right)\penalty\ ;\penalty\ (m\,\geq\,1)

\displaystyle=\,0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ;(m=0)\penalty\ .

Apart from the above relation, we also use the identity relation and the orthonormality condition for this polynomial. Those are given by

\displaystyle L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,=\,L^{n-m+1}_{m}(z)-L^{n-m+1}_{m-1}(z)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\,z^{n-m}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{n}(z)\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,=\,\dfrac{\Gamma\,(2n-m+1)}{n!}\,\delta_{mn}\penalty\ .

(3.165)

We can generate the expressions of
I_{1},I_{4}
, and
I_{5}
by using the above relations (Eqn.[
3.165
]). Those are given by,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{m!\,(n-m)(n-m-1)}{2\,n!\,\rho^{2}}\,\int^{\infty}_{0}\,z^{n-m-1}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,dz\,

\displaystyle+\,(2n+2m+1)\,\dfrac{f}{2\rho}-\dfrac{f^{2}}{2}(m+n+1)+(2n-2m+1)\dfrac{m!}{\rho^{2}\,n!}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}z^{n-m}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)L^{n-m+1}_{m-1}(z)\,dz

\displaystyle-\dfrac{2\,m!}{n!\rho^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,z^{n-m+1}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)L^{n-m+2}_{m-2}(z)\,dz,

(3.166)

\displaystyle I_{4}=-\dfrac{m!\,(n-m)}{2\,n!\,\rho^{2}}\,\int^{\infty}_{0}\,z^{n-m-1}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,dz\,+\,\dfrac{f}{2\rho}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{m!}{\rho^{2}\,n!}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}z^{n-m}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)L^{n-m+1}_{m-1}(z)\,dz\penalty\ ,

(3.167)

and

\displaystyle I_{5}=\dfrac{m!\,(m-n)^{2}}{2\,n!\,\rho^{2}}\,\int^{\infty}_{0}\,z^{n-m-1}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,dz\penalty\ :

(3.168)

where
f=\dfrac{a-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{a\rho}
. From Eqn.(
3.156
), we have

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|\,p_{1}^{2}\,|n,m-n\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=\,\dfrac{2(n-m+1)\,m!}{n!\,\rho^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,z^{n-m}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,L^{n-m+1}_{m-1}(z)\,dz

\displaystyle-\,\dfrac{2\,m!}{n!\,\rho^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,z^{n-m+1}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,L^{n-m+2}_{m-2}(z)\,dz

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n+m+1)}{2}\,\left[\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}}{a^{2}}\right]\penalty\ .

(3.169)

Now a special identity (deduced in the Appendix) is to be utilized to find out the final result. The identity is given by,

(n-m+1)\,\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,z^{n-m}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,L^{n-m+1}_{m-1}(z)\,dz=\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,z^{n-m+1}\,e^{-z}\,L^{n-m}_{m}(z)\,L^{n-m+2}_{m-2}(z)\,dz\penalty\ .

(3.170)

Now, Eqn.(
3.169
) yields

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|\,p_{1}^{2}\,|n,m-n\rangle_{H}\,=\,\dfrac{(n+m+1)}{2}\,\left[\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}}{a^{2}}\right]\penalty\ .

(3.171)

In the similar way, it can also be obtained that

{}_{H}\langle n,m-n|\,p_{2}^{2}\,|n,m-n\rangle_{H}\,=\,\dfrac{(n+m+1)}{2}\,\left[\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}}{a^{2}}\right]

(3.172)

We should remember that, although the results are presented in
[
83
]
, the process used to obtain them is not included. By following the detailed procedure outlined above, verifying the following expectation value will become straightforward.

\displaystyle\braket{x_{j}\,p_{k}}=\dfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon_{jk}(m-n)\,\,;

(3.173)

where
j,k=1,2
and
\epsilon_{jk}=-\epsilon_{kj}
with
\epsilon_{12}=1
.

Now that we are convinced we have all the necessary expectation values to calculate the energy expectation value (Eqn.(
3.134
)), we will proceed directly to achieve our goal.

3.4.3
Energetics of the model system

We begin by mentioning the expectation value of the canonical operators which are needed to compute the energy expectation value Eqn.(
3.134
). Those are given by the following
relations,

\displaystyle\braket{x_{j}^{2}}=\dfrac{\rho^{2}}{2}(n+m+1)\,\,;\,\,\braket{p_{j}^{2}}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}}{a^{2}}\right)\,(n+m+1)\,\,;\,\,\braket{x_{j}\,p_{k}}=\dfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon_{jk}(m-n)\,\,;

(3.174)

where
j,k=1,2
and
\epsilon_{jk}=-\epsilon_{kj}
with
\epsilon_{12}=1
. So, the energy expectation value
\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}
with regard to
\psi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)
, the Hamiltonian eigenstate, is found to be,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}=\dfrac{1}{2}\,(n+m+1)\left[b(t)\rho^{2}(t)+\dfrac{a(t)}{\rho^{2}(t)}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}(t)}{a(t)}\right]+c(t)\,(n-m)\,\,.

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\,(n+m+1)\left(b(t)\rho^{2}(t)+\dfrac{a(t)}{\rho^{2}(t)}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}(t)}{a(t)}\right)+(n-m)\left(\dfrac{f(t)\Omega(t)}{M}+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)\theta(t)}{f(t)}\right)\right].

(3.175)

It is noteworthy that even as the oscillation frequency
\omega
approaches zero, the expectation value of energy remains non zero. This occurs by reason of that
a(t)
,
b(t)
, and
c(t)
, the Hamiltonian coefficients remain finite as
\omega
approaches zero, as evident from Eqs. (
3.7
), (
3.8
), and (
3.9
).

We are about to explore another interesting part in this work. We will not only analytically examine the explicit form of
\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}
, under various damping conditions, but also graphically visualize the time dependent behaviour of the energy persisting in the system.

Energy profile for the exponential solution

The general form of the energy expectation value, for the exponentially varying EP solution set given by Eqn.(
3.93
), are deduced to be,

\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}=(n+m+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+c(t)\,(n-m)

(3.176)

where we have used the constraint relation given by Eqn.(
3.94
).

It is remarkable that in the general form of the energy expression mentioned above, time dependence is solely governed by the factor
c(t)
which (Eqn.(
3.9
)) disappears in the absence of NC parameters. Thus, the observed time dependence in the energy values obtained for the exponentially varying EP solution set can be attributed exclusively to the time dependent nature of the NC space.

\langle A\rangle
Solution Set-Ia

In this scenario of dissipation, we have chosen
f(t)=1
and
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}\,e^{-\Gamma\,t/2}
. The energy expectation value in the ground state is computed to be

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\,n\,\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,exp\,(\Gamma{t})-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})}{M}}\,\right.

(3.177)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \left.+\,\omega_{0}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t/2})\sqrt{\,M\sigma\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})-1\,}\right].

From Eqn.(
3.177
), it is evident that the energy expression turns complex after a specific value of time. The criterion for ensuring the energy expression remains real is given by:

\displaystyle M\,\sigma\,e^{-\Gamma\,t}\geq 1\,\,\Rightarrow\,t\leq\,\dfrac{ln(M\,\sigma)}{\Gamma}

(3.178)

\displaystyle e^{2\Gamma t}\geq\frac{M\omega_{0}^{2}}{\Delta}\,\,\Rightarrow\,t\geq\,\frac{1}{2\Gamma}\ln{\left(\frac{M\omega_{0}^2}{\Delta}\right)}\penalty\ .

(3.179)

Thus, we have the following time range within which the energy remains physical.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\Gamma}\ln{\left(\frac{M\omega_{0}^2}{\Delta}\right)}\leq t\leq\dfrac{ln(M\,\sigma)}{\Gamma}\penalty\ .

(3.180)

It is relevant to mention that the above restriction in time axis aligns with the hermiticity of the Hamiltonian operator (Eqn.
3.6
). For
t>ln(M\,\sigma)/\Gamma
, as indicated by Eqn.(
3.99
),
\theta(t)
becomes complex, making the time dependent coefficient
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.101
) complex as well. Similarly, for
t<\frac{1}{2\Gamma}\ln{\left(\frac{M\omega_{0}^2}{\Delta}\right)}
, Eqn.(
3.100
) shows that
\Omega(t)
turns out to be complex, leading the Hamiltonian parameter
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.101
) to be complex again. Consequently, the Hamiltonian operator (Eqn.
3.6
) is no longer hermitian outside this time range. Therefore, the restriction found in time axis, for achieving a physical energy expression, is consistent with the property of hermiticity of the Hamiltonian, as it remains hermitian only within that specific time range.

Figure 3.1:
Graph of the exponential energy expectation value (divided by
\omega_{0}
to eliminate the dimension), with respect to
\Gamma
t (also a dimensionless parameter). The numerical value (in natural units) of the constants are chosen as
M,\,\Gamma,\,\mu\,=\,1
;
\sigma,\,\Delta
=
10^{7}
;
\omega_{0}
=
10^{3}
and the quantum state is fixed as
m=0,n=1
. The energy expectation value
\langle E\rangle
is computed when
\langle A\rangle
Set-IA :
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}e^{-{\Gamma}t/2}
and
f(t)=1
;
\langle B\rangle
Set-IB :
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}
and
f(t)=e^{-{\Gamma}t}
;
\langle C\rangle

Set-IC :
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}e^{-{\Gamma}t/2}
and
f(t)=e^{-{\Gamma}t}
. For
\langle A\rangle
, the energy initially decays and then starts to grow with respect to time. For
\langle B\rangle
, the energy is always constant over time. For
\langle C\rangle
, the energy decreases and tends to zero over time.

It is evident from Fig.(
3.1
), that the energy exhibits an initial dissipation. However, after a certain time, it starts to grow over time. It happens in view of the fact that, at large time,
exp\,(-\Gamma{t/2})\approx\,0
, resulting in the following approximated expression of
energy,

E_{n,-n}(t)\approx\,(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\,n\,\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,exp\,(\Gamma{t})}{M}}\,\,

(3.181)

The increasing nature of the energy is reflection of the exponentially increasing Hamiltonian parameter
b(t)
as it becomes the dominant factor in the energy expression at large time. As shown in the exponential EP solution (Eqn.(
3.93
)), while
a(t)
decays with time,
b(t)
grows at the same rate to satisfy the EP equation.

\langle B\rangle
Solution Set-Ib

In this scenario of dissipation, we have chosen
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}
and
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}
. The energy expectation value in the ground state is computed to be,

\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\,n\,\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,}{M}}\,+\,\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}\right].

(3.182)

As shown in Fig.(
3.1
), the energy expectation value notably reduces to be a constant, which is consistent with Eqn.(
3.182
). This constancy is caused by the balancing effect among the various decaying and increasing time dependent factors
f(t)
,
a(t)
,
b(t)
those are varying at a same rate.

\langle C\rangle
Solution Set-Ic

In this scenario of dissipation, we have chosen
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}
and
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}\,e^{-\Gamma\,t/2}
. The energy expectation value in the ground state is computed to be,

\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\,n\,\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta\,-M{\omega_{0}}^{2}\,exp\left[-\Gamma{t}\right]}{M}}+{\omega_{0}}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t/2})\sqrt{M{\sigma}\,-1}\right].

(3.183)

It is evident from Fig.(
3.1
) that the energy dynamics exhibits a nice decaying nature, eventually stabilizing at a constant value at a very large time. This characteristic is also reflected in the plot depicting the variation of the expectation value of energy with time, as shown in Fig.(
3.1
).

Energy profile for the rational solution

The energy expectation value, for the rationally varying EP solution (at
k\,=\,2
) set given by Eqn.(
3.113
), takes the following form,

\displaystyle E_{n,-n}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{(n+1)}{2(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\left[2\left(\dfrac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}+\Delta\mu^{2}\right)+\dfrac{\mu^{2}\Gamma^{2}}{8\sigma}\right]

(3.184)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +n\left[\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\Gamma\,t+\chi}\,\sqrt{\dfrac{4\sigma\,M}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{2}}\,-\,1}\,+\,\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\dfrac{\omega_{0}^{\,2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{\,2}}}\right].

where we have used the constraint relation given by Eqn.(
3.114
).

The energy dynamics exhibits a fine dissipation similar to the dissipated harmonic oscillator in the canonical phase space. However, by reason of the NC parameters, the system cannot remain physical always. The system remains physical within a certain interval of time, which is as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma}\left(\sqrt{\frac{M}{\Delta}}\omega_{0}-\chi\right)\leq t\,\leq\,\dfrac{1}{\Gamma}(2\sqrt{M\,\sigma}-\chi).

(3.185)

The rationally varying energy remains physical within that particular interval of time for the similar reason as with the exponentially varying energy. The system’s Hamiltonian (Eqn.
3.6
) is hermitian only during this period because, beyond it,
\Omega(t)
and
\theta(t)
, the NC parameters turn imaginary. Consequently,
c(t)
, the Hamiltonian coefficient (Eqn.(
3.122
)) becomes complex, causing the Hamiltonian operator to lose its hermitian property.

Figure 3.2:
Graph of the rational energy expectation value (divided by
\omega_{0}
to eliminate the dimension), with respect to
\Gamma
t (also a dimensionless parameter). The numerical value (in natural units) of the constants are chosen as
M,\,\Gamma,\,\mu\,\chi\,=\,1
;
\sigma,\,\Delta
=
10^{7}
;
\omega_{0}
=
10^{3}
and the quantum state is fixed as
n=1,m=0
. The expectation value of
energy
\langle E\rangle
is computed when
\omega(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}
and
f(t)=1
.

From Fig.(
3.2
), indeed, we observe that the energy expectation value,
\langle E\rangle
, decreases over time according to a power law, consistent with the behaviour of rationally decaying solutions.

Energy profile for the elementary solution

The energy expectation value, for the elementary EP solution set given by Eqn.(
3.124
), is deduced to be,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}(n+1)\left[\left(\Delta\mu^{2}+\dfrac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}\right)\dfrac{1}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}+\dfrac{\mu^{2}\Gamma^{2}}{\sigma}\right]

(3.186)

\displaystyle+n\left[\dfrac{\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}+\dfrac{1}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M\,(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\right].

where we have used the constraint relation given by Eqn.(
3.125
).
Further, the constraint relation
\Delta\mu^{4}=\sigma
yields the following energy expression,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}(n+1)\left[\dfrac{2\sigma}{\mu^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}+\dfrac{\mu^{2}\Gamma^{2}}{\sigma}\right]+n\,\left[\dfrac{\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}+\dfrac{1}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M\,(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\right].

(3.187)

Here also, we found a specific limit of time beyond which the Hamiltonian becomes non hermitian, resulting in a non physical system acquiring an imaginary expectation value of energy. That limit of time is found to be,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\Delta}{M\,(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}\,\geq\,\omega_{0}^{2}\,\Rightarrow\,t\,\leq\,\dfrac{1}{\Gamma}\left[\dfrac{1}{\omega_{0}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}}-\chi\right].

(3.188)

As we discussed earlier, the above bound of time remains consistent with the hermiticity of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.
3.6
). Beyond this point in time, the Hamiltonian becomes non hermitian. This occurs because
\Omega(t)
turns out to be complex, causing the Hamiltonian parameter
c(t)
in Eqn.(
3.132
) to also become complex.

Figure 3.3:
Graph of the energy expectation value (divided by
\omega_{0}
to eliminate the dimension) corresponding to the elementary EP solutions, with respect to
\Gamma
t (also a dimensionless parameter). The numerical value (in natural units) of the constants are chosen as
M,\,\Gamma,\,\mu\,\chi\,=\,1
;
\sigma,\,\Delta
=
10^{7}
;
\omega_{0}
=
10^{3}
and the quantum state is fixed as
m=0,n=1
. The energy expectation value
\langle E\rangle
is computed when
\omega(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}
and
f(t)=1
.

As per the Fig.(
3.3
), the energy dynamics related to the elementary solution is again observed to follow a power law decay.

3.5
Summary

Here, the discussions in the present chapter is to be summarized. In this chapter, a two dimensional system of a damped harmonic oscillator with dynamic noncommutativity has been explored. The system is transformed into a canonical phase space by employing a variable shift known as the Bopp-shift, as described in existing literature. To solve the system using Lewis’s method, we developed a time dependent Hermitian invariant operator. This approach resulted in a nonlinear differential equation, commonly referred to as the Ermakov-Pinney (EP) equation. We begin by obtaining the Lewis invariant in terms of the cartesian coordinate variables. Next, we transform to polar coordinates and express our results accordingly. Using the operator approach, we determine the eigenstates of the invariant and derive the Lewis phase factor in an integrated form. According to Lewis theory, the hermitian invariant and the Lewis phase factor together construct the Hamiltonian eigenfunction in an exact form. Since the eigenfunction of the system is inherently linked to the EP equation, and we aim to study the eigenfunction under various damping factors and angular frequencies, we adopt two exact analytical solution sets of the EP equation from the literature
[
83
]
. We also provide a brief review of their derivation using the Chilleni integrability condition. The selected time dependent EP solutions from
[
83
]
are exponential and rational in nature. Additionally, we propose a very simple method for deriving another set of EP solutions, which we refer to as elementary solutions. With these three different sets of solutions, we design various damped scenarios by selecting the angular frequency and the damping factor explicitly. For each damped model, we compute the explicit eigenfunction of the invariant and calculate the time dependent NC parameters, enabling the exact integration of the Lewis phase factor. Consequently, a class of exact eigenstates for the damping system are generated. Next, in both the Hamiltonian eigenstate and the invariant’s eigenstate, we calculated the matrix elements of operators with a finite integer power. Additionally, we determined the expectation value of the momentum operator and the square form of that operator. Using these results, we computed the time dependent form of the energy expectation value for the system. With regard to the exponentially varying EP solutions, we identified three types of behaviour based on the damping factor and the oscillator frequency. When the damping factor is unity and the oscillator frequency decreases over time, the energy expectation value initially decreases before increasing. In this scenario, we also found a interval of time during which the energy dynamics behave physically. When the damping factor includes an exponentially decaying component and the oscillator frequency remains constant, the energy expectation value notably remains constant over time. When both the damping factor and the oscillator frequency decrease over time, the energy expectation value exhibits an exponential decay. With regard to the rationally decaying EP solution and elementary EP solutions, the energy dynamics are observed to follow the power law decay with a time interval within which the energy expressions remain real.

Chapter 4
Noncommutative Damped harmonic oscillator with magnetic field

The physicists have studied the Landau problem for a long time. The problem is regarding a dynamical charge particle affected by a magnetic field perpendicular to the particle’s two dimensional plane.
The discrete energy levels of such system are known as Landau levels which have pedagogical importance and various applications in literature. In this regard, we mention a very well known problem of a charged harmonic oscillator with a magnetic field perpendicular to the oscillator’s two dimensional plane. The previous investigations into this problem are documented in works such as
[
112
]
-
[
114
]
. Group theoretical techniques have been applied to studies in quantum Landau systems, notably in
[
115
]
, where it was significant to understand the Landau levels as local equivalence classes of the symmetry group of the configuration space. More recent studies on Landau levels are found in
[
116
]
,
[
117
]
. In
[
116
]
, by using su(2) and su(1,1) Lie algebras, the authors constructed the displaced number coherent states to explore the statistical features of those. The exited coherent states for the system of a charged particle placed in a uniform magnetic field were introduced in
[
117
]
. In a few recent communications
[
118
]
-
[
119
]
, the Landau problem was readdressed. Specially, in
[
119
]
, the dynamics of the quantum states of a parametric oscillator was studied by employing the Lie transformation methodology. Additionally, we can introduce an electric field acting along the oscillator’s plane. In
[
120
]
, the charged harmonic oscillator having dynamic mass and frequency was considered. Under the influence of both magnetic field and dynamic electric field, the eigensystem of that oscillator was constructed. The problem would be more intriguing if the Landau problem is treated with the concept of noncommutativity. This scenario was previously examined in
[
121
]
, where a dynamic magnetic field was introduced in such a NC space where the position operators hold noncommutativity.

In our work detailed in
[
90
]
, which forms the subject of this chapter, we build upon this model by investigating the system with dynamic noncommutativity. Unlike
[
121
]
, our study includes both spatial and momentum noncommutativity, and considers the NC space to be time dependent. Additionally, we introduce a damping factor to the oscillator to design a more practical scenario.
Though, several studies have examined the effects of magnetic fields and damping in physical systems
[
122
]
-
[
125
]
, we aim to investigate such model system that incorporates both spatial and momentum noncommutativity. While the existing noncommutativity in momentum space in
[
89
]
suggests that the system is already in a time varying magnetic field, perpendicular to the configuration plane, we still plan to introduce an external time dependent magnetic field. This is because the noncommutativity is assigned to be an inherent property of our system, and therefore, we cannot alter the field associated with it. To explore the interaction between damping and an external time dependent magnetic field, and its effect on the energy dynamics of a charged oscillator in a time dependent NC space, it is necessary to apply an external magnetic field to our previous model
[
89
]
.

In our previous work
[
89
]
, we observed that, due to the presence of damping, the energy dynamics of the oscillator shows a nice decaying nature even in a NC framework. In this study, our goal is to examine how the energy characteristics of that previous system change when the various forms of time dependent magnetic fields are applied externally on that system.

4.1
Hamiltonian of the model system in NC space

Here, we start with the two dimensional time dependent Hamiltonian describing a model system consisting of a damped harmonic oscillator influenced by a dynamic magnetic field applied externally in a dynamic NC background.

Thus, within the NC framework, the Hamiltonian of the present system reads,

H(t)=\dfrac{f(t)}{2M}\left[(P_{1}-qA_{1})^{2}+(P_{2}-qA_{2})^{2}\right]+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)}{2f(t)}({X_{1}}^{2}+{X_{2}}^{2})\penalty\ \penalty\ ;

(4.1)

where
f(t)
denotes the damping factor and it is given by,

f(t)=e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\eta(s)ds}\penalty\ ,

(4.2)

where
\eta(t)
represents the friction coefficient. In literature, this structure of the damping factor is very well known when the friction coefficient is set to be a constant and a two dimensional Hamiltonian (placed in a standard quantum mechanical phase space) consisting such a coefficient of friction with a constant angular frequency is popularly known as Caldirola and Kanai Hamiltonian
[
107
,
108
]
. As we further explore this model in a NC framework, later, it will be observed that this kind of damping factor leads to a certain time intervals within which the system remains physical.

In order to introduce an external effect of a time varying magnetic field
B(t)
, the magnetic vector potential
A_{i}
in our Hamiltonian is chosen in the Coulomb gauge as,

A_{i}=-\dfrac{B(t)}{2}\epsilon_{ij}X^{j}\penalty\ \penalty\ ;

(4.3)

where
i,j=1,2
and
\epsilon_{ij}=-\epsilon_{ji}
with
\epsilon_{12}=1
. Apart from it,
\omega(t)
is the oscillator’s angular frequency varying with respect to time.
M
and
q
implies its constant mass and charge parameter respectively.

Since the system is considered in a dynamic NC space, the commutator brackets among the NC canonical operators existing in the Hamiltonian read (including
\hbar
)

\displaystyle[X_{j},X_{k}]\penalty\ =i\theta(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,

\displaystyle[P_{j},P_{k}]\penalty\ =i\Omega(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,[X_{j},P_{k}]\,=i\,\hbar\,\left[1+\frac{\theta(t)\Omega(t)}{4}\right]\delta_{jk};

(4.4)

\displaystyle(j,k=1,2\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{jk}=-\epsilon_{kj}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{12}=1)

where
\theta(t)
and
\Omega(t)
are the dynamic NC parameters for the configuration space
and the momentum space respectively. In the other hand, the canonical operators
(x_{i},p_{i})
in standard quantum mechanical phase space follow the commutator brackets
[x_{j},p_{k}]=i\hbar\delta_{jk}
,
[x_{j},x_{k}]=0=[p_{j},p_{k}]
; (
j,k=1,2
).

Next, the standard Bopp-shift relations
[
71
]
(
\hbar=1
) is to be implemented into the system for reconstructing the original Hamiltonian (i.e. NC Hamiltonian) in terms of the standard commutative canonical operators explicitly. Now, the Bopp-shift relations are given by,

\displaystyle X_{1}=x_{1}-\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{2}\,\,\,;\,\,\,X_{2}=x_{2}+\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{1}

(4.5)

\displaystyle P_{1}=p_{1}+\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{2}\,\,\,;\,\,\,P_{2}=p_{2}-\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{1}\,\,.

(4.6)

Therefore, the original Hamiltonian in terms of
(x_{i},p_{i})
coordinate operators is revealed as
relation,

H=\dfrac{a(t)}{2}({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\dfrac{b(t)}{2}({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})+c(t)({x_{2}}{p_{1}}-{x_{1}}{p_{2}})\,\,\,;

(4.7)

where time dependent coefficients have the following structures,

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{f(t)}{M}+\dfrac{qB(t)f(t)\theta(t)}{2M}+\dfrac{1}{4}\left[\dfrac{q^{2}B^{2}(t)f(t)}{4M}+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)}{f(t)}\right]\theta^{2}(t),

(4.8)

\displaystyle b(t)=\dfrac{q^{2}B^{2}(t)f(t)}{4M}+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)}{f(t)}+\dfrac{qB(t)f(t)\Omega(t)}{2M}+\dfrac{f(t)\Omega^{2}(t)}{4M},

(4.9)

\displaystyle c(t)=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\dfrac{qB(t)f(t)}{M}\left(1+\dfrac{\theta(t)\Omega(t)}{4}\right)+\dfrac{\Omega(t)f(t)}{M}+\left(\dfrac{q^{2}B^{2}(t)f(t)}{4M}+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)}{f(t)}\right)\theta(t)\right]\penalty\ .

(4.10)

It should be remembered that while the generic form of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
4.7
)) shares a similar form to those in
[
83
]
and
[
89
]
for studying a simple harmonic oscillator and a damped harmonic oscillator in dynamic NC space, the structural form of the Hamiltonian parameters (given by Eqn(s).(
4.8
)-(
4.10
)) for the present study are modified. This is because our current study examines the damped harmonic oscillator with an external time dependent magnetic field in a dynamic NC background. As a result, both
f(t)
and
B(t)
(the damping coefficient and the magnetic field respectively) influence and modify the Hamiltonian parameters from those studied in earlier communications
[
83
]
and
[
89
]
. Furthermore, the coefficient
c(t)
, unlike in our previous model
[
89
]
, can no longer be considered as purely NC effect. Since,
b(t)
, the influence of the external magnetic field is present, the coefficient
c(t)
would not vanish when the NC background are not considered in the scenario. Additionally, it is also important to note that these coefficients also differ from those obtained in
[
121
]
, where the NC parameter is a constant quantity and belongs to the configuration space only.

4.2
Exact eigenstate of the model Hamiltonian

Once again, we utilize the Lewis technique
[
86
]
to solve the model system. A comprehensive discussion of this method can be found in the previous chapters
2
and
3
. Therefore, we will only briefly mention that by solving the Lewis invariant, such that

I(t)\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\epsilon\phi(x_{1},x_{2})

(4.11)

where
\epsilon
denotes the invariant’s eigenvalue corresponding to its time dependent eigenstate
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
, the eigenstate of
H(t)
can be obtained using the following relation

\psi(x_{1},x_{2},t)=e^{i\Theta(t)}\phi(x_{1},x_{2})\penalty\ ;

(4.12)

where
\psi(x_{1},x_{2},t)
is the eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
4.7
)) and
\Theta(t)
denotes the Lewis phase factor which is real.

Eigenfunction of the Lewis invariant operator and Lewis phase factor :

The analytical structure of the invariant’s eigenfunction is given by (for the positive integers
n
and
m
, such that
n+l\,=m\,\geqslant\,0
)

\displaystyle\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\braket{r,\theta|n,m-n}\penalty\

(4.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\dfrac{{(i\rho)}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{a(t)-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{2a(t){\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right),

(4.14)

where
\rho
adhere to the non linear EP equation (by setting the integration constant as zero)

\ddot{\rho}-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\dot{\rho}+ab\rho=\dfrac{a^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\penalty\ ;

(4.15)

and
\lambda_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}=\dfrac{1}{\pi{n!}{(\rho^{2})}^{1+n}}\penalty\ .

(4.16)

Here,
U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)
denotes the
Tricomi’s confluent hypergeometric function
[
109
,
110
]
and the Hamiltonian eigenfunction
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)
holds the orthonormality condition,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}rdr\phi^{*}_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)\phi_{n^{{}^{\prime}},m^{{}^{\prime}}-n^{{}^{\prime}}}(r,\theta)=\delta_{nn^{{}^{\prime}}}\delta_{mm^{{}^{\prime}}}.

(4.17)

The structure of
\Theta(t)
, the Lewis phase factor is given by
[
83
]
,

\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)\,=\,(\,n\,+\,l\,)\,\int_{0}^{t}\left(c(T)-\dfrac{a(T)}{\rho^{2}(T)}\right)dT\penalty\ .

(4.18)

For any provided given value of
l
( since
l=-n+m
), it would take the form,

\Theta_{\,n\,,\,m\,-\,n\,}(t)=m\int_{0}^{t}\left(c(T)-\dfrac{a(T)}{\rho^{2}(T)}\right)dT\penalty\ .

(4.19)

Therefore,
\psi_{n,m-n}\,(r,\theta,t)
, the eigensystem of the Hamiltonian can be obtained by using Eqn(s).(
4.12
,
4.14
,
4.19
) and it reads

\displaystyle\psi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\Theta_{n,m-n}(t)}\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)\penalty\

(4.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\dfrac{{(i\rho)}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\exp{\left[im\int_0^t \left(c(T)-\dfrac{a(T)}{\rho^2(T)} \right)dT \right]}\penalty\

\displaystyle\times\penalty\ r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{a(t)-i\rho\dot{\rho}}{2a(t){\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right).

4.3
Explicit solutions for the model system

In this study, we intend to investigate how the solution evolves when a time varying magnetic field is introduced into the system. Our goal is to determine the eigenfunctions of the Hamiltonian that result from the interaction between the damping factor and the external magnetic field. The different types of the damping scenarios under the influence of the external magnetic field are designed by various explicit forms of the Hamiltonian coefficients those are
a(t)
,
b(t)
and
c(t)
. Here we should be careful because those explicit analytical forms must be consistent with the nonlinear EP equation (setting
\xi
as
1
) specified by Eqn.(
3.18
). The procedure of constructing the exact analytical EP solutions follows the Chiellini integrability condition
[
93
,
94
,
95
,
83
]
, which was detailed in the previous chapter. In the following discussion, for our present model system, we will derive a class of exact analytical solutions which adhere to the nonlinear EP equtaion.

4.3.1
Exponential solution for model system : Set-I

We begin by mentioning the exponentially varying EP solution set which is provided in
[
83
]
and is also reviewed in the previous chapter
3
. The solution set read,

\displaystyle a(t)=\sigma e^{-\Gamma{t}}\,\,\,,\,\,\,b(t)=\Delta e^{\Gamma{t}}\,\,\,,\,\,\rho(t)={\mu}e^{-\Gamma{t/2}}

(4.21)

with the constraint relation

\mu^{4}=\dfrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{\sigma\Delta-\dfrac{1}{4}\Gamma^{2}}\penalty\ ;

(4.22)

where
\sigma,\Delta,\mu
and
\Gamma
are the real, positive constants.

Eigenfunctions of the invariant

As we have already discussed in the previous chapter that once the EP (exact parameter) parameters are determined, the explicit form of
\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)
, the eigenfunction of the invariant is fixed accordingly. Thus, for the exponential EP solution set (Set-I), the analytical expression of the invariant’s eigenfunction is found to be

\displaystyle\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)=\lambda_{n}\dfrac{{(i{\mu}e^{-\Gamma{t/2}})}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{2\sigma+i\mu^{2}\Gamma}{4\sigma\mu^{2}{e^{-\vartheta{t}}}}r^{2}}U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}{e^{\Gamma{t}}}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\penalty\ ,

(4.23)

where
\lambda_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{\,2}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{\pi\,n!\,[\mu^{2}\,exp\,(-\Gamma{t})]^{1+n}}\penalty\ .

(4.24)

Lewis phase factors

Now, we are about to examine the interesting interplay among the three parameters,
B(t)
,
f(t)
and
\omega(t)
by calculating the exactly integrated form of the Lewis phase factor as it changes its explicit form depending on those chosen parameters.

\langle a\rangle
Set-I , Case I

The damping effect and the external magnetic field can be chosen as

\displaystyle f(t)=e^{\,-\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \omega(t)={\omega_{0}}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ B(t)=B_{0}\penalty\ .

(4.25)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
4.8
,
4.9
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\dfrac{8Me^{-\Gamma\,t}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}\,}

\displaystyle\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma e^{-2\Gamma\,t}}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}\,\left(M\sigma\,-1\right)}-\dfrac{qB_{0}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}{2M}\right]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\Omega(t)

\displaystyle=-qB_{0}\,+2e^{\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta\,-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}\,}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ .

(4.26)

In the limit as
B\rightarrow 0
, we verify that the NC parameters
\theta(t)
and
\Omega(t)
simplify to those found in
[
89
]
. Additionally, for a simpler case, where
t=0
(with
B_{0}=0
), it is straightforward to see that
\theta(t)
and
\Omega(t)
reduce to the constant parameters. This corresponds to the standard time independent NC context. Furthermore, these constants can be fixed as zero, thereby recovering the results associated with the standard quantum mechanical phase space.

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
4.10
), we
get,

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}e^{-\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma e^{-2\Gamma\,t}}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right.

(4.27)

\displaystyle\left.-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right]+\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
4.18
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)\,=(n+l)\int_{0}^{t}\left[c(t)-\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\right]\penalty\ dT=(n+l)\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}-\dfrac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}\right]t

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n+l)\omega_{0}\sqrt{(M\sigma-1)}}{\Gamma}\,log\,\dfrac{\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}\,e^{\Gamma\,t}+\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{2\Gamma\,t}}}{\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}+\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n+l)\omega_{0}}{\Gamma}\left[tan^{-1}\dfrac{\omega_{0}e^{\Gamma\,t}}{\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{2\Gamma\,t}}}-tan^{-1}\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}}\right]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n+l)\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{M\Gamma}\left[tanh^{-1}\dfrac{2M\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{2\Gamma\,t}}}{qB_{0}}-tanh^{-1}\dfrac{2M\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}}{qB_{0}}\right]

\displaystyle-\dfrac{(n+l)\sqrt{(\Delta-M\omega_{0}^{2})\sigma}}{\Gamma}\left[tanh^{-1}\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma+4M\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{2\Gamma\,t}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}}-tanh^{-1}\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma+4M\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}}\right]

\displaystyle-\dfrac{(n+l)\omega_{0}}{\Gamma}\left[tan^{-1}\dfrac{2M\omega_{0}e^{\Gamma\,t}}{qB_{0}}-tan^{-1}\dfrac{2M\omega_{0}}{qB_{0}}\right]+\dfrac{(n+l)\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{2\Gamma\,M}\,log\,\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.28)

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
4.7
), for the selected case of damping and applied magnetic field, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
4.23
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
4.12
).

\langle b\rangle
Set-I , Case II

The damping effect and the external magnetic field can be chosen as

\displaystyle f(t)=e^{-\Gamma t}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \omega(t)={\omega_{0}}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ B(t)=B_{0}\,e^{\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ .

(4.29)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
4.8
,
4.9
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\dfrac{8Me^{-\Gamma\,t}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}\,}\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}\,\left(M\sigma\,-1\right)}-\dfrac{qB_{0}}{2M}\right]\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Omega(t)=-qB_{0}\,e^{\Gamma\,t}+2e^{\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta\,-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}\,}\penalty\ .

(4.30)

It is important to note that in this case, the product of two dynamic NC parameters simplifies to a constant. Additionally, this constant is identical to the one found in another case, as discussed in Eqn.(
4.58
). Furthermore, it can be verified that when
B\rightarrow 0
, the explicit form of
\theta(t)
and
\Omega(t)
match those derived in
[
89
]
.

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
4.10
), we
get,

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right.

(4.31)

\displaystyle\left.-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right]+\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

At this point, for this present case, we are about to investigate the Hamiltonian coefficients in the standard quantum mechanical phase space. In the standard phase space, the Hamiltonian parameters take the following form,

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{f(t)}{M}\penalty\ ,

(4.32)

\displaystyle b(t)=\dfrac{q^{2}B^{2}(t)f(t)}{4M}+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)}{f(t)}\penalty\ ,

(4.33)

\displaystyle c(t)=\dfrac{qB(t)f(t)}{2M}\penalty\ .

(4.34)

For the case of exponential decay, we observe from Eqn.(
4.21
) that
a(t)=\sigma e^{-\Gamma{t}}
. Additionally, Eqn.(
4.29
) provides
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma t}
. Substituting these parameters into Eqn.(
4.32
), we obtain
\sigma=\dfrac{1}{M}
.
Using this result, along with the expression
b(t)=\Delta e^{\Gamma{t}}
for the exponentially decaying scenario in Eqn.(
4.33
), we derive the condition,
\Delta=\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{4M}+M\omega_{0}^{2}
.
Furthermore, substituting
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma t}
and
B(t)=B_{0}\,e^{\Gamma\,t}
into Eqn.(
4.34
), we get,

\displaystyle c(t)=\dfrac{qB_{0}}{2M}\penalty\ ,

(4.35)

which is found to be a constant. Later, the energy dynamics of the above case in the standard quantum mechanical framework will also be explored.
Now we restart our discussions in the NC framework again. With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
4.18
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)\,=\dfrac{(n\,+\,l)}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}
