\displaystyle\left[\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right]t+(n+l)\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}-\dfrac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}\right]t\penalty\ .

(4.36)

Here, for this case, the phase is found to be linear function of time. The phase brings back its form (solution set Ib) derived in
[
89
]
at the limit
B_{0}\rightarrow\,0
.

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
4.7
), for the selected case of damping and applied magnetic field, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
4.23
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
4.12
).

Later, under these choices of damping factor and frequency, it was shown in
[
126
]
that a purely periodic nature can be introduced to the energy dynamics of a model system of such a damped harmonic oscillator in a dynamic NC space if a time varying magnetic field can be tuned with the system in such a way that it varies sinusoidally with an amplitude that increases exponentially over time.

\langle c\rangle
Set-I , Case III

The damping effect and the external magnetic field can be chosen as,

\displaystyle f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \omega(t)={\omega_{0}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ B(t)=B_{0}\,e^{-\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ .

(4.37)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
4.8
,
4.9
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\dfrac{8Me^{-\Gamma\,t}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-4\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}}

\displaystyle\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma\,e^{-4\Gamma\,t}}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}-\dfrac{qB_{0}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}}{2M}\right]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\Omega(t)

\displaystyle=-qB_{0}e^{-\Gamma\,t}+2e^{\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.38)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
4.10
), we
get,

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-4\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma e^{-4\Gamma\,t}}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right.

(4.39)

\displaystyle\left.-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-4\Gamma\,t}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right]+\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
4.18
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n\,,\,l}(t)\,=(n+l)\int_{0}^{t}\left[c(t)-\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\right]\penalty\ dT=(n+l)\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}-\dfrac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}\right]t

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n+l)\omega_{0}\sqrt{(M\sigma-1)}}{2\Gamma}\,log\,\dfrac{\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}\,e^{2\Gamma\,t}+\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{4\Gamma\,t}}}{\omega_{0}\sqrt{M\sigma-1}+\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n+l)\omega_{0}}{2\Gamma}\left[tan^{-1}\dfrac{\omega_{0}e^{2\Gamma\,t}}{\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{4\Gamma\,t}}}-tan^{-1}\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}}\right]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n+l)\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{2M\Gamma}\left[tanh^{-1}\dfrac{2M\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{4\Gamma\,t}}}{qB_{0}}-tanh^{-1}\dfrac{2M\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}}{qB_{0}}\right]

\displaystyle-\dfrac{(n+l)\sqrt{(\Delta-M\omega_{0}^{2})\sigma}}{2\Gamma}\left[tanh^{-1}\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma+4M\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)e^{4\Gamma\,t}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}}-tanh^{-1}\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma+4M\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}}\right]

\displaystyle-\dfrac{(n+l)\omega_{0}}{2\Gamma}\left[tan^{-1}\dfrac{2M\omega_{0}e^{2\Gamma\,t}}{qB_{0}}-tan^{-1}\dfrac{2M\omega_{0}}{qB_{0}}\right]+\dfrac{(n+l)\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{4\Gamma\,M}\,log\,\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-4\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.40)

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
4.7
), for the selected case of damping and applied magnetic field, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
4.23
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
4.12
).

\langle d\rangle
Set-I , Case IV

The damping effect and the external magnetic field can be chosen as

\displaystyle f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \omega(t)={\omega_{0}}e^{-\Gamma\,t/2}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ B(t)=B_{0}\,e^{\Gamma\,t}\penalty\ .

(4.41)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
4.8
,
4.9
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\dfrac{8Me^{-\Gamma\,t}}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}(M\sigma-1)}-\dfrac{qB_{0}}{2M}\right]\,,\,

\displaystyle\Omega(t)=-qB_{0}e^{\Gamma\,t}+2e^{\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\penalty\ .

(4.42)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
4.10
), we
get,

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\left[\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}(M\sigma-1)}\right.

(4.43)

\displaystyle\left.-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\right]+\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\penalty\ .

Performing the integration in Eqn.(
4.18
), the Lewis phase in a closed form can be derived for this case also. However, we provide it in the Appendix.

4.3.2
Rational solution for model system : Set-II

We begin by mentioning the rationally varying EP solution set which is provided in
[
83
]
and is also reviewed in the previous section. The solution set is given by the following
relations,

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{\sigma\,\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{\,(k+2)/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k+2)/k}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=\dfrac{\Delta\,\left(\dfrac{k+2}{k}\right)^{(k-2)/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k-2)/k}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \rho(t)=\dfrac{\mu\left(1+\dfrac{2}{k}\right)^{1/k}}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{1/k}}\penalty\ ;

(4.44)

with the constraint relation

\Gamma^{2}\mu=(k+2)^{2}\,\left(\sigma\Delta\mu-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\mu^{3}}\right).

(4.45)

Here
k
takes integer value and
\sigma
,
\Gamma
,
\Delta
,
\mu
and
\chi
are the constants and they must ensure
(\Gamma{t}+\chi)\penalty\ \neq\penalty\ 0
.

Eigenfunctions of the Lewis invariant

For the rational EP solution set (Set-II), the analytical expression of the invariant’s eigenfunction is found to be,

\displaystyle\phi_{n\,,\,m-n}(r,\theta)=\lambda_{n}\,\dfrac{{(i\mu)}^{\,m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\left[\dfrac{k+2}{k(\Gamma{t}+\chi)}\right]^{m/k}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{[\sigma\,(k+2)\,+\,i\mu^{2}\Gamma]\,\,(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{2/k}\,\,\,k^{2/k}}{2\sigma\,(k+2)^{\,(k+2)/k}\mu^{2}}r^{2}}

\displaystyle\times\,\,\,U\left(-m,1-m+n,\,\dfrac{r^{2}[k(\Gamma{t}+\chi)]^{2/k}}{\mu^{2}\left(k+2\right)^{2/k}}\,\right)\penalty\ ,

(4.46)

where
\lambda_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{\,2}=\dfrac{1}{\pi\,n!\mu^{2n+2}}\left[\dfrac{k(\Gamma{t}+\chi)}{k+2}\right]^{2(1+n)/k}.

(4.47)

Lewis phase factors

As per the rational EP solution set, we must tune the oscillator’s parameters
f(t)
and
\omega(t)
, and the background field
B(t)
. To investigate the solution of
H(t)
under the assumption of coefficients that decay rationally, we opt for a rationally decaying
\omega(t)
and
B(t)
while setting
f(t)=1
. Thus, we have the following relations,

\displaystyle\eta(t)=0\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,f(t)=1\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \omega(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ B(t)=\dfrac{B_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}.

(4.48)

\langle e\rangle
Set-II , Case I

To investigate how the characteristics of the rationally decaying solution change under the influence of a magnetic field, we choose
k=2
, in Eqn.(
4.44
). Previously, this system was examined without the presence of any external field for this specific value of
k
as reported in an earlier publication
[
89
]
.
The simplified form of the rational EP parameters, for the value of
k=2
, are found to be

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{4\sigma}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,2}}\,\,,\,\,b(t)\,=\,\Delta\,\,,\,\,\rho(t)=\left[\dfrac{2\mu^{\,2}}{\Gamma{t}+\chi}\right]^{1/2}.

(4.49)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
4.8
,
4.9
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\dfrac{8M}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}}

\displaystyle\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{M}+\omega_{0}^{2}4\sigma M-\omega_{0}^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}-\dfrac{qB_{0}}{2M}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)\right]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\Omega(t)=-\dfrac{qB_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}+2\sqrt{M\Delta-\dfrac{M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}}\penalty\ .

(4.50)

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
4.10
), we
get,

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}+q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}\left[\left(\dfrac{4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}+\dfrac{2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}+4\omega_{0}^{2}\sigma M}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\dfrac{2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}}{\Gamma\,t+\chi}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sqrt{M\Delta(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{M(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\right]+\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\dfrac{\omega_{0}^{2}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}}\penalty\ .

(4.51)

In the above expression, the extra terms arising from the magnetic field are mostly observed to be decreasing functions of time. Their impact becomes clearer when examining how the energy expectation value evolves over time in a subsequent section. The precise form of the phase factor has been determined and is shown in the Appendix.

\langle f\rangle
Set-II , Case II

It is observed from Eqn.(
4.44
) that the rational EP solution set is not useful when
k=-2
. At this limit,
a(t)
and
\rho(t)
vanish and
b(t)
diverges. Thus, we fix the following solution set

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{\sigma}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k+2)/k}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=\dfrac{\Delta}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{\,(k-2)/k}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \rho(t)=\dfrac{\mu}{(\Gamma{t}+\chi)^{1/k}}.

(4.52)

Setting
k=-2
in the above solution set, we proceed with the following solution set,

\displaystyle a=\sigma\penalty\ \penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ b(t)=\dfrac{\Delta}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}}\penalty\ \penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \rho=\mu\sqrt{\Gamma\,t+\chi}\,.

(4.53)

The constants in the above solution set are constrained to hold a relation which is found by substituting that solution set in Eqn.(
4.15
). The relation is given by

-\mu^{4}\Gamma^{2}+4\sigma\Delta\mu^{4}=4\,\sigma^{2}\,;

(4.54)

which is similar to the constraint relation (Eqn.(
4.22
)) found for the exponential EP solution set.

The analytical expression of the invariant’s eigenfunction is found for the present solution set to be

\displaystyle\phi_{n\,,\,m-n}(r,\theta)=\lambda_{n}\,\dfrac{{(i\mu\sqrt{(\Gamma\,t+\chi})}^{\,m}}{\sqrt{m!}}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{2\sigma-i\mu^{2}\Gamma}{4\sigma\mu^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}r^{2}}\penalty\ U\left(-m,1-m+n,\,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\mu^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\,\right)\penalty\ ,

(4.55)

where
\lambda_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{\,2}=\dfrac{1}{\pi\,n![\mu^{2}(\Gamma\,t+\chi)]^{1+n}}.

(4.56)

Again, the damping effect and the external magnetic field are chosen as,

\displaystyle f(t)=1\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \omega(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ B(t)=\dfrac{B_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\penalty\ .

(4.57)

By substituting the above relations in Eqn(s).(
4.8
,
4.9
), the explicit structure of the NC parameters, for the present case, are calculated to be

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\dfrac{8M(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}-\dfrac{qB_{0}}{2M}\right]\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Omega(t)=2\dfrac{\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}-\dfrac{qB_{0}}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\penalty\ .

(4.58)

Again, it is interesting to observe that the multiplication of the two dynamic NC parameters yields a constant value. As previously discussed in Eqn.(
4.30
), this multiplication results in a constant value, which remains same in this scenario as well.

Substituting the above form of the dynamic NC parameters in the structural form of
c(t)
in Eqn.(
4.10
), we get

\displaystyle c(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{(4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}+q^{2}B_{0}^{2})(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\left[\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right.

(4.59)

\displaystyle\left.-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{M}\right]+\dfrac{1}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

With the explicit form of
a(t)\,,\rho(t)
and
c(t)
in place, we perform the integration given in Eqn.(
4.18
) and achieve the following closed form of the Lewis phase. The phase is explicitly found to be

\displaystyle\Theta_{\,n,l\,}(t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(n+l)}{(4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}+q^{2}B_{0}^{2})\Gamma}\left[\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{M}\right]ln\dfrac{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{\chi}+\dfrac{(n+l)}{\Gamma}\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}-\dfrac{\sigma}{\mu^{2}}\right]\penalty\ ln\dfrac{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}{\chi}\penalty\ .

The eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
4.7
), for the selected case of damping and applied magnetic field, can now be readily determined by connecting the above phase factor to Eqn.(
4.55
), following the relation outlined in Eqn.(
4.12
)

4.4
Energetics of the model system

Here we aim to analyse the energy dynamics of the model under different conditions, similar to our previous research. According to
[
89
]
, the generalized expression for the expectation value of energy
\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}
in the eigenstate of Hamiltonian
\psi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta,t)
, reads

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\,(n+m+1)\left[b(t)\rho^{2}(t)+\dfrac{a(t)}{\rho^{2}(t)}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}(t)}{a(t)}\right]+c(t)\,(n-m)\,\,.

(4.61)

With the structure of
c(t)
(Eqn.(
4.10
)), the energy is also represented as

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\,(n+m+1)\left[b(t)\rho^{2}(t)+\dfrac{a(t)}{\rho^{2}(t)}+\dfrac{\dot{\rho}^{2}(t)}{a(t)}\right]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{(n-m)}{2}\left[\dfrac{qB(t)f(t)}{M}\left(1+\dfrac{\theta(t)\Omega(t)}{4}\right)+\dfrac{\Omega(t)f(t)}{M}+\left(\dfrac{q^{2}B^{2}(t)f(t)}{4M}+\dfrac{M\omega^{2}(t)}{f(t)}\right)\theta(t)\right]\penalty\ ;

(4.62)

which reproduces the energy expression in
[
89
]
while setting
B\rightarrow\,0
.

The energy has explicit charge dependence. It includes terms that are both linearly and quadratically dependent on the charge. Consequently, the energy is not invariant when the particle’s charge changes sign. Even the energy contains a non zero, finite value when the applied field is switched off (
B\rightarrow,0
) and the oscillator’s frequency (
\omega{\rightarrow}0
) is also zero. This is evident from
Eqn.(s)(
4.8
,
4.9
,
4.10
) that the Hamiltonian parameters remain non-zero while setting
B{\rightarrow}0
and
\omega{\rightarrow}0
.

Next, in various damping scenarios, we will examine the evolution of
\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}
in the presence of the diverse form of the external magnetic field.

4.4.1
Energy profile for Solution Set-I : Exponentially decaying solution

The energy expectation value, for the exponentially varying EP solution set given by Eqn.(
4.21
), takes the following form,

\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}=(n+m+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+c(t)\,(n-m)

(4.63)

where we have used the constraint relation given by Eqn.(
4.22
).

Here, it should be noted that the time dependence of this general expression found for the exponential EP solution set, unlike that found in the previous work
[
89
]
, is not a pure NC effect. This is because the Hamiltonian coefficient
c(t)
does not vanish in the absence of NC parameters due to the presence of an externally applied magnetic field.

\langle A\rangle
Set-I  , Case I

Here we set
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}
,
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}
and
B(t)=B_{0}
. With this the energy expression for the ground state takes the form,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\dfrac{n}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}e^{-\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma e^{-2\Gamma\,t}}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right]+n\,\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}.

(4.64)

The nature of the energy expectation value is influenced by the values of the constants. In particular, the sign of the charge is crucial in determining this expectation value. Notably, in both the limits
t\rightarrow\infty
and
B\rightarrow\,0
the energy expression converges to the same constant value as found in
[
89
]
for a damped oscillator in time dependent NC space. Additionally, incorporating a constant magnetic field into the system studied in
[
89
]
causes the Hamiltonian to become non hermitian after a certain time limit, beyond which the energy becomes imaginary. The condition for getting the expectation value of energy to be real is as follows,

t\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{2\Gamma}\,ln\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M\omega_{0}^{2}(1-M\sigma)}\penalty\ .

(4.65)

It is noteworthy that the upper time limit, within which the energy expectation value remains real, is independent of the oscillator’s charge sign. However, the actual energy value does depend on it. Figure
4.1
illustrates how the energy expectation value changes over time. Initially, there is a decrease in energy, but over time, it stabilizes and becomes constant.

Now, we aim to determine if the energy exhibits time variation in the absence of damping (
f(t)=1
, that is for
\Gamma=0
). In this case,
Eqn.(
4.64
) has a time independent form given by,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}|_{\Gamma\rightarrow\,0}

\displaystyle=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\dfrac{n}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right]+n\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}

(4.66)

\displaystyle=constant\penalty\ .

So, it is seen that the energy reduces to a constant due to the removal of the damping factor. The energetics of the system in this scenario is the same as that found in the
next case, where damping is present, but the applied magnetic field is exponentially expanding.

\langle B\rangle
Set-I  , Case II

Here we set
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}
,
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}
and
B(t)=B_{0}\,e^{\Gamma\,t}
. With this the energy expression for the ground state takes the form,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\dfrac{n}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right]+n\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}

(4.67)

Interestingly, the exponentially decaying function
f(t)
and the exponentially increasing function
B(t)
counterbalance each other, leading to a constant energy value. It can also observed from Fig.
4.1
. In the limit
B\rightarrow\,0
, the constant value of energy reduces to the same obtained in
[
89
]
for a damped oscillator in time dependent NC space.

Our next step involves examining the energy profile of the system in commutative space. By employing the energy expression for the system under exponentially decaying conditions [given by Eqn.(
4.63
)] and utilizing the expression for
c(t)
[given by Eqn.(
4.35
)] for the corresponding
commutative background, we derive the following

\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+n\dfrac{qB_{0}}{2M}\penalty\ ;

(4.68)

which remains a constant as we vary time.

\langle C\rangle
Set-I  , Case III

Here we set
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}
,
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}
and
B(t)=B_{0}e^{-\Gamma\,t}
. With this the energy expression for the ground state takes the form,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\dfrac{n}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-4\Gamma\,t}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\left[-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}e^{-4\Gamma\,t}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}e^{-2\Gamma\,t}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma e^{-4\Gamma\,t}}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right]+n\penalty\ \sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.69)

The time evolution of the energy expectation value in Case III closely resembles that of Case I, as depicted in Fig.
4.1
. However, upon closer inspection, it is evident that in Case III, the energy decays at a faster rate compared to Case I, for the same set of parameters. It must be because in Case III, unlike in Case I, the applied field is decaying as well with respect to time. Furthermore, the maximum allowable time before the system becomes non physical (where the energy becomes non real) is half of that obtained by Eqn.(
4.65
). Here the bound is
found to be

t\leq\,\dfrac{1}{4\Gamma}ln\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M\omega_{0}^{2}(1-M\sigma)}\penalty\ .

(4.70)

The above study suggests an important inference that when an external magnetic field is present, the energy of a damped oscillator typically decreases over time if the field either diminishes over time or remains constant. However, if the field grows at a rate comparable to the decay of the damping factor, the oscillator’s energy tends to remain constant. Figure
4.1
illustrates this scenario when the applied field is turned off, demonstrating that the energy remains constant when
B=0
, consistent with the expressions for energy expectation values in Eqn.(s)[
4.64
,
4.67
, and
4.69
]. Even a constant magnetic field is able to bring about time
variation in this energy value.

. This also results in a constant energy value. This energetics actually match those in case II, where the damping effect is nullified in the presence of an expanding magnetic field.

Here we set
f(t)=e^{-\Gamma\,t}
,
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}e^{-\Gamma\,t/2}
and
B(t)=B_{0}e^{\Gamma\,t}
. With this the energy expression for the ground state takes the form,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}

\displaystyle=(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta+\dfrac{n}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}+4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\left[-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}{M}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}(M\sigma-1)}\right]

\displaystyle+n\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}e^{-\Gamma\,t}}\penalty\ .

The energy expectation value described above follows a decaying trend in the absence of a magnetic field, as demonstrated in
[

]
. For the parameters depicted in Fig.

, the energy initially decreases, then rises, and ultimately stabilizes as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Notably, if the applied magnetic field is switched off in this scenario, the energy, as depicted in Fig.
4.1
, decays over time without exhibiting the earlier observed exponential growth. Consequently, the oscillator’s energy remains notably lower in the absence of this exponentially growing field within the studied time interval. The system also possess two lower bounds of time below which it becomes non-physical due to the imaginary value of energy. The conditions are as follows,

\displaystyle t\penalty\ \geq\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{\Gamma}ln\left[\dfrac{M\omega_{0}^{2}}{\Delta}\right]\penalty\ ;\penalty\ t\penalty\ \geq\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{\Gamma}ln\left[\dfrac{4M\omega_{0}^{2}(1-M\sigma)}{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}\right]\penalty\ .

(4.72)

The greater of the two bounds serves as the actual lower bound.

In the preceding section, we examined two distinct solution sets derived from Eqn.(

\displaystyle+\dfrac{n}{4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}+q^{2}B_{0}^{2}}\left[-\dfrac{2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}}{\Gamma\,t+\chi}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sqrt{M\Delta(\Gamma\,t+\chi)^{2}-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{M(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\right.

which is a decaying function of time and is seen to reduce to the same obtained in

\dfrac{1}{\Gamma}\left(\omega_{0}\sqrt{\dfrac{M}{\Delta}}-\chi\right)\leq\penalty\ t\leq\penalty\ \dfrac{1}{\Gamma}\left[\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{M\omega_{0}^{2}}+4\sigma M}-\chi\right]\penalty\ .

(4.75)

In Figure

, we conducted a comparative analysis of how the energy varies over time under two conditions: with and without an applied magnetic field. The results indicate that in both scenarios, the energy diminishes over time. However, with the magnetic field applied, the oscillator’s energy is notably higher due to the additional magnetic energy present in the system.

. Although the energy in both cases decays rationally over time, the decay rate in case I is greater than in the other case. This is because, in case I, both parameters

and

Although the nature of the energy decay remains the same in both the presence and absence of the field, the energetics increase in value when the field is applied.

For the rationally decaying solution given by Eqn.(
4.53
), the energy expectation value takes the following form,

\braket{E_{n,m-n}(t)}=\dfrac{(m+n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta}{\Gamma\,t+\chi}+c(t)(n-m)\penalty\ .

(4.76)

where we have used the constraint relation given by Eqn.(
4.54
).
Here we set
f(t)=1
,
\omega(t)=\omega_{0}/(\Gamma\,t+\chi)
and
B(t)=B_{0}/(\Gamma\,t+\chi)
. With this the energy expression for the ground state takes the form,

\displaystyle\braket{E_{n,-n}(t)}=\dfrac{(n+1)\mu^{2}\Delta}{\Gamma\,t+\chi}+\dfrac{n}{(4M^{2}\omega^{2}_{0}+q^{2}B_{0}^{2})(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\left[-2qB_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}-\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}}{M}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(4M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}+2qB_{0}\sqrt{M\Delta-M^{2}\omega_{0}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{\dfrac{q^{2}B_{0}^{2}\sigma}{4M}+\omega_{0}^{2}(M\sigma-1)}\right]+\dfrac{n}{(\Gamma\,t+\chi)}\sqrt{\dfrac{\Delta}{M}-\omega_{0}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.77)

According to Fig.
4.2
, the energy expectation value decreases over time. In fact, it approaches zero for large times, albeit at a slower rate compared to Case I. This discrepancy is anticipated because in Case II, one of the Hamiltonian parameters,
b(t)
decreases over time while another parameter,
\rho(t)
increases. Consequently, the overall energy decreases as
b(t)
decreases faster (at a rate
\sim\penalty\ t^{-2}
) than

\rho(t)
increases (at a rate
\sim\penalty\ t^{1/2}
). Upon turning off the field under these conditions, Fig.
4.2
shows that the oscillator’s energy continues to decrease due to the diminishing angular frequency of the oscillator. However, in this scenario, the oscillator’s energy diminishes specifically because the magnetic energy component is absent from the system.

4.5
Summary

In this chapter, our work focused on examining the impact of an external time dependent magnetic field on a two dimensional damped harmonic oscillator in NC space. Initially, we established the Hamiltonian for our system under a general magnetic field in NC space. Using Bopp-shift relations, we then transformed this Hamiltonian into commutative variables. Subsequently, we derived the exact solution for this time dependent system with an applied time-varying magnetic field by employing the well known Lewis invariant, which leads to a nonlinear differential equation known as the EP equation. To generate a class of exact solutions for this system, similar to the initial work
[
89
]
, we consider two types of EP solutions, namely, exponentially decaying solutions and rationally decaying solutions. For each set of EP solutions, we design various model systems by selecting different explicit forms of the damping factor, angular frequency, and applied magnetic field. Consequently, we are able to obtain explicit, closed form solutions for various models. Next, we calculate the expectation value of the Hamiltonian. As expected, the expectation value of the energy changes over time. Similar to our previous work, we not only derived analytical expressions for the energy of various designed models but also graphically represented their energy profiles. In the exponentially varying scenario, an exponentially growing magnetic field can maintain the energy at a constant level despite damping, while a constant or exponentially decaying field causes the energy to decrease more rapidly due to damping. Interestingly, when an exponentially decaying frequency is present along with the damping factor, the system’s behaviour under the influence of an exponentially growing field becomes even more interesting. Initially, the energy decreases over time due to the damping, but later it begins to increase under the influence of the growing field. In some cases, we identified time regions, both bounded and unbounded, where the energy cannot remain physical as the system becomes non hermitian. In the rationally varying scenario, even without the damping factor, a rationally decaying magnetic field combined with a rationally decaying angular frequency can eventually dampen the oscillator’s energy. For Case I of the rational EP solution, the decaying oscillation cannot remain physical at zero energy due to the upper time bound. Conversely, for Case II, the energy physically dampens to zero over time. Additionally, we observe that at any given moment, the expectation value of energy is higher with the magnetic field present than when it is absent.

Chapter 5
Berry phase from Lewis phase for noncommutative periodic models

An adiabatic process involves the gradual change of external parameters
[
127
,
128
]
. Typically, such processes are characterized by two distinct time scales. One is the “internal time,” which relates to the motion of the system itself, while the other is the “external time,” describing the time range through which the system parameters achieve significant changes. As per the adiabatic processes, the “external time” should be very larger than the “internal time,” indicating that the Hamiltonian parameters vary very gradually with respect to time. In this regard, one can mention the motion of a simple harmonic oscillator (pendulum) placed on an platform oscillating gradually. The frequency of the oscillating platform is very much lesser than the same of the pendulum. For such adiabatically evolving quantum system, the quantum state of the initial Hamiltonian remains same after the Hamiltonian takes its final form after a certain time. In a simpler version, the system state does not change through out the adiabatic process. Nevertheless, a phase shift can be observed after the adiabatic evolution. As anticipated by M. V. Berry
[
129
,
130
]
, the phase picked up by the system is not only dynamic, evolving over time, but also geometric, depending solely on the geometry of the path traversed by the system. Such geometric phase is popularly known as ”Berry’s phase” in literature and it can be measured physically
[
128
]
. Berry phase is relevant in a vast areas in modern physics, especially regarding the topic of topological insulators
[
131
,
132
]
. Although Berry was initially concerned that the path dependent Berry phase might be dominated by a bigger dynamic phase, later it was ensured that the appropriate experiments can be fabricated to identify the geometric part of the phase
[
133
,
134
]
. In such experiments, the geometric phases acquired by the photons can be measured by the rotation of the polarization plane of light. If the plane of the polarized light rotates by an angle “
\alpha
”, the corresponding geometric phase measurement would be “
\pm\alpha
” depending on the spin (parallel or antiparallel) of the photons. The experimental verification of the geometric phase acquisition by spins during adiabatic evolution is well documented, particularly with neutrons
[
135
,
136
]
. There are certain conditions of achieving a non zero Berry phase. The minimum numbers of the periodic parameters in a Hamiltonian must be two. Moreover, the eigenfunction of that Hamiltonian must be complex in nature. With these conditions in place, the periodic Hamiltonian can pick up a geometric phase if it adiabatically evolves around a closed path on its parameter space over a full time period
T

[
137
]
. Nevertheless, this phase would be zero when the time reversal (TR) symmetry of the system is broken
[
138
]
. In the theoretical perspective, after being introduced with the concept of geometric phase in adiabatically evolving systems, the physicists got interested in studying these phases for both classical and quantum systems
[
139
]
. The studies regarding the generalized quantum harmonic oscillators which is capable to provide a geometric phase have been very well known over a long time
[
140
,
141
,
142
]
. In this regard, we wish to highlight an interesting work by Dittirich
et
al
[
96
]
where a generalized harmonic oscillator having a TR symmetry breaking scale invariant term in its Hamiltonian was studied for the purpose of deriving geometric phase from the system. To solve the Hamiltonian, they employed the Lewis-Riesenfeld technique
[
86
]
. Under the adiabatic condition, they derived the form of geometric phase using that Lewis technique. Notably, the TR symmetry breaking scale invariant term can arise in the Hamiltonian when it is considered in a special NC framework which is proposed in
[
72
]
to study a geometric phase which has a pure NC origin.

In this chapter, we discuss our study
[
91
]
on two periodic models of the quantum harmonic oscillator in NC space. We examine two distinct systems. System I’s Hamiltonian includes an explicit term that is scale invariant and breaks TR symmetry. In contrast, system II lacks this term initially. Since, the system II is considered in the newly defined NC framework
[
72
]
, the geometric phase forming scale invariant term arises in the Hamiltonian after transforming it from the NC space to the standard quantum mechanical phase space. In system I, basically, we extend the work of
[
96
]
in a two dimensional NC space. Although system II’s Hamiltonian was also studied in
[
72
]
, we solve it and deduce the Berry’s geometric phase using the Lewis method of invariants, which is a very simple and elegant way to treat the time dependent Hamiltonians.

Our primary focus is on investigating the path dependent Berry phase in these systems. Previous studies on similar systems
[
96
,
72
]
have predicted the presence of a non zero Berry phase when a scale invariant term is present in the Hamiltonian. In this discussion, by choosing various periodic form of the Hamiltonian parameters and the NC parameters, the explicit investigation of the geometric phases for such systems will be explored.

5.1
Periodic model system in noncommutative space

As previously mentioned, we analyse harmonic oscillators in a two dimensional NC space. We investigate two distinct types of systems. The first type incorporates an explicit scale invariant term in its original Hamiltonian and is considered in a time dependent NC space that adheres to the standard Bopp-shift relation. The second type, although lacking a scale invariant term in its original Hamiltonian, is considered a time dependent NC space defined by newly introduced coordinate mapping relations
[
72
]
. In both cases, the parameters of the Hamiltonians are periodic functions that depend on time.

5.1.1
Hamiltonian of system-I : Generalized harmonic oscillator in noncommutative framework I

The system we are examining consists of two dimensional generalized harmonic oscillators in a dynamic NC framework. Such a model of generalized harmonic oscillator placed in a standard quantum mechanical phase space in one dimension is a largely explored topic in literature
[
143
]
-
[
146
]
.
Now, the system’s Hamiltonian reads,

H(t)|_{NC}=P(t)({P_{1}}^{2}+{P_{2}}^{2})+Q(t)({X_{1}}^{2}+{X_{2}}^{2})+R(t)(X_{1}P_{1}+P_{1}X_{1}+X_{2}P_{2}+P_{2}X_{2})\penalty\ .

(5.1)

Here
P(t),R(t)
and
Q(t)
denote the time varying Hamiltonian parameters. This system can be considered as generalized harmonic oscillators having the Hamiltonian coefficients
(P,Q,R)(t)
those change slowly over time. The last term of the Hamiltonian mentioned above is a scale invariant term, appearing in scenarios where an external field interacts with a harmonic oscillator. For an example, when the gravitational waves interact with a harmonic oscillator
[
55
]
,
[
63
]
,
[
64
]
,
[
100
]
,
[
147
]
the system gains such a scale invariant term in its Hamiltonian. In this context, we would like to mention that, in a recent communication
[
100
]
, under Lewis-Riesenfeld framework, there has been significant research on the geometric phase arising from the periodic model of two dimensional quantum harmonic oscillator interacting with gravitational waves.

Since the system is considered in a dynamic NC space, the commutator brackets among the canonical operators existing in the Hamiltonian read (including
\hbar
)

\displaystyle[X_{j},X_{k}]\penalty\ =i\theta(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,

\displaystyle[P_{j},P_{k}]\penalty\ =i\Omega(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,[X_{j},P_{k}]\,=i\,\hbar\,\left[1+\frac{\theta(t)\Omega(t)}{4}\right]\delta_{jk};

(5.2)

\displaystyle(j,k=1,2\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{jk}=-\epsilon_{kj}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{12}=1)

where
\Omega(t)
and
\theta(t)
, respectively, represent the dynamic NC parameters for momentum space
and configuration space. On the other hand, the canonical operators
(x_{i},p_{i})
in standard quantum mechanical phase space follow the commutator brackets
[x_{j},p_{k}]=i\hbar\delta_{j,k}
,
[x_{j},x_{k}]=0=[p_{j},p_{k}]
; (
j,k=1,2
).

Next, the standard coordinate mapping relations those are the Bopp-shift relations
[
71
]
(
\hbar=1
) are to be implemented into the system for reconstructing the original Hamiltonian in terms of the standard phase space operators (
x_{i},p_{i}
). Those coordinate mapping relations read,

\displaystyle X_{1}=x_{1}-\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{2}\,\,\,;\,\,\,X_{2}=x_{2}+\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{1}

(5.3)

\displaystyle P_{1}=p_{1}+\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{2}\,\,\,;\,\,\,P_{2}=p_{2}-\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{1}\,\,.

(5.4)

Therefore, the original Hamiltonian in terms of
(x_{i},p_{i})
coordinate operators is revealed as
relation,

H=\dfrac{a(t)}{2}({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\dfrac{b(t)}{2}({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})+c(t)({x_{2}}{p_{1}}-{x_{1}}{p_{2}})+d(t)(x_{1}p_{1}+p_{1}x_{1}+x_{2}p_{2}+p_{2}x_{2})\,\,\,.

(5.5)

The structural form of the Hamiltonian’s parameters are derived as,

\displaystyle a(t)=2P(t)+\dfrac{\theta^{2}(t)}{2}\,Q(t)\penalty\ ,b(t)=2\,Q(t)+\dfrac{P(t)\Omega^{2}(t)}{2}\penalty\ ;

\displaystyle c(t)=P(t)\Omega(t)+Q(t)\theta(t)\penalty\ ,d(t)=\left(1-\dfrac{\Omega(t)\theta(t)}{4}\right)R(t)\penalty\ .

(5.6)

The explicit structure of the Hamiltonian parameters (
a(t),b(t),c(t)
) align to those found in the previous communications
[
83
]
and
[
89
]
on the exploration of a simple and damped (respectively) harmonic oscillator with dynamic noncommutativity in two dimension. However, since our present study aims to explore the Berry’s geometric phase acquired by the periodic Hamiltonian after the full time period
T
, we now assign these time dependent coefficients to have only the periodic functions as their explicit form. Additionally, the above Hamiltonian is different from the same treated in
[
83
]
and
[
89
]
because, unlike our present system, the earlier studies lack a scale invariant term in the Hamiltonian expressions. Our periodic Hamiltonian and the treatment performed on it are also very similar to
[
96
]
to derive the Berry’s geometric phase for a generalized harmonic oscillator placed in a conventional phase space in one dimension. Consequently,
R(t)
, the Hamiltonian coefficient with the scale invariant term, leads to a latter Hamiltonian expression shown in Eqn.(
5.5
), which gains a term associated with
d(t)
. Because of the removal of
R(t)
, the Hamiltonian reduces to the form studied in
[
89
]
.

5.1.2
Hamiltonian of system-II : Harmonic oscillator in noncommutative framework II

In this section, we examine a slightly altered system within a different NC framework. The Hamiltonian for this system lacks the scale invariant term. Therefore, it takes a similar form to that
considered in
[
83
,
89
]
. Nevertheless, here, we chose a modified version of the standard NC framework (NC framework II) which is recently proposed in
[
72
]
to demonstrate that their newly proposed NC structure causes the emergence of Berry phase.

Now the system’s Hamiltonian read

H(t)|_{NC}=P(t)(P_{1}^{2}+P_{2}^{2})+Q(t)(X_{1}^{2}+X_{2}^{2})\penalty\ ,

(5.7)

where
P(t)
and
Q(t)
are arbitrary time dependent periodic coefficients of the Hamiltonian.

Since the system is considered in a dynamic NC space, the commutator brackets among the canonical operators existing in the Hamiltonian read (including
\hbar
)

\displaystyle[X_{j},X_{k}]\penalty\ =i\theta(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,

\displaystyle[P_{j},P_{k}]\penalty\ =i\Omega(t)\epsilon_{jk}\,,\,[X_{j},P_{k}]\,=i\,\hbar\,\delta_{jk};

(5.8)

\displaystyle(j,k=1,2\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{jk}=-\epsilon_{kj}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \epsilon_{12}=1)

where
\Omega(t)
and
\theta(t)
, respectively, represent the dynamic NC parameters for momentum space
and configuration space. On the other hand, the canonical operators
(x_{i},p_{i})
in standard quantum mechanical phase space follow the commutator brackets
[x_{j},p_{k}]=i\hbar\delta_{j,k}
,
[x_{j},x_{k}]=0=[p_{j},p_{k}]
; (
j,k=1,2
).

Next, a generalized version of the standard coordinate mapping relations
[
72
]
(
\hbar=1
) are to be implemented into the system for reconstructing the original Hamiltonian in terms of the standard phase space operators (
x_{i},p_{i}
). Those coordinate mapping relations read,

\displaystyle X_{1}=x_{1}-\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{2}+\dfrac{\sqrt{-\theta(t)\Omega(t)}}{2}x_{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ X_{2}=x_{2}+\dfrac{\theta(t)}{2}p_{1}-\dfrac{\sqrt{-\theta(t)\Omega(t)}}{2}x_{1}\penalty\ ;

\displaystyle P_{1}=p_{1}+\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{2}+\dfrac{\sqrt{-\theta(t)\Omega(t)}}{2}p_{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ P_{2}=p_{2}-\dfrac{\Omega(t)}{2}x_{1}-\dfrac{\sqrt{-\theta(t)\Omega(t)}}{2}p_{1}\penalty\ .

(5.9)

Therefore, the original Hamiltonian in terms of
(x_{i},p_{i})
coordinate operators is revealed as
relation,

H=\dfrac{a(t)}{2}({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\dfrac{b(t)}{2}({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})+c(t)({x_{2}}{p_{1}}-{x_{1}}{p_{2}})+d(t)(x_{1}p_{1}+p_{1}x_{1}+x_{2}p_{2}+p_{2}x_{2})\,\,\,.

(5.10)

The structural form of the Hamiltonian’s parameters are derived as,

\displaystyle a(t)=2\,P(t)\left(1-\dfrac{\theta(t)\Omega(t)}{4}\right)+Q(t)\dfrac{\theta^{2}(t)}{2}\penalty\ ,b(t)=2\,Q(t)\left(1-\dfrac{\theta(t)\Omega(t)}{4}\right)+P(t)\dfrac{\Omega^{2}(t)}{2}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle c(t)=P(t)\Omega(t)+\theta(t)Q(t)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ d(t)=\dfrac{\sqrt{-\theta(t)\Omega(t)}}{4}\left[\Omega(t)P(t)-\theta(t)Q(t)\right]\penalty\ .

(5.11)

It is noteworthy to mention that,
d(t)
, the Hamiltonian parameter
associated with the scale invariant term, is emerged because of the presence of noncommutativity, since, unlike our system I, it vanishes when
\Omega(t),\theta(t)
tend to zero. Hence, it is evident that the geometric phase for the present system has a pure NC origin.

Before proceeding to solve the Hamiltonians (Eqn.(
5.5
) and Eqn.(
5.10
), we also present its another convenient form which is as follows

H=\dfrac{a(t)}{2}\left[\left(\,p_{1}\,+\,2\,\dfrac{d}{a}x_{1}\right)^{2}\,+\,\left(\,p_{2}\,+\,2\,\dfrac{d}{a}x_{2}\right)^{2}\right]\,+\dfrac{\omega_{p}^{2}(t)}{2a}\left(\,x_{1}^{2}\,+\,x_{2}^{2}\,\right)\,+\,c(t)\left({p_{1}}{x_{2}}-{p_{2}}{x_{1}}\right)\penalty\ ;

(5.12)

where
\omega_{p}\,=\,\sqrt{a\,b-4\,d^{2}\penalty\ }
acts as the effective frequency of the system.

5.2
Exact eigenstate of the model Hamiltonian

As usual, like in our previous works
[
89
,
90
]
, we employ the Lewis technique
[
86
]
to solve the Hamiltonians [Eqn.(
5.5
) and Eqn.(
5.10
)] representing both the systems (I and II). By solving the Lewis invariant, such that

I(t)\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\epsilon\phi(x_{1},x_{2})

(5.13)

where
\epsilon
denotes the invariant’s eigenvalue corresponding to its time dependent eigenstate
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
, the eigenstate of
H(t)
can be obtained using the following relation

\psi(x_{1},x_{2},t)=e^{i\Theta(t)}\phi(x_{1},x_{2})\penalty\ ;

(5.14)

where
\psi(x_{1},x_{2},t)
is the eigenfunction of the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
4.7
)) and the real function
\Theta(t)
is the Lewis phase factor, from which, as described in the previous chapter
2
also, the geometric phase will be derived for our model systems.

Although the detailed procedure for solving a time dependent Hamiltonian using the Lewis method of invariant is covered in previous chapters
2
and
3
, we will present some important intermediate steps here before providing the final structure of the eigenfunction for the Hamiltonians considered in this study. This is because the Hamiltonian structures (Eqn.(
5.5
) and Eqn.(
5.10
)) in the present system models are more general than those in the previously discussed models.

5.2.1
Ermakov-Lewis invariant system

First, we assume an ansatz of the Lewis invariant operator which corresponds to the latter Hamiltonian [Eqn.(
5.5
) and Eqn.(
5.10
). Conventionally, as we also discussed earlier, its structural format should be as follows,

I(t)=\alpha(t)({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\beta(t)({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})+\gamma(t)(x_{1}{p_{1}}+p_{1}{x_{1}}+x_{2}{p_{2}}+p_{2}{x_{2}}).

(5.15)

Next, applying the property of invariance

\dfrac{dI}{dt}=\partial_{t}{I}+\dfrac{1}{i}[I,H]=0

(5.16)

on the considered ansatz, we have the following set of equations hold by its time dependent coefficients.

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\alpha(t)\,d(t)-2a(t)\gamma(t)\penalty\ ,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\dot{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2b(t)\gamma(t)-4\beta(t)\,d(t)\penalty\ ,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,b(t)\alpha(t)-\beta(t)a(t)\,\penalty\ .

(5.19)

Parametrizing
\alpha\,=\,\rho^{2}
in the above relations, we have the parametrized form of those above relations

\displaystyle\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\rho}{a}(2\rho\,d-\dot{\rho})\penalty\ ,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\beta(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{(\dot{\rho}-2\rho\,d)^{2}}{a^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}\penalty\ ;

(5.21)

which are linked to the following non linear differential equation

\ddot{\rho}-\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}\dot{\rho}+\rho\,\left(ab-2\dot{d}-4d^{2}+2\dfrac{\dot{a}}{a}d\right)\,=\,\dfrac{a^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\penalty\ ,

(5.22)

known as the Ermakov-Pinney equation, which is derived by setting the integration constant to be unity. The derived EP equation resembles the form presented by
[
96
]
,
[
143
]
-
[
146
]
for exploring a time dependent generalized harmonic oscillator situated in the conventional quantum mechanical phase space. It is obvious that Eqn.(
5.22
) simplifies to the equation obtained in
[
83
]
and
[
89
,
90
]
when
d(t)
, the Hamiltonian coefficient is fixed to be zero.

Next, with the parametrized form of
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, the invariant (Eqn.(
5.15
)) in terms of
\rho
is expressed as

I(t)=\rho^{2}({p_{1}}^{2}+{p_{2}}^{2})+\left[\dfrac{(\dot{\rho}-2\rho\,d)^{2}}{a^{2}}+\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right]({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})-\dfrac{\rho}{a}(\dot{\rho}-2\rho\,d)(x_{1}{p_{1}}+p_{1}{x_{1}}+x_{2}{p_{2}}+p_{2}{x_{2}})\penalty\ ;

(5.23)

which aligns with the one found in
[
96
]
for a time dependent generalized harmonic oscillator situated in the conventional quantum mechanical phase space. At the limit
d(t)\rightarrow\,0
, the above expression becomes identical to the same derived in
[
83
]
and
[
89
,
90
]
.

5.2.2
Soultion of the Ermakov-Lewis invariant system and Lewis phase factor

Following the method demonstrated in the portion
3.2.2
from the previous chapter
3
, we first define the unitary operators as

\displaystyle\hat{U}=exp\left[\dfrac{i(2\rho\,d-\dot{\rho})}{2a(t)\rho}({x_{1}}^{2}+{x_{2}}^{2})\right],\,\,\,\hat{U^{\dagger}}\hat{U}=\hat{U}\hat{U^{\dagger}}=\textbf{I}.

(5.24)

Next, we briefly describe the remaining procedure, which is already discussed in sections
3.2.2
,
3.2.3
, and
3.2.4
under chapter
3
, and mention the important results modified due to
d(t)
, the additional parameter that appears in the Hamiltonian (Eqn.(s) (
5.5
) and (
5.10
)) of our interest.

The invariant
I(t)
, when subjected to the transformation
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)=\hat{U}I\hat{U^{\dagger}}
, gains the Hamiltonian structure of a simple harmonic oscillator. Consequently, the ladder operators can be defined in the standard manner. Upon applying the reverse transformation, they have the following form,

\displaystyle\hat{a_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\dfrac{1}{\rho}x_{j}+i\rho{p_{j}}+\dfrac{i(2\rho\,d-\dot{\rho})}{a}x_{j}\right]\penalty\ ,

(5.25)

\displaystyle\hat{{a_{j}}^{\dagger}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\dfrac{1}{\rho}x_{j}-i\rho{p_{j}}-\dfrac{i(2\rho\,d-\dot{\rho})}{a}x_{j}\right]\penalty\ ;

(5.26)

with the commutation relation
[{{\hat{a}_{i}}},{{\hat{a}_{j}}^{\dagger}}]=\delta_{ij}
.

We can quickly derive the final set of operators to solve the invariant in the polar coordinate system by linearly combining the above operators as shown below

\displaystyle\hat{a}(t)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{a}_{1}-i\hat{a}_{2})=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\left(\rho{p_{r}}+\dfrac{2\rho\,d-\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}r\right)-i\left(\dfrac{r}{\rho}+\dfrac{\rho{p_{\theta}}}{r}+\dfrac{\rho}{2r}\right)\right]e^{-i\theta}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle{\hat{a}}^{\dagger}(t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{i}{\sqrt{2}}({\hat{a}_{1}}^{\dagger}+i{\hat{a}_{2}}^{\dagger})=\dfrac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}\left[\left(\rho{p_{r}}+\dfrac{2\rho\,d-\dot{\rho}}{a(t)}r\right)+i\left(\dfrac{r}{\rho}+\dfrac{\rho{p_{\theta}}}{r}+\dfrac{\rho}{2r}\right)\right]\penalty\ .

(5.27)

It is evident from the above equation that the ladder operators prepared in this study have been modified by the parameter
d(t)
. When
d(t)
is set to zero, these operators revert to those derived in the previous model
[
89
]
.

The Lewis invariant
I(t)
can also be presented in terms of the polar coordinates as

\displaystyle I(t)=\dfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}r^{2}+\left(\rho{p_{r}}-\dfrac{\dot{\rho}-2\rho\,d}{a}r\right)^{2}+\left({\dfrac{\rho{p_{\theta}}}{r}}\right)^{2}-\left({\dfrac{\rho}{2r}}\right)^{2}\penalty\ ,

(5.28)

Again, starting from the existing form of the invariant
I(t)
in Eqn.(
5.28
), a new and appropriate form of the invariant
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
is to be derived, as done in
[
83
,
89
]
, in terms of the ladder operator, such that,

I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)=\dfrac{I}{4}-\dfrac{p_{\theta}}{2}=\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)-p_{\theta}\penalty\ .

(5.29)

Next, we redo the procedure demonstrated in the portion
3.2.4
from the chapter
3
and the eigenfunction of the invariant
I^{{}^{\prime}}(t)
is found to be

\displaystyle\phi_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)=\lambda_{n}(t)\dfrac{{(i\rho)}^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}r^{n-m}e^{i(m-n)\theta-\dfrac{a-i\rho(\dot{\rho}-2\rho\,d)}{2a{\rho}^{2}}r^{2}}U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\penalty\ ;

(5.30)

where
n
and
m
are positive integers constrained by
n+l\,=m\,\geqslant\,0
and the normalization factor
\lambda_{n}(t)
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{2}(t)=\dfrac{1}{\pi{n!}{(\rho^{2})}^{1+n}}\penalty\ ;

(5.31)

and
U\left(-m,1-m+n,\dfrac{r^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)
presents
Tricomi’s confluent hypergeometric function
[
109
,
110
]
. It is noteworthy to state that the exact analytical form of the eigenfunction of the Lewis invariant corresponding to our Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
5.5
) and Eqn.(
5.10
), which have a more general form than those considered in
[
83
,
89
,
90
]
), appears with an additional Hamiltonian coefficient
d(t)
, and reduces to the one found for the models in
[
83
,
89
,
90
]
when
d(t)
equals zero.

As usual, the Hamiltonian eigenfunction holds the following orthonormal relation,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}rdr\phi^{*}_{n,m-n}(r,\theta)\phi_{n^{{}^{\prime}},m^{{}^{\prime}}-n^{{}^{\prime}}}(r,\theta)=\delta_{nn^{{}^{\prime}}}\delta_{mm^{{}^{\prime}}}.

(5.32)

Now, we proceed to have the Lewis phase which plays very crucial role in this work. It is crucial not only for obtaining the eigensystem of the Hamiltonian, as demonstrated in our earlier works
[
89
,
90
]
, but also for determining the geometric phase form that a system acquires after a full time period
T
.

The Lewis phase form matches that found in
[
83
,
89
,
90
]
. It is presented as,

\Theta_{n,l}(t)=(n+l)\int_{0}^{t}{\left[c(\tau)-\dfrac{a(\tau)}{\rho^{2}(\tau)}\right]d\tau}\penalty\ .

(5.33)

It is important to note that, as we discussed in chapter
2
while deriving the geometric phase for a one dimensional generalized harmonic oscillator model, the parameter
d(t)
which is crucial for forming a Berry phase is not explicitly present in the phase factor. However, by utilizing the EP equation, we can incorporate this factor into the phase (Eqn.(
5.33
)). This allows us to proceed to the essential part of our work, deriving Berry’s geometric phase from the adiabatically approximated form of the Lewis phase.

5.3
Berry’s geometric phase from Lewis phase

First, we revisit the portion
2.3.2
(in the chapter
2
), where we derived the Berry phase from the Lewis phase of a one dimensional generalized harmonic oscillator. Given that our Hamiltonian is more generalized than the one previously considered, this section will detail the procedure specific to our current study.

We start with the rearranged form of the EP equation as given in Equation (
5.22
). We rearrange the equation as

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\rho}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{\dot{\rho}}{a}\right)-\left[2\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)-\left(\dfrac{ab-4d^{2}}{a}\right)+\dfrac{a}{\rho^{4}}\right]=0\penalty\ .

(5.34)

Substituting
\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}
from the above equation into the Lewis phase form, it is possible to bring the factor
d(t)
inside it. But for the sake of simplification of the calculation, we shall do it after enforcing adiabatic approximation on the system.

5.3.1
Lewis Phase Factor under adiabatic approximation

We begin by assuming that Hamiltonian is varying adiabatically, meaning the system parameters are changing gradually. Next, the Lewis phase factor is to be investigated under the condition while varying the Hamiltonian adiabatically, adopting the procedure demonstrated in
[
96
]
.

In order to include the adiabatic effect, we assume that, in the parameter space, both the Hamiltonian parameters and the EP parameter evolve gradually over a full time period
T
, such that
[a,b,c,d,\rho]\,(0)=[a,b,c,d,\rho]\,(T)
.
As an adiabatic effect, it is valid to ignore the variation rate of second order and the product of the variations having first order. Hence, we drop out the term including
\dot{\rho}
from the equation mentioned above.
The EP equation (Eqn.(
5.34
)) is now approximated as,

\displaystyle\left[2\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)-\left(\dfrac{ab-4d^{2}}{a}\right)+\dfrac{a}{\rho^{4}}\right]=0\penalty\ .

(5.35)

At this moment, the value of
a/\rho^{2}
stands as

\displaystyle\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\penalty\ \approx\penalty\ \omega_{p}\sqrt{1-\dfrac{2\,a}{{\omega_{p}}^{2}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)}\penalty\ ;

(5.36)

where
\omega_{p}
is given by,

\displaystyle\omega_{p}=\sqrt{ab-4d^{2}}.

(5.37)

Next, we impose the binomial approximation under the following condition,

\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)\,<<\,\dfrac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{2a}\penalty\ ;

(5.38)

which is consistent with the adiabatic condition, as shown by the Hamiltonian structure in Eqn.(
5.12
), indicating that the rate of change of the external influence is negligible compared to the effective frequency of the system.
Under
binomial approximation,
a/\rho^{2}
takes on the following form,

\displaystyle\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\penalty\ \approx\penalty\ \omega_{p}\left[1-\dfrac{a}{{\omega_{p}}^{2}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)\right]\penalty\ ;

(5.39)

So, the adiabatically approximated expression of the Lewis phase is given by,

\displaystyle\Theta_{n,l}(t)=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int_{0}^{t}\left[c-\dfrac{a}{\rho^{2}}\right]\penalty\ d\tau

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle(n+l)\int_{0}^{t}\left[c-\omega_{p}\left\{1-\dfrac{a}{{\omega_{p}}^{2}}\dfrac{d}{d\tau}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)\right\}\right]\penalty\ d\tau\penalty\ .

(5.40)

The structure of the approximated phase factor resembles that derived in
[
96
]
. Nevertheless, due to our system being situated in a NC space, there is an extra component that involves the Hamiltonian coefficient
c(t)
. This component of the phase is purely dynamic and continues to evolve over time.

5.3.2
Derivation of the geometric Phase

We assume that the Hamiltonian which evolves adiabatically in its parameter space
\mathcal{R}
, undergoes a cycle through a time period
T
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}(0)=\mathcal{R}(T)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ;\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{R}=(a,b,c,d)\penalty\ ;

(5.41)

For deriving the geometric phase expression , we substitute the following relation in Eqn.(
5.40
),

\dfrac{d}{dt}=\dfrac{d\mathcal{R}}{dt}\nabla_{\mathcal{R}}.

(5.42)

Substituting it in Eqn.(
5.40
), we get the geometric part of the phase, i.e. the Berry
phase as,

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}=(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ .

(5.43)

It is important to note that the derived phase is exactly similar to that presented in
[
96
]
, which examines a time dependent generalized harmonic oscillator situated in a standard quantum mechanical phase space in one dimension. Additionally,
[
72
]
provides another consistent expression for the geometric phase, achieved through the application of a specific gauge transformation.

\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}d\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)=-\dfrac{b}{\omega_{p}}d\left(\dfrac{d}{b}\right)-d\left[tan^{-1}\sqrt{\dfrac{ab}{4d^{2}}-1}\right]=-\dfrac{b}{\omega_{p}}d\left(\dfrac{d}{b}\right)-d\left[tan^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\omega_{p}}{2d}\right)\right]\penalty\ .

(5.44)

Therefore, another form of Eqn.(
5.43
) is given by ,

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}=-(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{b}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{b}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ .

(5.45)

5.4
Berry phases for various choices of Hamiltonian parameters

We now aim to investigate the explicit existence of the Berry phase by choosing various periodic functional form of the Hamiltonian parameters. Most of the earlier communications investigating the Berry phase for the harmonic oscillators have only derived a generic form of the geometric phase, without calculating the exact phase value for the system in question. Integrating Eqn.[
5.43
] and computing the exact phase value for a periodic set of Hamiltonian parameters is crucial to verify whether a non trivial geometric phase is acquired by the periodic system as it undergoes a cycle on the parameter space. In order to have the Berry phase in an explicit form, the periodic functional form of the original Hamiltonian (before employing the coordinate mapping relations) parameters
P(t)
,
Q(t)
,
R(t)
and those of the NC parameters are to be chosen at first.
Next, by using Eqn.(s)(
5.6
and
5.11
),
a
,
b
,
c
, and
d
, the coefficients of the latter (after employing the coordinate mapping relations) Hamiltonians for both the systems (I and II) are to be determined explicitly. Finally, as per Eqn.(
5.43
), the calculated periodic parameters (
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
) corresponding to the chosen periodic parameters (
P
,
Q
,
R
,
\theta
,
\Omega
) result in an explicit value of the Berry’s geometric phase.

It is also important to note that, unlike in our previous works
[
89
,
90
]
, the chosen forms of the Hamiltonian parameters do not need to satisfy the exact form of the EP equation in Eqn.(
5.22
), as that equation is now approximated in Eqn.(
5.39
).

5.4.1
Explicit investigation of Berry phase : system I

Here, for our first system which includes a scale invariant term in the Hamiltonian from the beginning and hence the presence of the considered NC (standard) framework is not essential to generate a Berry phase, we intend to investigate a finite , non zero value of Berry phase explicitly.

Case I : Non trivial geometric phase without noncommutativity

In this scenario, the noncommutativity is removed by assigning the explicit value of the NC parameters as zero. Hence, the parameter space in which the periodic system evolves exists in a conventional quantum phase space. The coefficients of the original Hamiltonian Eqn.(
5.1
) are set to hold the following forms ,

\displaystyle P(t)=\dfrac{1}{M}=constant\penalty\ ,\penalty\ Q(t)=\dfrac{1}{2}M\omega_{0}^{2}=constant\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{2}}\,cos\,(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.46)

The NC parameters are

\displaystyle\theta(t)=0,\penalty\ \Omega(t)=0\penalty\ .

(5.47)

It demonstrates a model persisting a periodically varying coefficient of the scale invariant term in its Hamiltonian. Since, this is a standard quantum mechanical scenario, it can be designed in the laboratory.

By using Eqn.(
5.6
), the above chosen values lead to calculate the following (latter) Hamiltonian parameters as

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{2}{M}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=M\omega_{0}^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ c(t)=0\penalty\ ,\penalty\ d(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{2}}\,cos(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.48)

Finally, by using Eqn.(
5.43
), the above calculated periodic forms result in the following value of Berry phase as

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{2\pi/f}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

(5.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(n+l)\int^{2\pi/f}_{0}\dfrac{f}{2}dt

(5.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(n+l)\pi\penalty\ .

(5.52)

Again, by using Eqn.(
5.45
), the phase emerges to be

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}=-(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{b}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{b}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}=(n+l)\pi\penalty\ .

(5.53)

However, the equivalence between both the values of
\Theta^{G}_{n,l}
can easily be verified in the following manner,

\displaystyle e^{i\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}=e^{-i(n+l)\pi}=e^{i(n+l)\pi}=cos[(n+l)\pi].

(5.54)

Therefore, because of the presence of a scale invariant term in the original Hamiltonian, a non trivial Berry phase is achieved in the standard quantum mechanical framework.

Additionally, the emergence of a non zero Berry phase can also be analysed through a graphical representation. We aim to visualize how the finite phase is picked up by the system. To illustrate it, we require the integral expression of the above phase, which is found to be

\displaystyle I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.55)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{t}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

(5.56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(n+l)\dfrac{ft}{2}\penalty\ .

(5.57)

As seen from Fig.(
5.1
), the dynamic nature of
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
is aperiodic. This causes the Hamiltonian to acquire a non zero geometric phase after a time period
T
. As expected, after the ending of each cycle, the phase remains constant.

Case II : Non trivial geometric phase with momentum noncommutativity

Here we remove the spatial noncommutativity and consider the system in such a framework where the momentum operators do not commute. The coefficients of the original Hamiltonian Eqn.(
5.1
) are set to hold the following forms ,

\displaystyle P(t)=\dfrac{1}{M}=constant\penalty\ ,\penalty\ Q(t)=\dfrac{1}{2}M[\omega_{0}\,sin^{3}(ft)]^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{2}}\,sin^{3}(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.58)

The NC parameters are

\displaystyle\theta(t)=0\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Omega(t)=\Omega_{0}\,cos(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.59)

By using Eqn.(
5.6
), the above chosen values lead to calculate the following (latter) Hamiltonian parameters as,

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{2}{M}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=M\,\omega_{0}^{2}\,sin^{6}(ft)+\dfrac{\Omega_{0}^{2}}{2M}\,cos^{2}(ft)

(5.60)

\displaystyle c(t)=\dfrac{\Omega_{0}}{M}\,cos(ft)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ d(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{2}}\,sin^{3}\,(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.61)

Finally, by using Eqn.(
5.43
), the above calculated periodic forms result in the following value of Berry phase as

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.62)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{2\pi/f}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{2\pi/f}_{0}\dfrac{3\,M\,\omega_{0}f}{\sqrt{2}\,\Omega_{0}}\dfrac{\left[1-cos(2ft)\right]}{2}dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\dfrac{3\,M\,\omega_{0}\,\pi}{\sqrt{2}\,\Omega_{0}}\penalty\ .

Once again, like the previous case, we analysis the above non zero result graphically. Hence,
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
, the integral expression corresponding to the above phase is given by

\displaystyle I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{t}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

(5.64)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\dfrac{3\,f\,M\,\omega_{0}}{2\sqrt{2}\,\Omega_{0}}\left[t-\dfrac{1}{2f}\,sin(2ft)\right].

(5.65)

Here also, as observed from Fig. (
5.1
), the nature of
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
is found to be aperiodic and is consistent with the non zero finite value of the Berry phase found for this case.

Figure 5.1:
Plot of the evolution of the integral expression (
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
) associated with the derived geometric phase, with respect to
ft
(dimensionless quantity), where
f
denotes the frequency of the periodic parameters. The value of the constants (in natural units) related to the periodic parameters are chosen as
l\,=\,0
;
n\,=\,1
;
M,\omega_{0}\,=\,1
;
\theta_{0},\Omega_{0}\,=\,2
. Case I deals with the system I situated in the standard quantum mechanical phase space and case II, III deal with the same system with dynamic noncommutativity. While case I and Case II show an aperiodic nature of

I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
with respect to time, in case III, the nature of
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
is purely periodic which is consistent with the vanishing of the geometric phase.

Case III : Vanishing of Berry phase in a noncommutative framework

Here we consider the NC framework in which our original Hamiltonian (Eqn.(
5.1
)) was considered at first. The coefficients of the original Hamiltonian Eqn.(
5.1
) are set to hold the following forms,

\displaystyle P(t)=\dfrac{1}{M}=constant\penalty\ ,\penalty\ Q(t)=\dfrac{1}{2}M\omega_{0}^{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{2}}\penalty\ .

(5.66)

The NC parameters are

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\theta_{0}sin(ft)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Omega(t)=\Omega_{0}sin(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.67)

By using Eqn.(
5.6
), the above chosen values lead to calculate the following (latter) Hamiltonian parameters as,

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{2}{M}+\dfrac{M\,\omega_{0}^{2}\,\theta_{0}^{2}}{4}\,sin^{2}(ft)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=M\,\omega_{0}^{2}+\dfrac{\Omega_{0}^{2}sin^{2}(ft)}{2M}

(5.68)

\displaystyle c(t)=\left(\dfrac{\Omega_{0}}{M}+\dfrac{M\omega_{0}^{2}\theta_{0}}{2}\right)sin(ft)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ d(t)=\dfrac{\omega_{0}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1-\dfrac{\theta_{0}\Omega_{0}sin^{2}(ft)}{4}\right)\penalty\ .

(5.69)

Finally, by using Eqn.(
5.43
), the above calculated periodic forms result in the following value of Berry phase as

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.70)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{2\pi/f}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\penalty\ .

Surprisingly, for the above chosen parameters, the phase is revealed to be vanished, although there was a scale invariant term (which is necessary to generate a geometric phase) in the Hamiltonian from the beginning.

In this scenario as well, we proceed to analyse
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
, the integral expression of the derived phase. Our objective is to visually explore why the system does not acquire a finite phase graphically, unlike in the earlier cases. Hence,
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
, the integral expression corresponding to the above phase is given by

\displaystyle I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.71)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{t}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

(5.72)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(n+l)\,tan^{-1}\left(\dfrac{M\theta_{0}\omega_{0}}{2\sqrt{2}}sin(ft)\right)\penalty\ .

(5.73)

As seen from Fig.(
5.1
), the dynamic nature of
I_{\Theta^{G}_{n,l}}
is purely periodic. This causes the Hamiltonian to acquire a null geometric phase after a time period
T
.

5.4.2
Explicit investigation of Berry phase : system II

In the second system (II) lacking an explicit scale invariant interaction term in its original Hamiltonian, the geometric phase has a pure NC origin since the modified version
[
72
]
of the standard noncommutativity provides a scale invariant term that is a geometric phase forming term, in the latter Hamiltonian. As evident from Eqn.(
5.43
) and Eqn.(
5.45
), to achieve a non zero finite value of Berry phase,
d(t)
, the coefficient of that scale invariant term must not vanish. Now, in system II,
d(t)
remains non zero until both the NC parameters (
\theta(t)
,
\Omega(t)
) are present. Therefore, as also discussed in
[
72
]
, it indicates an apparent existence of a non zero geometric phase in the NC framework where both the NC parameters have non zero finite explicit forms.

Now, that crucial role of noncommutativity to provide a Berry phase is to be verified explicitly and for doing so, we consider the NC framework in which our original Hamiltonian of the second system (Eqn.(
5.7
)) was considered.

Case I: Vanishing of Berry phase in noncommutative framework

The coefficients of the original Hamiltonian Eqn.(
5.7
) are set to hold the following forms,

\displaystyle P(t)=\dfrac{1}{M}=constant\penalty\ ,\penalty\ Q(t)=\dfrac{1}{2}M\omega_{0}^{2}\penalty\ .

(5.74)

The NC parameters are

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\theta_{0}\,cos(ft)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Omega(t)=-\Omega_{0}\,cos(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.75)

By using Eqn.(
5.11
), the above chosen values lead to calculate the following (latter) Hamiltonian parameters as,

\displaystyle a(t)=\dfrac{2}{M}\left(1+\dfrac{\theta_{0}\Omega_{0}cos^{2}(ft)}{4}\right)+\dfrac{1}{4}M\omega_{0}^{2}\theta_{0}^{2}cos^{2}(ft)\,,\,b(t)=\left[M\omega_{0}^{2}\left(1+\dfrac{\theta_{0}\Omega_{0}cos^{2}(ft)}{4}\right)+\dfrac{\Omega_{0}^{2}cos^{2}(ft)}{2M}\right]

\displaystyle c(t)=-\dfrac{\Omega_{0}cos(ft)}{M}+\dfrac{M\omega_{0}^{2}\theta_{0}cos(ft)}{2}\,,\,d(t)=\dfrac{\sqrt{\Omega_{0}\theta_{0}}cos(ft)}{4}\left(\dfrac{-\Omega_{0}cos(ft)}{M}-\dfrac{1}{2}M\omega_{0}^{2}\theta_{0}cos(ft)\right)\penalty\ .

(5.76)

Finally, by using Eqn.(
5.43
), the above calculated periodic forms result in the following value of Berry phase as

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{2\pi/f}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\penalty\ .

Despite fixing a non zero finite value for the NC parameters which result in a non zero coefficient of the scale invariant term in the latter Hamiltonian, the geometric phase vanishes surprisingly.

Case II: Vanishing of Berry phase in noncommutative framework

The coefficients of the original Hamiltonian Eqn.(
5.7
) are set to hold the following forms,

\displaystyle P(t)=\dfrac{sin^{2}(ft)}{M},\penalty\ Q(t)=\dfrac{1}{2}M\omega_{0}^{2}sin^{2}(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.78)

The NC parameters are,

\displaystyle\theta(t)=\theta_{0}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Omega(t)=-\Omega_{0}\penalty\ .

(5.79)

By using Eqn.(
5.11
), the above chosen values lead to calculate the following (latter) Hamiltonian parameters as,

\displaystyle a(t)=\left[\dfrac{2}{M}\left(1+\dfrac{\theta_{0}\Omega_{0}}{4}\right)+\dfrac{M\omega_{0}^{2}\theta_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]sin^{2}(ft)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ b(t)=\left[M\omega_{0}^{2}\left(1+\dfrac{\theta_{0}\Omega_{0}}{4}\right)+\dfrac{\Omega_{0}^{2}}{2M}\right]sin^{2}(ft)

\displaystyle c(t)=-\dfrac{\Omega_{0}sin^{2}(ft)}{M}+\dfrac{\theta_{0}M\omega_{0}^{2}sin^{2}(ft)}{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ d(t)=-\dfrac{\sqrt{\Omega_{0}\theta_{0}}}{4}\left(\dfrac{\Omega_{0}}{M}+\dfrac{\theta_{0}}{2}M\omega_{0}^{2}\right)sin^{2}(ft)\penalty\ .

(5.80)

Finally, by using Eqn.(
5.43
), the above calculated periodic forms result in the following value of Berry phase as

\displaystyle\Theta^{G}_{n,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\oint^{\mathcal{R}}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\,\overrightarrow{\nabla}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right).d\overrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}

(5.81)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+l)\int^{2\pi/f}_{0}\dfrac{a}{\omega_{p}}\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac{d}{a}\right)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\penalty\ .

Again, the phase is revealed to be zero.

In this context,
\dfrac{d(t)}{a(t)}
remains constant, leading to the result that despite non zero NC parameters, the Berry phase is still zero in both cases examined. Therefore, the presence of the non zero NC parameters do not necessarily result in a non zero geometric phase. This is a significant conclusion of this work.

5.5
Summary

In this chapter, we explore whether a periodic Hamiltonian consisting a geometric phase forming scale invariant term can ensure the actual existence of a geometric phase. Two distinct types of systems are examined under two different kinds of NC framework. While the first system which already includes a scale invariant term in its original Hamiltonian is considered in a standard NC framework, the second one which lacks the geometric phase forming term in the original Hamiltonian gains that term in its latter form of the Hamiltonian after being transformed by a modified version of the standard coordinate mapping relations. Both the systems are treated with Lewis-Riesenfeld approach for determining their exact eigenstate. Under the adiabatic approximation, a generalized expression for the Berry phase is derived. We then, under various framework of space (both configuration and momentum space), find out the Berry phases explicitly for both systems by using different periodic functional forms of the Hamiltonian parameters. For the first system, we could achieve an actual existence of Berry phase when the NC parameters were not present in the place, which is expected because of the presence of that scale invariant term in the original Hamiltonian. Interestingly, in the presence of the NC parameters, the phase is revealed to be vanished for a specific set of periodic parameters. Those phenomena were also explained graphically by plotting the integral expressions corresponding those derived phases with respect to time. Expectedly, the non zero geometric phases the aperiodic dynamics of their integral expressions over time and the vanishing Berry phase showed a pure periodic nature corresponding to its integral expression over time. In the second system, the Berry phase is found as a pure NC effect in literature. Although the structure of that newly proposed NC framework
[
72
]
suggested an apparent existence of Berry phase due to its ability of inserting a scale invariant term in the latter Hamiltonian of the periodic system, for most choices of the periodic parameters, we have observed the geometric phases to be vanished even in the presence of the non zero, finite NC parameters.

Chapter 6
Simple harmonic oscillator with modified noncommutativity

In the realm of noncommutativity, using the standard Bopp-shift relations
[
71
]
as a coordinate mapping relations to transform a system from NC space to ordinary commutative space has proven convenient. Nevertheless, as discussed in the previous chapter, a recent study
[
72
]
proposed a generalized version of these standard Bopp-shift relations. In the proposed relations, while the commutator brackets among configuration operators and those among momentum operators match those in standard noncommutativity, the commutator brackets between configuration and momentum coordinates are the same as in the ordinary phase space of quantum mechanics,
[X_{k},P_{l}]=i\delta_{kl}
, for
k,l=1,2
. This is in contrast to the standard Bopp-shift relations, where the commutator
[X_{k},P_{l}]
depends on the NC parameters
\theta
and
\Omega
. Another distinction is that while both the standard and modified versions involve real constants for the NC parameters
\theta
and
\Omega
, the modified version imposes an additional condition
\theta\Omega<0
to prevent the appearance of imaginary numbers in the coordinate mapping structure. Furthermore, an intriguing aspect highlighted in
[
72
]
